## Decisions and Dispositions

### *Wood v. Bartholomew*, 516 U.S. 1

Decided October 10, 1995

From: The Ninth Circuit reversed the denial of federal habeas relief after concluding that undisclosed polygraph results concerning Rodney Bartholomew and Tracy Dormady were material under *Brady*. Premeditation distinguished aggravated first-degree murder from ordinary first-degree felony murder, and Rodney and Tracy supplied the direct testimony that Bartholomew had announced an intention to leave no witnesses. The polygraph examiner found deception in Rodney’s denials that he assisted the robbery or entered the laundromat with Bartholomew. ([govinfo.gov](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-1.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Question presented: Whether the undisclosed results were material when the results themselves were inadmissible but directly supported an existing defense theory and identified a concrete route to admissible investigation or impeachment.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Kyles v. Whitley* requires collective assessment of suppressed favorable information and asks whether nondisclosure leaves the verdict unworthy of confidence. It does not reduce materiality to a sufficiency test or require proof that acquittal probably would have followed. `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

Certiorari granted; judgment of the Ninth Circuit affirmed, 5-4.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

The conditional writ remains in force. Washington must, within the reasonable period fixed below, provide a new trial on the premeditated-murder charge or reduce the conviction to ordinary first-degree murder.

#### Opinions

The Court issued a per curiam opinion joined by Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas noted their dissent from the summary disposition without filing a separate opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Brady materiality of inadmissible information

Holding: The inadmissibility of suppressed information does not categorically exclude it from *Brady* materiality. The polygraph information was material because it directly corroborated the defense’s existing claim that a principal prosecution witness concealed his participation, identified a specific avenue for admissible investigation and impeachment, and bore on the direct testimony establishing the element that distinguished the greater conviction.

Operative rule: Suppressed inadmissible information may satisfy *Brady* when its disclosed contents would have provided a concrete, record-supported route to admissible evidence concerning a key witness or disputed element. An unspecified hope that further investigation might uncover something useful remains insufficient.

Reasoning: Rodney’s possible participation was not an after-the-fact theory invented from the polygraph. It was central to the defense presented at trial. The examination indicated deception on the same participation questions, while Rodney and Tracy supplied the direct evidence of an announced plan to eliminate witnesses. Trial counsel’s retrospective statement that the information would not have changed his cross-examination is relevant, but it cannot conclusively establish what timely disclosure and investigation would have produced. Considering the suppressed information with the complete record, nondisclosure undermines confidence in the premeditation finding.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not make every undisclosed polygraph result material, permit use of inadmissible results before the jury, or treat speculation about unidentified evidence as sufficient.

Legal basis: The Due Process Clause and *Brady v. Maryland*.

Authority of the proposition: The five-Justice per curiam coalition necessarily decided the stated proposition. Because the decision is summary, its precedential force is confined to the concrete relationship among the suppressed information, the existing defense theory, the key witness’s possible participation, and the disputed element.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined the per curiam opinion.

Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that *Kyles* permits materiality to rest on a concrete investigative and impeachment route even when the suppressed item itself could not have been admitted.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Kyles* is clarified rather than displaced. Its collective-confidence inquiry contains no categorical admissibility prerequisite. The clarification does not eliminate the requirement that the claimed downstream value be concrete and supported by the record.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court summarily reversed, 5-4; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer dissented from summary disposition. In the simulation, Stone joins those four under the broader *Kyles* materiality position already adopted in the simulated law, producing a 5-4 affirmance. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-1.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the record, historical disposition, and historical lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-1.pdf))

### *Doe v. Taylor Independent School District*, 15 F.3d 443

Decided October 16, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: The en banc Fifth Circuit held that public-school students possess a Fourteenth Amendment bodily-integrity right against sexual abuse by a school employee. It affirmed denial of qualified immunity to Principal Eddy Lankford but reversed as to Superintendent Mike Caplinger. The interlocutory record must be viewed in Doe’s favor and includes repeated complaints and observations concerning the teacher’s conduct that reached Lankford before and during the abuse, while Caplinger entered later and received materially thinner information. ([Fifth Circuit Court](https://www.ca5.uscourts.gov/opinions/pub/90/90-08431.CV1.wpd.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether the asserted constitutional right and a limited individual-supervisor rule are established, and whether either administrator is entitled to qualified immunity on the plaintiff-favorable assumed facts.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Elder v. Holloway* requires consideration of the complete body of materially relevant law in deciding whether a right was clearly established. *Johnson v. Jones* permits interlocutory resolution of the legal immunity question on assumed facts but does not authorize appellate reassessment of evidentiary sufficiency or credibility. `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Principal Lankford | Fifth Circuit affirmed, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | The individual-capacity claim may proceed on the assumed facts. No liability finding is entered. |
| Superintendent Caplinger | Fifth Circuit affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The individual-capacity claim against Caplinger shall be dismissed. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports both judgment components | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Supports judgment for Caplinger; opposes denial of immunity to Lankford | Would not treat the asserted individual-supervisor obligation as sufficiently established on these assumed facts |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Bodily integrity and individual supervisory responsibility

Holding: Intentional sexual assault of a student by a public-school teacher acting through school authority violates the student’s Fourteenth Amendment right to bodily integrity. A supervisor is not liable by title or respondeat superior.

Operative rule: Individual supervisory liability requires proof that the official had actual awareness, established directly or circumstantially, of facts or a pattern plainly pointing to a substantial risk that the subordinate was sexually abusing students; consciously failed to take an obviously necessary protective step within the official’s authority; and thereby caused the plaintiff’s injury.

Reasoning: The underlying constitutional wrong is the teacher’s intentional misuse of governmental authority, not a generalized failure to protect against a private actor. The supervisor rule therefore demands personal notice, conscious inaction, and causation. General awareness that an employee is unconventional, unpopular, or overly familiar is insufficient. A sufficiently specific and sustained pattern may, however, permit the factfinder to infer actual awareness.

Limits and questions left open: The rule does not constitutionalize negligence, every inadequate investigation, or ordinary school-management error. Municipal liability, the teacher’s separate liability, damages, and the truth of disputed facts remain outside this decision.

Legal basis: The Fourteenth Amendment and 42 U.S.C. § 1983.

##### Defendant-specific qualified immunity

Holding: The assumed facts permit a finding that Lankford received repeated, increasingly specific warnings, consciously failed to take an obviously necessary protective step, and allowed the abuse to continue. Those assumed facts describe conduct outside the protected range of reasonable official judgment. Caplinger’s more remote knowledge does not establish comparable notice, deliberate indifference, or causation; he is entitled to judgment and, independently, qualified immunity.

Reasoning: Lankford allegedly received parental complaints, staff reports of inappropriate physical behavior, widespread warnings, and a personal note strongly indicating an improper relationship, yet failed to preserve the evidence, conduct a meaningful inquiry, or impose a protective restriction. Caplinger did not receive the same earlier history, and the record attributed to him does not establish the same conscious disregard. The distinction turns on each defendant’s notice, response, and causal role, not rank.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm as to both administrators.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground. His public position is the Court’s defendant-specific notice, deliberate-indifference, causation, and qualified-immunity analysis.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would grant immunity to Lankford as well as Caplinger because the asserted supervisory obligation was not established at the required degree of specificity. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for individual supervisory liability arising from a subordinate public employee’s direct sexual invasion of a student’s bodily integrity. *Elder* and *Johnson v. Jones* are applied without alteration: the complete legal landscape governs clearly established law, while disputed factual sufficiency remains outside interlocutory review.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated Court affirms the principal results reached by the en banc Fifth Circuit: the claim against Lankford proceeds, while Caplinger receives judgment. The Supreme Court did not historically issue a merits decision in this case. ([Fifth Circuit Court](https://www.ca5.uscourts.gov/opinions/pub/90/90-08431.CV1.wpd.pdf))

Research sources: The published en banc Fifth Circuit opinion for the record, supervisory formulation, summary-judgment posture, and historical judgment. ([Fifth Circuit Court](https://www.ca5.uscourts.gov/opinions/pub/90/90-08431.CV1.wpd.pdf))

### *Hodge v. Jones*, 31 F.3d 157

Decided October 23, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: Maryland retained a confidential record accurately stating that a child-abuse investigation had occurred and had been classified as unsubstantiated and ruled out. The Hodges alleged no family separation, public dissemination, present loss of custody or employment, or alteration of legal status. The Fourth Circuit rejected the constitutional claims and held the responsible officials protected by qualified immunity. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/31/157/592262/))

Questions presented: Whether confidential retention violated familial privacy, informational privacy, or a state-created procedural interest, and whether the officials were entitled to qualified immunity.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Doe v. Taylor Independent School District* recognizes liability for direct misuse of public authority and for causally responsible supervisory indifference. It does not establish a federal right to destruction of confidential governmental records. *Elder* continues to govern the clearly-established-law inquiry.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The expired prospective expungement claim is dismissed as moot. The individual-capacity federal damages claims shall be dismissed. Any independently viable state-law claim remains for a court possessing jurisdiction.

Justice O’Connor delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Confidential retention of a ruled-out investigation

Holding: Retention of a confidential record accurately stating that an investigation occurred and was ruled out does not deprive the investigated family of a federal liberty or property interest when the record is not publicly or materially disseminated, does not alter legal status, does not separate the family, and produces no denial of employment, custody, benefit, or other concrete entitlement.

Reasoning: Government has legitimate child-protection and administrative reasons to preserve a truthful history of an investigation and its exonerating disposition. Reputation or anxiety concerning possible future misuse, without a legally consequential disclosure or status change, does not satisfy *Paul v. Davis*. Carefully restricted record retention also falls within the governmental information practices permitted by *Whalen v. Roe*. A State’s failure to follow an additional state procedure does not itself create a federal deprivation when state law authorizes retention and no substantive protected status has changed.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not govern a registry disclosed to employers, licensing bodies, or custody decisionmakers; a record that omits the ruled-out disposition; an entry that automatically imposes a legal disability; or retention contrary to a substantive state-law entitlement to expungement.

Legal basis: The Fourteenth Amendment.

##### Qualified immunity

Holding: Even assuming a materially narrower record-based right could later be recognized on different facts, no sufficiently specific authority in 1989 established a federal duty to destroy this confidential ruled-out record. The individual defendants are entitled to qualified immunity.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s distinction between direct governmental interference with family integrity and confidential record retention lacking a concrete legal consequence.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Doe* is distinguished. Its direct constitutional invasion and defendant-specific causal responsibility do not create a general expungement right. The decision establishes a narrow no-deprivation rule for confidential, accurate, nonconsequential retention while reserving materially disseminated or disability-producing registries.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment accords with the Fourth Circuit’s historical result, including dismissal of the federal claims and recognition of qualified immunity. The Supreme Court did not historically decide the case on the merits. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/31/157/592262/))

Research sources: The published Fourth Circuit opinion for the record, judgment, constitutional analysis, and Justice Powell’s historical judgment-only concurrence. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/31/157/592262/))

### *Tuggle v. Netherland*, 516 U.S. 10

Decided October 30, 1995

From: Virginia sought death on future-dangerousness and vileness grounds. The prosecution presented unrebutted psychiatric future-dangerousness evidence after Tuggle had been denied the independent psychiatric assistance required by *Ake*. The Virginia Supreme Court later invalidated the future-dangerousness aggravator but allowed the death sentence to stand because the vileness aggravator remained; the Fourth Circuit adopted that reasoning on habeas review. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-10.pdf))

Question presented: Whether a surviving aggravating circumstance automatically sustains the sentence despite the conceded *Ake* error, and what federal habeas prejudice inquiry governs.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *O’Neal v. McAninch* requires relief when, after examination of the complete record under *Brecht*, the habeas court remains in grave doubt whether constitutional error substantially and injuriously affected the judgment. `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

Certiorari granted; judgment vacated and remanded, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

Tuggle’s conviction remains undisturbed. The lower court shall review the complete penalty record under *Brecht*. If the *Ake* error had a substantial and injurious effect on the jury’s life-or-death selection, or leaves the court in grave doubt under *O’Neal*, it shall issue a conditional writ requiring a new penalty proceeding within a reasonable period.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Per curiam Opinion of the Court | The Court | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Scalia and Thomas except the grave-doubt paragraph | Supports vacatur and remand | Unanimous as to the *Zant* error and application of *Brecht*; seven Justices as to *O’Neal* |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Supports vacatur and remand | Applies *Brecht* but preserves disagreement with *O’Neal*’s treatment of genuine equipoise |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Effect of a surviving aggravating circumstance

Holding: A valid aggravating circumstance establishes continued death eligibility, but it does not automatically cure a constitutional error that altered the evidence, rebuttal, mitigation, and assistance available to the sentencer.

Reasoning: *Zant v. Stephens* concerned an invalid aggravating label attached to otherwise admissible and fully contestable evidence. Tuggle’s case is different. The *Ake* violation prevented the defense from testing the State’s psychiatric presentation, developing responsive expert evidence, assisting counsel, and pursuing mitigation. Removing the formal future-dangerousness aggravator after trial did not remove the unrebutted testimony from the jury’s deliberations or recreate the penalty case the defense was denied.

Legal basis: The Due Process Clause, *Ake*, and federal habeas law.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support this proposition and the resulting vacatur.

##### Federal habeas prejudice review

Holding: The conceded *Ake* violation is reviewed under *Brecht* by examining the complete penalty record and the denied expert’s possible functions in rebuttal, mitigation, and counsel preparation. A court in genuine grave doubt about the error’s effect must grant relief under *O’Neal*.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not presume what a defense expert would have testified, declare *Ake* error structural, or decide prejudice on the present appellate record.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support application of *Brecht*. Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer support the *O’Neal* grave-doubt consequence.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand.

Public role: Joined the per curiam Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone publicly joins the requirement that the complete penalty record be reviewed under *Brecht* and that genuine grave doubt receive the consequence fixed by *O’Neal*.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, agrees that *Brecht* governs and that the surviving vileness aggravator does not answer prejudice. The concurrence preserves their disagreement with treating equipoise under *O’Neal* as nonharmless error.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*O’Neal* is applied without alteration. *Zant* is clarified: survival of an eligibility factor does not replace harmless-error review of a constitutional defect affecting the evidence and defense presented during capital selection.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court also vacated and remanded unanimously, holding that *Zant* did not automatically sustain the sentence. Historically, the per curiam opinion left harmless-error analysis for the lower courts, while Scalia separately identified *Brecht*. The simulation makes *Brecht* and the already controlling *O’Neal* consequence express parts of the remand instruction. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-10.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the record, historical per curiam disposition, and Scalia’s historical concurrence. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-10.pdf))

### *Citizens Bank of Maryland v. Strumpf*, 516 U.S. 16

Argued October 3, 1995 | Decided October 31, 1995

From: Citizens Bank temporarily refused withdrawals that would reduce Strumpf’s account below the amount of its asserted setoff and, five days later, sought relief from the automatic stay. It did not permanently settle the mutual debts or enter a final accounting application before seeking judicial authorization. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-16.pdf))

Question presented: Whether that temporary administrative hold constituted a prohibited setoff or another act barred by 11 U.S.C. § 362(a).

Controlling simulated law before decision: No prior simulated holding directly construes the interaction among §§ 362, 542(b), and 553 in this setting.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The contempt judgment based on the administrative hold shall be vacated. Any unresolved dispute concerning the bank’s substantive entitlement to setoff, the amount subject to setoff, or other bankruptcy relief remains for the bankruptcy court.

Justice Scalia delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Temporary administrative hold and the automatic stay

Holding: Citizens Bank’s temporary, amount-limited refusal to pay while it promptly sought relief from the stay was not a completed setoff and did not violate § 362(a)(3), (6), or (7).

Operative rule: A setoff occurs when a creditor decides to settle mutual obligations and takes action giving that settlement legal and accounting effect. A temporary refusal to pay that preserves the disputed amount while the creditor promptly seeks stay relief, without a permanent reduction or bookkeeping application, is not itself the completed setoff prohibited by § 362(a)(7).

Reasoning: Sections 542(b) and 553 preserve the substantive possibility of setoff while § 362 regulates when it may be exercised. Treating every temporary refusal to pay as an immediate setoff would force payment before the bankruptcy court could adjudicate the preserved defense. The bank maintained the status quo and invoked the bankruptcy court’s authority rather than unilaterally extinguishing either debt.

Limits and questions left open: The holding does not authorize an indefinite freeze, an amount exceeding the asserted setoff, unreasonable delay in seeking relief, or an accounting entry that actually applies the deposit to the loan.

Legal basis: 11 U.S.C. §§ 362, 542(b), and 553.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse.

Public role: Joined Scalia’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule distinguishing a temporary status-preserving account hold from a completed setoff. It does not alter the separate rule requiring obedience to a bankruptcy injunction pending authorized review.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimous lineup, opinion author, and principal statutory rule match the historical decision. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-16.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the record, statutory analysis, historical judgment, and lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-16.pdf))

### *Libretti v. United States*, 516 U.S. 29

Argued October 3, 1995 | Decided November 7, 1995

From: Libretti pleaded guilty during trial to engaging in a continuing criminal enterprise, agreed to forfeit identified property under 21 U.S.C. § 853, and broadly waived jury trial. The plea colloquy made clear that the ongoing jury proceeding would end and that sentencing and forfeiture would follow before the court. The trial evidence, indictment, plea materials, and sentencing findings addressed the enterprise and the listed assets. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-29.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether Rule 11(f) requires a separate factual-basis inquiry for stipulated criminal forfeiture, and whether Libretti validly waived the former Rule 31(e) jury determination of forfeitability.

Controlling simulated law before decision: No prior simulated holding directly construes § 853, Rule 11(f), or Rule 31(e).

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

The forfeiture judgment remains in force. No third party’s separate claim under § 853(n) is decided.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer, with partial joins stated at right | Supports affirmance | Parts I and II-A joined by all listed Justices; Parts II-B and II-C joined by Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer; Parts III and IV joined by Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Souter | None | Supports affirmance | Joins Parts I and II; avoids the constitutional status of the Rule 31(e) right and finds the general jury advice adequate |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Ginsburg | None | Supports affirmance | Joins Parts I and II; treats knowledge of the Rule 31(e) right as necessary but finds awareness established by the record |
| Dissent | Stevens | None | Would vacate and remand the forfeiture order | Would require an independent determination that the entire forfeiture remained within § 853’s statutory bounds |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Rule 11(f) and statutory authority for forfeiture

Holding: Criminal forfeiture under § 853 is punishment imposed following guilt, not a separate substantive charge to which another guilty plea is entered. Rule 11(f) therefore does not require a second guilty-plea factual-basis colloquy directed specifically to forfeiture.

Holding: Section 853 independently limits forfeiture to property bearing the statutory relationship to the offense or qualifying as substitute property. A plea agreement does not enlarge the court’s statutory authority. The indictment, trial evidence, plea admissions, sentencing materials, and district court’s findings supplied adequate support for the forfeiture entered here.

Reasoning: Rule 11(f)’s reference to the factual basis for a guilty plea concerns the substantive offense. Forfeiture follows as punishment. That procedural conclusion does not permit the parties to create forfeiture authority by agreement alone. The district judge heard evidence concerning Libretti’s enterprise and assets, understood the statutory requisites, confined later-discovered property to drug-tainted assets, and applied § 853 when entering the order.

Limits and questions left open: A bare omnibus forfeiture clause unsupported by admissions, evidence, or findings would not authorize a judgment exceeding § 853.

Legal basis: 21 U.S.C. §§ 848 and 853 and Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 11(f).

Authority of the proposition: Eight Justices support the Rule 11(f) holding. O’Connor, Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer support the independent statutory-nexus proposition and its application.

##### Waiver of the Rule 31(e) jury determination

Holding: The former Rule 31(e) special-verdict right is statutory and waivable. Libretti validly waived it by knowingly ending the ongoing jury trial, agreeing to forfeiture, and submitting the matter directly to sentencing, even though the court did not separately recite Rule 31(e)’s number and text.

Reasoning: The written agreement broadly waived jury trial and specifically contemplated forfeiture. The colloquy made clear that no further matter would be submitted to the jury. Nothing indicated coercion, misunderstanding, or an effort to reserve a special forfeiture verdict.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide whether a materially different forfeiture statute making a fact alter the legally authorized maximum would present an independent constitutional jury question.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor, Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Breyer support the Court’s statutory characterization and waiver rule. Souter and Ginsburg concur in the judgment on narrower awareness grounds.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone’s public position includes both the inapplicability of Rule 11(f) and the independent requirement that the forfeiture remain supported within § 853’s statutory boundaries.

#### Material Separate Writings

Souter and Ginsburg avoid deciding the constitutional status of the Rule 31(e) right. Each concludes that the trial and plea record adequately informed Libretti that the jury would no longer decide forfeiture. Stevens agrees that Rule 11(f) does not apply by its terms but would remand for a fuller independent determination of the statutory basis for the entire forfeiture. These propositions are noncontrolling except to the extent incorporated into the Court’s statutory-nexus holding.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision establishes separate recurring rules for stipulated criminal forfeiture: Rule 11(f) does not create a second plea inquiry; § 853’s statutory nexus remains independently controlling; and the former Rule 31(e) verdict may be waived through a record establishing knowledge and voluntariness without a prescribed verbal formula.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated 8-1 judgment and opinion structure track the historical decision, with Stone occupying the Chief Justice’s joins. The simulation expressly preserves the six-Justice proposition that the record and sentencing findings, rather than agreement alone, establish the statutory basis for the forfeiture. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-29.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the record, opinion sections, partial joins, separate writings, and historical judgment. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-29.pdf))

### *A. St. P. C. v. B. C.*, No. 94-7810; 643 So. 2d 743

Decided November 14, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

#### Entering Posture and Questions

From: Louisiana’s statute required termination of all visitation and contact once sexual abuse was found, with supervised visitation potentially recoverable only after successful completion of prescribed treatment and a later best-interest finding. The Louisiana Supreme Court held that the preponderance standard was constitutionally inadequate because the order produced a total and partly permanent loss of parent-child contact. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/louisiana/supreme-court/1994/93-ca-1125-6.html))

The carried question broadly concerns Louisiana’s categorical no-contact system. The judgment under review, however, decided only the standard of proof at the abuse-factfinding stage. Notice, emergency procedure, treatment conditions, periodic review, written findings, and restoration procedures are therefore not reached.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Doe* distinguishes direct governmental interference with bodily and familial interests from generalized failures to protect. *Hodge* holds that confidential record retention without a concrete legal consequence is not direct family interference. This case involves an operative judicial prohibition of all contact. Pre-divergence *Mathews v. Eldridge* and *Santosky v. Kramer* supply the governing procedural principles.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas.

The Louisiana judgment requiring clear and convincing proof remains in force. No further remand instruction is required.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Thomas | Would reverse the federal due-process ruling | Would distinguish parental termination and leave the ordinary civil proof standard in place |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Proof required for a termination-like no-contact order

Holding: At the final merits factfinding stage, a State or other proponent seeking a complete and indefinite or partly permanent prohibition of all parent-child visitation and contact based on abuse or serious harm must establish the predicate facts by clear and convincing evidence.

Operative rule: Failure to satisfy the heightened proof requirement bars the long-term complete prohibition. Satisfaction permits the State to impose the protective order otherwise authorized by law. The rule does not govern temporary emergency protection, ordinary visitation scheduling, supervised-contact conditions, or short-term restrictions.

Reasoning: The private interest is the continuing parent-child relationship. An erroneous finding under this statute eliminates all communication, permanently removes some forms of visitation, and makes even supervised contact dependent on conditions that may remain beyond the parent’s practical control. The consequences of error are therefore substantially more severe than in ordinary civil litigation. A higher proof standard materially reduces that risk without preventing immediate temporary protection or prohibiting a complete order after serious harm is reliably established. The State’s compelling child-safety interest does not depend on assigning nearly equal error risk at the final adjudicative stage.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide the constitutionality of the treatment condition; the required form of notice or written findings; emergency procedures; the timing or form of periodic review; or whether and when a separate restoration procedure is required.

Legal basis: The Due Process Clause, applying *Mathews* and *Santosky*.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Souter’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s limited standard-of-proof holding and its express reservation of the broader procedural questions.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor, joined by Scalia and Thomas, would confine *Santosky* to formal termination and would hold that the combination of judicial factfinding and continuing state protective authority leaves the preponderance standard constitutionally permissible. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision establishes a new procedural due-process proof rule for complete, indefinite, or partly permanent parent-child no-contact orders. *Hodge* is distinguished because confidential record retention did not itself alter family relations; Louisiana’s order directly and legally prohibits the relationship.

#### Historical Comparison

Historically, the Supreme Court denied certiorari in No. 94-7810, leaving the Louisiana judgment in place without creating Supreme Court precedent. The simulation reaches the merits and affirms 6-3, making the heightened proof requirement controlling federal law within its stated boundary. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep515/usrep515orders/usrep515orders.pdf))

Research sources: The official Supreme Court order for docket identity and historical action, and the published Louisiana opinion for the record, question decided, and state judgment. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep515/usrep515orders/usrep515orders.pdf))

### *NLRB v. Town & Country Electric, Inc.*, 516 U.S. 85

Argued October 10, 1995 | Decided November 28, 1995

From: The Board found that eleven applicants genuinely sought employment with Town & Country and that the company refused to interview or retain them because of union affiliation and organizing plans. Some would also have received union compensation while organizing if hired. The Eighth Circuit categorically excluded such paid organizers from the statutory term “employee.” ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-85.pdf))

Question presented: Whether simultaneous union payment and an organizing purpose prevent a worker who genuinely seeks and performs work for the targeted employer from being an employee under NLRA § 2(3).

Controlling simulated law before decision: *NLRB v. Health Care & Retirement Corp. of America* requires labor status to turn on enacted functions and actual authority rather than labels or assumptions about which institutional interest the work serves.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Eighth Circuit shall review and enforce the Board’s order without categorically excluding the applicants because the union paid them. Any preserved applicant-specific or remedial issue remains for ordinary review.

Justice Breyer delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Employee status of paid union organizers

Holding: A worker who genuinely seeks or performs work for an employer may be an “employee” under NLRA § 2(3) even while a union separately pays the worker to organize that employer.

Operative rule: Simultaneous union compensation and organizing purpose do not create a categorical exclusion from employee status. Whether an applicant genuinely intended to work, performed assigned duties, or engaged in misconduct remains governed by the Act’s ordinary factual and remedial rules.

Reasoning: Section 2(3) uses the broad phrase “any employee.” Common-law agency also recognizes that a person may serve more than one principal in relation to different duties. The employer’s proposed exclusion would make protected organizing purpose itself the reason the worker loses statutory protection. Concerns about sabotage, disobedience, deception material to lawful hiring, or misuse of confidential information can be addressed directly without rewriting the employee definition.

Limits and questions left open: The holding does not protect an applicant who never intended to work, a refusal to perform assigned duties, sabotage, theft, or independently unlawful conduct.

Legal basis: NLRA §§ 2(3), 7, and 8.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Breyer’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates a new recurring employee-status rule for paid organizers. *Health Care & Retirement Corp.* is applied without changing its separate supervisor test; both decisions require statutory labor status to follow actual functions rather than status labels.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court unanimously vacated and remanded and adopted the same substantive employee-status rule. The simulation uses reversal and remand while preserving the unanimous lineup and Breyer’s authorship. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-85.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the record, statutory analysis, historical disposition, and lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-85.pdf))

### *Thompson v. Keohane*, 516 U.S. 99

Argued October 11, 1995 | Decided November 29, 1995

From: Thompson confessed during a roughly two-hour stationhouse interrogation conducted without prior *Miranda* warnings. The state court found no custody, and the federal courts treated that ultimate conclusion as a factual determination entitled to the former § 2254(d) presumption of correctness. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-99.pdf))

Question presented: Whether the ultimate *Miranda*-custody determination is a factual issue entitled to statutory deference or a mixed constitutional question requiring independent federal review.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Stansbury v. California* defines custody through the objective circumstances manifested to the person questioned and asks whether the restraint was comparable to formal arrest.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 8-1.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

The federal courts shall accept supported state findings concerning what officers and Thompson said and did, independently determine whether those circumstances amounted to custody, and then apply the ordinary suppression and federal habeas harmless-error rules. The Court makes no present custody determination.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | None | Would affirm | Would treat the ultimate custody conclusion as sufficiently fact-bound to receive the statutory presumption |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Standard of federal habeas review for Miranda custody

Holding: A state court’s ultimate determination that a suspect was or was not in custody for *Miranda* purposes is not a factual issue entitled to the former § 2254(d) presumption. Federal habeas courts independently apply the objective custody standard to the supported historical facts.

Operative rule: The reviewing court first determines what happened, including the location, duration, statements, restrictions, transportation, and freedom communicated to the suspect. Those are historical facts. It then asks whether a reasonable person in that position would have understood the restraint as comparable to formal arrest. That ultimate application is a mixed constitutional question reviewed independently.

Reasoning: Credibility findings and reconstruction of events appropriately receive deference. Once those facts are fixed, however, the custody inquiry has a uniquely legal dimension. Independent application promotes uniformity, guides law enforcement, and does not require the federal court to second-guess demeanor findings.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide whether Thompson was in custody or whether admission of the confession was harmless.

Legal basis: Former 28 U.S.C. § 2254(d) and the Fifth Amendment rule implemented through *Miranda*.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand.

Public role: Joined Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Stansbury* remains the governing objective custody standard and is unchanged. This decision adds the recurring federal-habeas rule separating deferential review of historical facts from independent review of the ultimate custody conclusion.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court vacated and remanded, 7-2; Chief Justice Rehnquist joined Thomas’s dissent. Stone does not inherit that vote and joins Ginsburg’s reasoning, producing an 8-1 simulated judgment. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-99.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the interrogation record, standard-of-review issue, historical opinion, and lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-99.pdf))

### *Klinger v. Department of Corrections*, 31 F.3d 727

Decided December 4, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: Nebraska housed women at NCW and men at several separate institutions. The plaintiff class limited its operative comparison to NSP and challenged disparities across educational, vocational, work, rehabilitation, recreation, visiting, legal, medical, psychological, and classification services. After a liability trial, the district court found numerous unjustified disparities. The Eighth Circuit held that aggregate institutional differences made the two populations categorically dissimilar and directed dismissal of the equal-protection claim. ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/031/31.F3d.727.93-2928.html))

Question presented: Whether separate sex-segregated prisons may be declared incomparable in the aggregate, and what comparator and scrutiny framework governs particular programming disparities.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Kelley v. Board of Trustees of the University of Illinois* requires an express sex-conscious allocation to serve an actual important objective through a substantial, nonstereotyped relationship. *J.E.B. v. Alabama* rejects sex as a proxy for individual capacity. Facially neutral rules remain governed by the discriminatory-purpose requirement. `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

The Eighth Circuit shall reinstate the equal-protection claim and conduct service-specific review. No final liability finding, statewide funding requirement, or order establishing a particular prison program is entered.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm dismissal | Would permit aggregate institutional differences to defeat the proposed comparison and would give correctional administrators broader threshold deference |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Comparator and scrutiny for sex-structured prison services

Holding: A court may not reject an equal-protection comparison between sex-segregated prisons solely because the institutions differ in the aggregate. The relevant comparison is tied to the particular service and to characteristics that legitimately bear on access to or provision of that service.

Operative rule: For each challenged service, the court must identify the relevant eligibility, need, custody, safety, scale, staffing, demand, and cost characteristics. An express allocation by sex receives intermediate scrutiny, requiring an important governmental objective and a substantial, nonstereotyped relationship between the classification and that objective. When a facially neutral rule produces a disparity, the plaintiffs must establish discriminatory purpose before sex-based scrutiny attaches. Legitimate correctional differences then operate within the comparator, purpose, and fit inquiries to which they are legally relevant.

Reasoning: Treating entire institutions as incomparable because the State houses men and women separately would permit the segregation itself to foreclose review. The opposite approach, which treats every program difference as a facial sex classification, would disregard neutral decisions based on security, scale, need, or cost. Service-specific analysis avoids both errors. *Turner v. Safley* informs the assessment of genuine correctional objectives and evidence, but it does not replace the scrutiny required for an express sex classification.

Application: The Eighth Circuit erred by ending the inquiry at the institutional level. The district court’s liability findings cannot simply be reinstated, however, because each challenged program must be classified and reviewed under the correct sequence, including the discriminatory-purpose requirement for facially neutral decisions.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not require identical prisons, equal per-capita spending, identical program menus, or disregard of legitimate differences in security, population, need, demand, staffing, and cost. Disparate impact alone does not establish discriminatory purpose.

Legal basis: The Equal Protection Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s service-specific comparator and scrutiny framework.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would affirm on the ground that the institutions’ materially different populations, sentence profiles, security demands, and scale defeat the NSP comparison at the threshold. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Kelley* is clarified for sex-segregated correctional institutions. Its intermediate scrutiny governs an express sex-based service allocation, while *Feeney*’s purpose requirement continues to govern facially neutral rules. The decision adds service-specific comparator analysis and confirms that correctional deference does not displace the scrutiny attached to an express sex classification.

#### Historical Comparison

Historically, certiorari was denied and the Eighth Circuit’s categorical no-comparator judgment remained in place. The simulation reverses 7-2 and requires service-specific review, while preserving the Eighth Circuit’s separate point that facially neutral disparities require discriminatory purpose. ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/031/31.F3d.727.93-2928.html)) `OT_1995CHUNK1.md`

Research sources: The published Eighth Circuit opinion for the interlocutory posture, operative comparison, majority reasoning, discriminatory-purpose discussion, and dissent. ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/031/31.F3d.727.93-2928.html))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### *Bailey v. United States; Robinson v. United States*, 516 U.S. 137

Argued October 30, 1995 | Decided December 6, 1995

From: The District of Columbia Circuit affirmed both § 924(c)(1) convictions under a facilitation standard. Bailey’s loaded pistol was locked in his automobile trunk while cocaine was in the passenger compartment. Robinson’s unloaded handgun was stored in a locked bedroom trunk with drugs and cash. The Government proved possession and availability, but no firing, brandishing, display, reference, barter, or other employment of either firearm during the predicate offenses. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/137/))

Question presented: Whether the evidence was sufficient to establish “use” of a firearm under the then-operative 18 U.S.C. § 924(c)(1).

Controlling simulated law before decision: No earlier simulated holding construes the conduct element of § 924(c)(1). *Staples v. United States* and *United States v. X-Citement Video, Inc.* require fidelity to enacted criminal elements, but neither defines firearm use. `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| *Bailey*, No. 94-7448 | Reversed and remanded, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The judgment resting on the broad-use instruction is reversed. Any carry theory must be separately charged, preserved, and supported under ordinary procedural law. |
| *Robinson*, No. 94-7492 | Reversed and remanded, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The judgment resting on the broad-use instruction is reversed. The prior verdict may not be converted into a finding on an untried carry element. |

Justice O’Connor delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Active employment of a firearm

Holding: “Use” under the then-operative § 924(c)(1) requires active employment of the firearm during and in relation to the predicate offense. Neither petitioner's stored weapon satisfies that element.

Operative rule: Active employment includes firing or attempting to fire, brandishing, displaying, striking with, bartering, or referring to a firearm in a manner calculated to affect another person. Possession, concealment, storage, strategic placement, proximity, or availability may show why a firearm was kept, but those facts do not themselves constitute use.

Reasoning: Ordinary usage distinguishes employing an object from merely possessing or storing it. Congress also used the separate verb “carry,” making it improper to expand “use” until the two terms substantially overlap. The Government’s emboldenment and facilitation theory proves a purpose for possession, not employment of the weapon as an operative instrument of the offense.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not define the full scope of “carry,” decide constructive-possession rules under other statutes, or address later amendments using broader language. A court may not sustain either judgment on a carry theory unless the charging instrument, trial procedure, preservation, and evidence independently permit that disposition.

Legal basis: 18 U.S.C. § 924(c)(1).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand in both dockets.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s text-and-structure distinction between active employment and possession or storage.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision supplies the first controlling simulated construction of firearm “use” under § 924(c)(1). The existing mens rea rules from *Staples* and *X-Citement Video* are applied as principles of element preservation but are not altered.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimous lineup, opinion author, and active-employment construction match the historical decision. The remand also preserves the historical distinction between the failed use theory and any procedurally available carry issue. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/137/))

Research sources: The official report and associated lower-court record for the evidence, statutory question, historical judgment, and opinion structure. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/137/))

### *Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue*, 28 F.3d 1501

Decided December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: The Eighth Circuit reversed a district-court judgment for the School District. The parent-run Club met monthly after classes, required parental consent, disclaimed school sponsorship, and used skits, songs, Bible reading, prayer, and discussion to address friendship, morality, and youth development. The amended policy retained immediate after-school access for Scouts and athletic groups while expressly forbidding religious speech in the permitted Scout meetings. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/28/f3d/1501/good-newsgood-sports-club-v-school-district-of-city-of-ladue-h))

Questions presented: Whether the policy discriminated against a religious viewpoint and whether equal access would violate the Establishment Clause.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Rosenberger* distinguishes equal civic access from direct governmental financing of devotional activity. *Capitol Square v. Pinette* and *Chabad-Lubavitch v. Miller* require contextual attribution and hold that neutrally admitted, privately sponsored religious expression does not become governmental speech merely because it occurs on public property. `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens and Ginsburg.

The district court shall enjoin exclusion of the Club from the 3-to-6 p.m. access program because of its religious viewpoint. The District may enforce neutral scheduling, supervision, safety, parental-consent, identification, and disclaimer rules applicable to comparable private groups.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | Thomas | Supports affirmance | Emphasizes that viewpoint discrimination remains impermissible in a nonpublic forum once the permitted subject has been defined |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg | Would reverse | Would permit the District to define the immediate after-school period more narrowly because of the students’ age, timing, and attribution concerns |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Religious viewpoint discrimination in an after-school access program

Holding: A school district that permits private groups to address youth character and moral development may not exclude religious treatment of those subjects or require otherwise eligible groups to omit religious content.

Operative rule: Once a school opens a private-access channel for a defined subject, it may impose reasonable subject-matter and operational restrictions, but it may not identify a religious perspective on the permitted subject as the forbidden viewpoint.

Reasoning: Scouts were permitted to address character, responsibility, and youth development. The policy nevertheless required them to omit religion and excluded the Club because it addressed the same subjects through prayer, Bible reading, and religious discussion. That distinction turns on perspective, not on a neutral definition of the forum’s subject. Availability after 6 p.m. does not cure denial of the materially different late-bus access period afforded the favored groups.

Legal basis: The Free Speech Clause.

##### Attribution and the Establishment Clause

Holding: Equal access for the Club does not establish religion on this record.

Reasoning: The meetings are voluntary, parent-run, outside instructional time, dependent on parental consent, unsupported by public financing, and subject to a clear private-sponsorship disclaimer. The District neither selects the Club’s content nor uses teachers to lead its activities. The students’ age permits careful identification and consent safeguards, but it does not transform neutral access into school-sponsored worship.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not require access during instruction, authorize teacher-led recruitment or devotion, excuse disruption or coercion, or bar neutral rules governing supervision and safety. It also does not require the District to retain the forum indefinitely if it closes or redefines it through genuinely viewpoint-neutral rules.

Legal basis: The Establishment Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s distinction between nonfunded private access and governmental religious activity.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, emphasizes that a forum need not be fully public before viewpoint discrimination becomes impermissible. Once moral and character development are admitted subjects, the government cannot require a secular answer.

Stevens, joined by Ginsburg, would permit the District to reserve the immediate post-class period for organizations with a closer historic relationship to the schools. The dissent considers the late-bus setting and junior-high audience sufficient to create a greater attribution risk. Those propositions are noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Rosenberger* is distinguished because no public money is transferred for devotional activity. *Pinette* and *Chabad-Lubavitch* are applied to an after-school facility program, with parental consent, private sponsorship, timing, public control, and disclaimers supplying the attribution inquiry. The decision adds a recurring school-access application without changing those rules.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court did not historically decide the case on the merits. The simulation affirms the Eighth Circuit’s historical result that exclusion of the Club, while Scouts addressed comparable moral-development subjects, constituted religious-viewpoint discrimination and that equal access would not establish religion. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/28/f3d/1501/good-newsgood-sports-club-v-school-district-of-city-of-ladue-h))

Research sources: The published Eighth Circuit opinion for the policy, record, historical judgment, and lower-court division. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/28/f3d/1501/good-newsgood-sports-club-v-school-district-of-city-of-ladue-h))

### *Southern Christian Leadership Conference of Alabama v. Sessions*, 56 F.3d 1281

Decided December 18, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: The en banc Eleventh Circuit affirmed judgment for Alabama in challenges to at-large, numbered-place, majority-vote judicial election systems covering ten judicial circuits and four county district-court systems. The claims relied on racially polarized voting, electoral history, structural features, and proposed subdistrict or alternative-election arrangements. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-11th-circuit/1336383.html))

Question presented: Whether the Eleventh Circuit applied the correct § 2 framework to the proposed comparators, the *Gingles* predicates, the totality of circumstances, and the institutional features of judicial elections.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Holder v. Hall* requires a concrete, workable comparator grounded in objective law, practice, history, or a closely comparable institution. *Johnson v. De Grandy* requires the *Gingles* predicates and the full statutory totality, treats proportionality as relevant but not dispositive, and rejects maximization. `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 6-3.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

The district court shall analyze each challenged electoral unit separately. It shall determine whether a concrete lawful alternative permits comparison, apply the *Gingles* predicates where applicable, assess the full statutory totality, and fashion relief only for a unit in which liability is proved. No proposed remedy is approved by this judgment.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Scalia, Thomas | Would affirm | Would give controlling weight to the absence of a demonstrated workable benchmark preserving Alabama’s judicial linkage and institutional structure |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Comparator and totality in judicial-election claims

Holding: Popular election of judges is not categorically exempt from Voting Rights Act § 2. A claim concerning a judicial electoral unit requires a concrete lawful comparator, the applicable *Gingles* predicates, and proof under the full statutory totality that protected voters possess less electoral opportunity.

Operative rule: For each challenged unit, the court must first identify a workable alternative grounded in objective law, established practice, history, or a closely comparable institution. Comparator sufficiency permits comparison but does not establish liability. The court must then evaluate cohesion, legally relevant bloc voting, and the complete statutory totality without presuming proportional officeholding or racial maximization.

Reasoning: The judicial character of the office affects the comparator, the State’s asserted interests, and the fit of a possible remedy. It does not create categorical immunity once the State chooses popular election. Conversely, underrepresentation or the possibility of constructing a different electoral system does not itself show unequal opportunity. The claims concern materially different circuits and districts, so an aggregate conclusion cannot substitute for unit-specific analysis.

Application: The en banc analysis did not adequately preserve the distinction among comparator sufficiency, the *Gingles* predicates, statutory liability, and remedial feasibility across each challenged unit. Vacatur is required, but the present record does not permit a Supreme Court finding of liability or selection of a remedy.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not require election of judges of any particular race, guarantee proportional officeholding, approve cumulative voting or subdistricts, or disregard legitimate concerns about jurisdiction, linkage, geography, judicial administration, and state structure.

Legal basis: Voting Rights Act § 2.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand.

Public role: Joined Souter’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s comparator-first, unit-specific *Gingles* and totality sequence.

#### Material Separate Writings

Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas, would affirm. The dissent concludes that the plaintiffs did not establish a lawful, institutionally coherent benchmark and that § 2 does not authorize federal restructuring based on underrepresentation alone. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Holder* is applied and clarified for judicial elections. Its comparator requirement remains a comparative merits prerequisite rather than an Article III bar. *De Grandy* is applied without alteration: proportionality is relevant evidence, not a safe harbor or entitlement, and § 2 does not require maximization.

#### Historical Comparison

Historically, the Supreme Court did not issue a merits decision, and the en banc Eleventh Circuit judgment for Alabama remained in force. The simulation vacates that judgment 6-3 and requires a unit-specific sequence separating comparator, statutory predicates, totality, and remedy. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-11th-circuit/1336383.html))

Research sources: The published en banc Eleventh Circuit opinion for the systems challenged, evidence, lower-court judgment, and historical procedural anchor. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-11th-circuit/1336383.html))

### Grant, Vacatur, and Remand: *Lawrence v. Chater*, 516 U.S. 163

Decided January 8, 1996

Acting Court: Supreme Court of the United States.

Relief requested: Reconsideration of the denial of Social Security survivor benefits after the Social Security Administration changed the statutory position it had urged below.

Public action: Certiorari granted; judgment vacated and case remanded, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

Publicly stated basis: A six-Justice per curiam opinion concludes that an unconsidered postjudgment development may support a GVR when there is a reasonable probability that the lower court’s decisional premise or result may change and the equities favor reconsideration. Stone concurs only in the narrower judgment that the Fourth Circuit should consider the Government’s new position without the Supreme Court deciding its validity, entitlement to deference, or effect. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/163/))

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Per curiam opinion | The Court | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports the GVR | General § 2106 authority, reasonable-probability inquiry, and equitable application |
| Concurrence | Stevens | None | Supports the GVR | Emphasizes discretionary administration of the certiorari docket |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone | None | Supplies the seventh vote for the GVR | Limited reconsideration without deciding cognizability, deference, or benefits entitlement |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Opposes the GVR | Would not vacate without first identifying legal error or a traditional intervening ground |

Effect on judgment below: The Fourth Circuit judgment is vacated. That court shall consider the Solicitor General’s intervening position and may remand to the Commissioner if appropriate. The Supreme Court expresses no view on the position’s legality, persuasive force, or Lawrence’s ultimate entitlement.

Next procedural stage: Reconsideration in the Fourth Circuit, with any administrative remand governed by that court’s lawful disposition.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Grant, vacate, and remand.

Public role: Concurrence in the judgment.

Announced ground: Section 2106 permits the Court to request lower-court consideration of a materially changed Government position. Stone does not join a broader rule resolving whether the new interpretation is cognizable, deferentially reviewed, or outcome determinative.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court also GVR’d 7-2. Chief Justice Rehnquist concurred in the Lawrence disposition, Justice Stevens wrote separately, and Justice Scalia dissented with Justice Thomas. Stone occupies Rehnquist’s judgment position but states the narrower ground supplied by his controlled brief. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/163/))
