### *Yamaha Motor Corp., U.S.A. v. Calhoun*, 516 U.S. 199

Argued October 31, 1995 | Decided January 9, 1996

From: Twelve-year-old Natalie Calhoun died in a recreational jet-ski accident in Puerto Rico’s territorial waters. She was neither a seaman nor a maritime worker. The Third Circuit accepted an interlocutory appeal from a certified order and held that state wrongful-death and survival remedies were not categorically displaced. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-199.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether § 1292(b) permitted the Third Circuit to decide the state-remedies issue as part of the certified order, and whether federal maritime law displaced state remedies for a recreational nonseafarer’s territorial-water death.

Controlling simulated law before decision: No earlier simulated holding defines the scope of a § 1292(b) appeal or the remedial regime governing this category of maritime death.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

The action may proceed with otherwise applicable state wrongful-death and survival remedies available. Liability, choice of law, recoverable damages, and other unresolved issues remain for ordinary proceedings below.

Justice Ginsburg delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Scope of an accepted § 1292(b) appeal

Holding: A court of appeals that accepts a § 1292(b) appeal may decide an issue fairly included within the certified order even though the district court did not separately formulate that issue as the controlling question.

Operative rule: Section 1292(b) authorizes an appeal from the certified order. The court of appeals may resolve an antecedent or necessary issue contained within that order, but may not use the appeal to review a separate order or unrelated matter lacking its own appellate basis.

Reasoning: The certified order assumed that federal maritime law exclusively governed the Calhouns’ remedies and then addressed consequences flowing from that premise. Whether state remedies remained available was therefore part of the same order and logically antecedent to the certified damages questions.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not authorize plenary interlocutory review of every issue in the litigation.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. § 1292(b).

##### State remedies for a nonseafarer’s territorial-water death

Holding: State wrongful-death and survival remedies remain available for the death of a recreational nonseafarer in territorial waters when no federal statute or controlling uniform maritime rule supplies an exclusive remedial regime.

Operative rule: A court must distinguish the general maritime cause of action from status-specific federal statutes, location-specific federal statutes, and supplementary state remedies. Federal displacement follows from an applicable exclusive statute or controlling maritime rule, not from an abstract preference for uniformity.

Reasoning: *Moragne* supplied a general maritime wrongful-death action but did not extinguish every state remedy historically available in territorial waters. *Miles* coordinated remedies in the congressionally regulated seaman setting, and the Death on the High Seas Act governs deaths within its territorial scope. Natalie Calhoun fell within neither category.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide remedies for seamen, longshore workers, deaths governed by the Death on the High Seas Act, or a state rule that directly conflicts with a specific federal maritime command.

Legal basis: General maritime law and the relevant federal maritime statutes.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rules governing the scope of an accepted § 1292(b) appeal and the coexistence of state remedies for territorial-water deaths outside an exclusive federal maritime regime.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment, two controlling holdings, and opinion author match the historical decision. The supplied brief’s attribution to Justice Stevens is corrected to Justice Ginsburg. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-199.pdf))

Research sources: The official report for the record, argument date, jurisdictional holding, maritime-remedies holding, historical judgment, and opinion author. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-516/pdf/USREPORTS-516-199.pdf))

### *Allridge v. Scott*, 41 F.3d 213

Decided January 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: Texas relied on future dangerousness in obtaining Allridge’s death sentence. Allridge remained legally eligible for parole, although he proffered expert evidence outside the jury’s presence that release was highly unlikely. His preserved mitigation theories included lay testimony from his father concerning alleged mental illness and abuse during a prior incarceration, and a separate argument concerning medical evidence that was not introduced because of its possible adverse effect on the future-dangerousness issue. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/41/213/564532/))

Questions presented: Whether *Simmons* extends to practical or delayed parole consequences when parole remains legally possible, and when the Texas special issues require an additional instruction permitting constitutionally meaningful consideration of mitigating evidence.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Simmons v. South Carolina* requires accurate parole-ineligibility information when future dangerousness is argued and the available life sentence legally forecloses parole. *Penry* requires a meaningful vehicle for relevant mitigation. The earlier same-term decision in *Tuggle* supplies *Brecht* and *O’Neal* review only if constitutional error is first established. `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The denial of federal habeas relief remains in force. The conviction and death sentence are undisturbed by the issues within the controlled grant.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion announcing the judgment and Opinion of the Court in part | O’Connor | Stone and Kennedy in full; Scalia and Thomas as to the parole holding, record-specific *Penry* application, and judgment; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer as to the general *Penry* formulation | Supports affirmance | Different issue coalitions as stated |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Supports affirmance | Declines the broader *Penry* formulation and preserves disagreement with *Simmons* |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports the general *Penry* rule but opposes the judgment | Would extend due-process protection to the proffered parole information and find the mitigation instruction inadequate |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Parole information when parole remains legally possible

Holding: When future dangerousness is argued, due process does not require an instruction or evidentiary presentation predicting the timing or likelihood of discretionary parole if the defendant remains legally eligible for parole.

Operative rule: *Simmons* requires correction of a legally false release premise when the only noncapital sentence forecloses parole. It does not require courts to predict future parole-board decisions or convert practical estimates of release into constitutionally fixed sentencing facts.

Reasoning: Allridge’s life-sentence alternative did not make release legally impossible. The proffer therefore concerned how parole rules and discretionary decisions might operate decades later, not correction of the false legal premise that activated *Simmons*. Such estimates are contingent and may change over time.

Limits and questions left open: A State remains free to provide accurate, nonmisleading information about a legally fixed minimum period of confinement. The holding does not narrow *Simmons* when the life sentence legally eliminates parole.

Legal basis: The Due Process Clause.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s opinion is joined on this proposition by Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, an actual five-Justice majority.

##### When an additional Penry instruction is required

Holding: A supplemental instruction is required when mitigating evidence actually presented possesses constitutionally relevant force that the statutory questions do not permit the jury meaningfully to express.

Operative rule: The defendant need not establish a formal diagnosis, a uniquely severe permanent disability, or a rigid causal nexus between the mitigation and the offense. The inquiry is whether there is a reasonable likelihood that the existing questions prevent jurors from giving the actually presented evidence its full mitigating effect.

Reasoning: *Lockett*, *Eddings*, and *Penry* protect the sentencing jury’s ability to consider and give effect to relevant mitigation. The constitutional inquiry concerns the legal operation of the special issues, not whether the evidence satisfies a narrow diagnostic category created by a lower court.

Limits and questions left open: No supplemental instruction is required for evidence never introduced, or when the mitigating force of the evidence can be expressed through the existing questions.

Legal basis: The Eighth Amendment.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s opinion is joined on this formulation by Stone, Kennedy, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing a seven-Justice majority.

##### Application to Allridge’s mitigation theories

Holding: Allridge’s preserved mitigation theories do not establish that the Texas questions prevented constitutionally meaningful consideration on this record.

Reasoning: The father’s lay testimony concerning alleged mental illness and prior abuse did not establish a mitigating force that necessarily fell outside the deliberateness and future-dangerousness inquiries or operated only as aggravation. The separate theory concerning medical evidence fails because the evidence was never introduced. Without its content, reliability, mitigating significance, and possible aggravating implications, a court cannot determine that the statutory questions prevented the jury from giving it effect.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not approve a system in which reliable, genuinely two-edged mitigation can operate only as aggravation.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s application is supported by Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, an actual five-Justice majority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s opinion in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s refusal to extend *Simmons* to discretionary parole predictions, the broader seven-Justice *Penry* formulation, and the conclusion that neither preserved mitigation theory establishes constitutional error on this record.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, agrees that *Simmons* does not apply and that the particular *Penry* claims fail. The concurrence would retain a narrower account of mitigation and does not join the Court’s rejection of a rigid diagnosis or causal-nexus prerequisite.

Souter, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer, agrees with the Court’s general *Penry* formulation but would find that the father’s testimony lacked a meaningful mitigating outlet. The dissent would also permit accurate evidence concerning Allridge’s practical confinement consequences as an answer to the State’s future-dangerousness case. It would remand for the prejudice review required by *Tuggle*.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Simmons* is declined extension beyond legal parole ineligibility. *Penry* is clarified: formal diagnosis, unique severity, and a rigid causal nexus are not categorical prerequisites, but the evidence must actually be presented and must possess mitigating force the statutory questions cannot meaningfully express. *Tuggle* remains unchanged and is not activated because the controlling coalition finds no constitutional error.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court did not historically decide the case on the merits. The simulation affirms the Fifth Circuit’s denial of habeas relief but rejects its suggestion that *Penry* categorically requires a uniquely severe disability causally linked to the crime. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/41/213/564532/))

Research sources: The published Fifth Circuit opinion for the parole proffer, sentencing questions, mitigation evidence, lower-court reasoning, judgment, and corrected decision date. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/41/213/564532/))

### *Bank One Chicago, N.A. v. Midwest Bank & Trust Co.*, 516 U.S. 264

Argued November 28, 1995 | Decided January 17, 1996

From: Bank One alleged that Midwest Bank violated Regulation CC in returning a check and failing to provide timely notice of nonpayment. The district court entered summary judgment for Bank One, but the Seventh Circuit vacated that judgment and ordered dismissal for lack of subject-matter jurisdiction, reasoning that the Act’s federal enforcement provisions did not encompass interbank suits. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/264/))

Question presented: Whether the Expedited Funds Availability Act and Regulation CC authorize an interbank federal claim enforceable in a federal district court.

Controlling simulated law before decision: No prior simulated holding construes the EFAA’s interbank civil-liability provisions. Existing simulated law keeps statutory duty, cause of action, jurisdiction, and ultimate liability analytically separate.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

The federal courts shall exercise jurisdiction over Bank One’s pleaded federal interbank claim. Coverage, breach, causation, defenses, and damages remain for proceedings below.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer in full; Scalia except the drafting-history discussion | Supports reversal and remand | Core statutory holding supported by all nine |
| Concurrence | Stevens | Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Defends consideration of public drafting history as confirmation of the statutory reading |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | None | Supports reversal and remand | Relies on enacted text and structure and rejects the drafting-history discussion |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Federal interbank liability and jurisdiction

Holding: The EFAA authorizes a depository bank to bring an action against another depository institution for violating the Act’s interbank liability rules and implementing regulations, and the action may be brought in federal court.

Operative rule: When § 4010(f) authorizes interbank liability standards, Regulation CC supplies those standards, and § 4010(d) authorizes federal or state adjudication of any action under the section, a regulated bank may bring a federal interbank claim. Recognition of the claim and jurisdiction does not establish breach or damages.

Reasoning: Section 4010 is a civil-liability provision. Subsection (a) addresses liability to persons other than banks, while subsection (f) covers liability between banks and delegates development of the governing standards to the Federal Reserve Board. Subsection (d) then provides a judicial forum for actions under the section. Nothing grants the Board adjudicatory machinery for resolving private interbank disputes. The enacted arrangement follows the familiar sequence in which an agency regulates and a court adjudicates.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not determine whether Midwest violated Regulation CC, what damages are recoverable, or whether state law is displaced in circumstances presenting no actual conflict.

Legal basis: 12 U.S.C. § 4010, Regulation CC, and 28 U.S.C. § 1331.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support the text-and-structure holding. Scalia does not join the separate drafting-history discussion.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens, joined by Breyer, regards the public drafting history as confirming why Congress placed the more complex interbank provisions in subsection (f).

Scalia agrees with the Court’s judgment and operative statutory construction but rejects reliance on drafting history. The separate disagreement does not diminish the unanimous authority of the interbank-liability and federal-forum propositions.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule recognizing a federal interbank claim under the EFAA and Regulation CC. It preserves the existing distinction among statutory duty, judicial cause of action, jurisdiction, and ultimate liability.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, author, partial joins, and statutory result match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the Chief Justice’s full join. The supplied comparator’s description of a wholly unanimous opinion is corrected: the judgment was unanimous, but Scalia declined part of the Court’s reasoning and Stevens wrote separately with Breyer. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/264/))

Research sources: The official report for the corrected docket, argument date, statutory provisions, lower-court posture, judgment, and opinion topology. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/264/))

### *Neal v. United States*, 516 U.S. 284

Argued December 4, 1995 | Decided January 22, 1996

From: Neal’s LSD was distributed on blotter paper. *Chapman v. United States* had construed the statutory phrase “mixture or substance” to include the carrier medium when calculating the mandatory minimum under 21 U.S.C. § 841(b). The Sentencing Commission later adopted a standardized per-dose weight for Guidelines calculations but did not amend the statute. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/284/))

Question presented: Whether the later Guidelines amendment displaced *Chapman*’s statutory method for determining the mandatory minimum.

Controlling simulated law before decision: No simulated holding has displaced *Chapman*. The Sentencing Commission’s delegated power over Guidelines calculations is distinct from Congress’s statute and the Court’s controlling construction of it.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

Neal’s statutory mandatory minimum remains governed by *Chapman*. Any otherwise lawful Guidelines adjustment remains subject to that statutory floor.

Justice Kennedy delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Effect of the Guidelines amendment on statutory precedent

Holding: *Chapman* continues to require inclusion of the LSD carrier medium when applying § 841(b)’s mandatory minimum, notwithstanding the Sentencing Commission’s different per-dose method for calculating the Guidelines range.

Reasoning: Congress enacted the statutory phrase, and the Court authoritatively construed it. The Commission later exercised a different delegated function. Its amendment changes the Guidelines within its authority, but it neither amends § 841(b) nor supersedes the Court’s statutory construction. The two calculations can coexist because one determines the statutory floor and the other determines the advisory framework applicable above that floor.

Limits and questions left open: Agency developments may inform a future reconsideration of precedent, but an agency cannot itself overrule a judicial construction of an enacted statute. Congress remains free to amend the statutory rule.

Legal basis: 21 U.S.C. § 841(b), 28 U.S.C. § 994, and statutory stare decisis.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Chapman* is reaffirmed for statutory mandatory minimums. The Guidelines amendment governs the separate Guidelines calculation to the extent it does not contradict the statutory floor. No new rule of agency deference is created.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment, Kennedy authorship, and distinction between statutory and Guidelines calculations match the historical decision. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/284/))

Research sources: The official report for the amendment, statutory issue, historical judgment, opinion author, and controlling rationale. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/284/))

### *American Life League v. Reno*, 47 F.3d 642

Decided January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: The Fourth Circuit rejected facial Commerce Clause and First Amendment challenges to the Freedom of Access to Clinic Entrances Act. FACE prohibits intentional force, threats of force, and physical obstruction undertaken because a person is obtaining or providing reproductive-health services, and it preserves activity protected by the First Amendment. The action did not arise from a prosecution based solely on peaceful prayer, leafletting, picketing, or advocacy. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/47/642/471880/))

Questions presented: Whether FACE is within Congress’s commerce authority after *Lopez*, and whether it facially targets, burdens, or chills protected anti-abortion expression.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *United States v. Lopez* forbids regulation of noneconomic local conduct through an attenuated chain from violence to social costs and national productivity, but permits regulation of channels, instrumentalities, economic activity substantially affecting an actual interstate market, and local conduct reasonably necessary to prevent impairment of a valid economic regime. *Madsen* confirms that government may regulate demonstrated obstruction, threats, and access interference while leaving protected advocacy intact. `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

No facial injunction shall issue. A future enforcement action remains subject to an as-applied challenge if protected advocacy, ambiguous rhetoric, passive presence, de minimis delay, or selective viewpoint enforcement supplies the basis for liability.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Thomas as to the First Amendment holding | Supports affirmance | Eight-Justice Commerce Clause holding; unanimous First Amendment holding |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Thomas | None | Supports the First Amendment judgment; opposes the Commerce Clause judgment | Would require a more direct jurisdictional or comprehensive-regime basis for federal regulation of local obstruction |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Commerce Clause authority over access-directed obstruction

Holding: FACE is within Congress’s commerce authority because it regulates a class of intentional force, threats of force, and physical obstruction directed at preventing the provision or receipt of reproductive-health services in an interstate service market.

Operative rule: Congress may regulate access-directed conduct whose object and operation are to prevent transactions and services in an interstate market. The constitutional connection must arise from the regulated conduct’s direct interference with the market itself, not from a generalized chain through social costs, health consequences, productivity, or crime.

Reasoning: Clinics purchase supplies, employ labor, participate in service networks, and serve patients and providers who travel across state lines. More importantly, FACE does not attach federal liability to every local assault or trespass near a clinic. Its operative elements require conduct directed at stopping persons from obtaining or providing the defined service. Intentional blockades and coercive interference prevent the underlying service transactions from occurring and directly impair the interstate market Congress protected.

Limits and questions left open: FACE’s access-directed conduct and intent elements must perform real work in every application. The decision does not authorize federal prosecution of every local assault, trespass, protest, or disturbance associated with a medical facility.

Legal basis: The Commerce Clause and Necessary and Proper Clause.

Authority of the proposition: Stone’s opinion is joined on this issue by Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, an eight-Justice majority.

##### Facial First Amendment challenge

Holding: FACE does not facially violate the First Amendment because it regulates force, threats of force, and physical obstruction according to their coercive effect on access, not according to agreement or disagreement with an anti-abortion viewpoint.

Operative rule: Peaceful advocacy, prayer, leafletting, picketing, persuasion, and association remain protected. The access-related intent element identifies the conduct Congress regulated; it does not make opposition to abortion an element of liability. Difficult boundary applications must be addressed on their facts rather than through facial invalidation of the statute’s plainly legitimate sweep.

Reasoning: The same anti-abortion message remains lawful when communicated without force, threat, or material physical obstruction. FACE also protects the provision of reproductive-health services generally rather than criminalizing a particular political opinion. Its First Amendment savings provision cannot cure an unconstitutional prosecution, but it confirms that protected expression lies outside the statute’s lawful reach.

Limits and questions left open: A prosecution resting on ambiguous rhetoric, passive presence, immaterial delay, protected counseling, or viewpoint-selective enforcement remains open to as-applied review. RFRA and other issues outside the controlled grant are not decided.

Legal basis: The First Amendment.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support the facial First Amendment holding.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone distinguishes direct obstruction of an interstate service market from the attenuated consequences rejected in *Lopez*, and distinguishes regulation of coercive conduct from suppression of anti-abortion advocacy.

#### Material Separate Writings

Thomas agrees that FACE does not facially suppress protected expression. He dissents from the commerce holding because the statute reaches local noneconomic obstruction without requiring a case-specific interstate element and, in his view, is not an integral component of a comprehensive federal economic regime. That Commerce Clause proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Lopez* is clarified rather than narrowed. Generalized economic consequences remain insufficient, but conduct directed at preventing the provision and receipt of services in an actual interstate market may be regulated because the interference operates on the market itself. *Madsen*’s distinction between protected advocacy and access interference is applied, while its injunction-specific fit standard is not extended to this generally applicable statute.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court did not historically issue a merits decision. The simulation affirms the Fourth Circuit’s historical judgment sustaining FACE against the facial Commerce Clause and First Amendment challenges, while making *Lopez*’s direct-market boundary controlling Supreme Court law. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/47/642/471880/))

Research sources: The published Fourth Circuit opinion, FACE’s enacted text, and date-eligible circuit authority for the statute’s elements, regulated conduct, historical result, and post-*Lopez* commerce analysis. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/47/642/471880/))

### *Beadle v. City of Tampa*, 42 F.3d 633

Decided February 5, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: Beadle’s Seventh-day Adventist faith prevented work from sundown Friday to sundown Saturday. Tampa’s police academy required recruits to rotate among field-training officers and assignments in a continuously operating department. The City gave Beadle his first Saturday off but did not permit trades between recruits or offer a generally workable swap system. The Eleventh Circuit nevertheless affirmed judgment for the City. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/42/633/604504/))

Question presented: Whether Title VII required a permanent Sabbath exemption from academy assignments and whether the resulting scheduling and training burdens constituted undue hardship.

Controlling simulated law before decision: No simulated holding displaces the then-governing rules of *Trans World Airlines v. Hardison* and *Ansonia Board of Education v. Philbrook*.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas. Opposing: Ginsburg and Breyer.

The Title VII judgment for Tampa remains in force. The Court does not rely on the premise that Tampa supplied a workable shift-swap accommodation.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | Breyer | Would vacate and remand | Would require a more concrete showing that accommodation short of permanent exemption was examined and that the asserted burdens were unavoidable |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Permanent exemption and undue hardship

Holding: Under the Title VII accommodation standard then controlling, Tampa was not required to guarantee Beadle a permanent exemption from required academy assignments when the record showed that the exemption would impose more than a de minimis operational burden on the rotating field-training system.

Reasoning: The academy used rotating assignments so recruits would receive varied supervision and training. A standing exception covering every Friday evening and Saturday would not resolve an isolated conflict. It would repeatedly constrain assignment of training officers, remove Beadle from required rotations, and transfer recurring instructional and staffing burdens to other participants in a continuously operating police department. Those demonstrated effects satisfy the *Hardison* standard then in force.

Application: Affirmance rests on undue hardship from the requested permanent exemption. It does not rest on a finding that trades or swaps were actually available, because the record shows that academy trainees were not permitted to exchange their assigned duties.

Limits and questions left open: The decision creates no categorical public-safety exception. Speculative inconvenience, coworker hostility, or a nominal accommodation that is predictably unavailable would not establish undue hardship. The City remains free to offer broader voluntary accommodation, and a materially different future practice would require its own analysis.

Legal basis: Title VII, 42 U.S.C. §§ 2000e(j) and 2000e-2.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the undue-hardship holding based on the proved recurring effect of a guaranteed permanent exemption and does not rely on an unsupported shift-swap premise.

#### Material Separate Writings

Ginsburg, joined by Breyer, would remand. The dissent concludes that Tampa failed to investigate or provide a genuine accommodation and that the operational evidence did not establish why narrower schedule adjustments could not be made. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Hardison* and *Ansonia* are applied without alteration. The decision establishes no separate rule for police departments or public-safety employment and does not treat an unavailable swap arrangement as a reasonable accommodation.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court did not historically decide the case on the merits. The simulation affirms the Eleventh Circuit’s historical judgment, but confines the ground to undue hardship from the demanded permanent exemption and corrects the record concerning the absence of shift trading. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/42/633/604504/))

Research sources: The published Eleventh Circuit opinion for the academy procedures, Sabbath conflict, unavailable trades, operational evidence, historical judgment, and corrected decision date. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/42/633/604504/))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### *Sherman v. Community Consolidated School District 21*, 980 F.2d 437

Decided February 12, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: The Seventh Circuit affirmed summary judgment for the School District. Illinois required daily recitation of the Pledge in public elementary schools, but the accepted record established that Richard Sherman could remain silent and seated without standing, saluting, leaving the room, or suffering punishment. The controlled grant concerns only the Establishment Clause consequences of the school-led Pledge containing “under God.” ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F2/980/437/335765/))

Question presented: Whether a public school violates the Establishment Clause by conducting voluntary recitation of the Pledge of Allegiance containing “under God” when students remain free not to participate.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue* protects clearly private religious expression admitted to a school access program on neutral terms. *Kiryas Joel*, *Capitol Square*, *Rosenberger*, and *Chabad-Lubavitch* preserve the separate constitutional question presented when government itself selects and delivers the expression.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.

Supporting: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Breyer. Opposing: Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg.

The School District may continue the voluntary daily Pledge under the conditions established by this record. No damages issue is decided, and the Attorney General is not reinstated as a defendant.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stone | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Would reverse and remand for limited prospective relief | Treats “under God” as a government-led religious affirmation distinct from compelled-speech analysis |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Voluntary school-led Pledge containing “under God”

Holding: A public school does not violate the Establishment Clause by leading the Pledge of Allegiance in its established form, including “under God,” when the exercise operates as a patriotic civic observance rather than a devotional exercise and no student is required to recite, stand, salute, leave, or otherwise manifest agreement.

Operative rule: A school-led civic observance may include a brief, historically embedded acknowledgment of religion when its object and operation remain patriotic rather than devotional and students retain a complete, practical right of nonparticipation. The government may not use that principle to conduct prayer, religious instruction, profession of faith, or an exercise that penalizes or formally identifies dissenters.

Reasoning: The Pledge’s principal function is to affirm allegiance to the Republic and its constitutional commitments. The two challenged words do not direct prayer, invoke divine assistance, expound doctrine, or ask students to perform a religious act. Their setting within a familiar patriotic exercise and the absence of any official consequence for silence distinguish the practice from the school prayers invalidated in *Engel*, *Schempp*, and *Lee*. *Barnette* independently prevents compelled participation, and the record demonstrates that the District honored that boundary.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not permit punishment, compelled standing, compelled departure, teacher-led prayer, devotional elaboration on the Pledge, or selective treatment of students who decline to join. It does not govern a materially different exercise whose language or administration converts civic acknowledgment into religious profession.

Legal basis: The Establishment Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Author of the dissent, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg.

Announced ground: Stone would hold that freedom to remain silent cures compelled profession under *Barnette* but does not answer whether government may place a theistic proposition inside its own daily affirmation of civic allegiance. Because the District selects the words, leads the exercise, and presents the proposition to an elementary-school audience, Stone regards “under God” as governmental religious expression rather than private speech or neutral historical instruction. He would permit a secular school-led Pledge and voluntary private recitation of the current text.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Good News/Good Sports Club* is distinguished. Its protected expression was privately sponsored and admitted through a neutral access program; the Pledge is government selected and government led. The present decision creates a narrow rule for a voluntary, nondevotional civic observance and does not expand the private-forum attribution rules.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court historically denied certiorari, leaving the Seventh Circuit’s judgment in force without a Supreme Court merits precedent. The simulation reaches the merits and affirms 5-4, converting the lower court’s result into a narrow controlling rule for the voluntary school-led Pledge. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F2/980/437/335765/))

Research sources: The published Seventh Circuit opinion for the statute, summary-judgment record, nonparticipation safeguards, lower-court judgment, and historical procedural anchor. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F2/980/437/335765/))

### *Behrens v. Pelletier*, 516 U.S. 299

Argued November 7, 1995 | Decided February 21, 1996

From: The Ninth Circuit entertained Behrens’s first appeal from denial of qualified immunity at the pleading stage but dismissed a second appeal after discovery and denial of summary judgment, applying a one-interlocutory-appeal limitation. The later order rested on a materially developed record. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/299/))

Question presented: Whether the Ninth Circuit had jurisdiction over the separable legal qualified-immunity question presented by the summary-judgment order.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Johnson v. Jones* permits immediate review of the legal immunity question on assumed facts but bars appellate reweighing of evidentiary sufficiency. *Swint v. Chambers County Commission* separately bars enlargement of interlocutory jurisdiction to nonappealable associated matters.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg. Opposing: Stevens and Breyer.

The Ninth Circuit shall exercise jurisdiction over any separable legal immunity issue presented by the summary-judgment order. It shall dismiss any contention that merely disputes whether the evidence supports Pelletier’s factual account.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens | Would affirm dismissal of the second appeal | Would limit an official to one pretrial immunity appeal absent materially exceptional circumstances |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Successive collateral-order appeal of qualified immunity

Holding: An earlier appeal from denial of qualified immunity at the pleading stage does not exhaust immediate appellate review of a later summary-judgment order that presents a separable legal immunity question on the developed record.

Operative rule: Each order denying qualified immunity is assessed according to what that order decides. A later order is immediately appealable to the extent it determines whether conduct, taken under the facts assumed by the district court or the plaintiff-supported version of disputed facts, violates clearly established law. Immediate review does not extend to whether the record is sufficient to prove those facts.

Reasoning: Pleading-stage review accepts allegations, while summary judgment tests the legal consequences of a developed evidentiary record. The officer’s right not to stand trial on a legally barred claim would be incompletely protected if an earlier appeal foreclosed review of the distinct legal question presented later. That conclusion does not diminish *Johnson*: appellate jurisdiction stops when the appellant’s argument depends on rejecting the factual version the district court was required to assume.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not authorize repeated appeals from unchanged orders, review of discovery management, reconsideration of witness credibility, or repackaging of an evidence-sufficiency argument as a legal issue.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. § 1291 and the collateral-order doctrine.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Scalia’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s order-specific finality rule and its preservation of *Johnson*’s boundary against factual sufficiency review.

#### Material Separate Writings

Breyer, joined by Stevens, would permit only one interlocutory immunity appeal in the ordinary case because successive review increases delay and fragments litigation. The dissent would leave later immunity issues for review after final judgment unless the first appeal could not reasonably have presented them. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Johnson v. Jones* is applied and clarified. Its factual-sufficiency bar remains unchanged, while the present decision establishes that a materially later order may generate a new appealable legal immunity question. *Swint* remains unaffected.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, 7-2 lineup, Scalia authorship, and principal jurisdictional rule match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the Chief Justice’s historical join. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/299/))

Research sources: Officially reported materials for the two immunity motions, successive appeals, argument and decision dates, historical judgment, author, joins, and dissent. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/299/))

### *Peacock v. Thomas*, 516 U.S. 349

Argued November 6, 1995 | Decided February 21, 1996

From: Thomas first obtained an ERISA judgment against Tru-Tech, not Peacock. After collection efforts failed, Thomas filed a new federal action seeking to pierce the corporate veil and impose the existing judgment personally on Peacock based on alleged transfers, concealment, and misuse of corporate assets. The Fourth Circuit sustained jurisdiction and the resulting personal judgment. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/349/))

Question presented: Whether the later action against Peacock required an independent basis of federal subject-matter jurisdiction.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Kokkonen v. Guardian Life Insurance Co.* confines ancillary enforcement jurisdiction to matters incorporated into the original decree, jurisdiction expressly retained, or controversies otherwise supported by an independent jurisdictional grant.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 8-1.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

The personal judgment against Peacock is vacated. The action shall be dismissed for lack of subject-matter jurisdiction, without prejudice to a suit in a court possessing an independent jurisdictional basis. Ordinary execution against Tru-Tech and its property remains available.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | None | Would vacate the particular relief but preserve ancillary authority to compel restoration of assets | Would permit a narrower judgment-enforcement proceeding against a person controlling the debtor |

#### Threshold Decision

Threshold holding: A federal court’s ancillary enforcement jurisdiction does not support a new action imposing personal liability for an existing judgment on a nonparty through new facts and legal theories. Such an action requires an independent statutory basis of federal jurisdiction.

Reasoning and application: Federal courts may execute their judgments, garnish or attach property belonging to the judgment debtor, determine ownership questions necessary to execution, and use authorized supplementary process. Thomas’s action did something different. It sought a new judgment against Peacock based on alleged fraudulent transfers, corporate control, veil piercing, and personal misconduct. Those issues require adjudication of new liability, defenses, and facts. Neither the importance of collecting a federal judgment nor ERISA’s general remedial purposes enlarges the jurisdiction Congress supplied.

Effect on judgment below: The judgment against Peacock is vacated and the action dismissed without prejudice. No veil-piercing, fraudulent-transfer, or personal-liability merits issue is decided.

Questions not reached: Whether Peacock diverted assets, whether state law permits recovery, whether diversity jurisdiction exists, and whether any separate federal cause of action reaches the alleged conduct.

Legal basis: Article III, 28 U.S.C. §§ 1331 and 1367, and the limits of ancillary jurisdiction.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and direct dismissal.

Public role: Joined Thomas’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s distinction between execution directed to the judgment debtor or its property and creation of new personal liability against a different party.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens would preserve jurisdiction to order a controlling insider to restore assets fraudulently removed from the judgment debtor, although he agrees that the judgment requiring Peacock to satisfy the entire debt went too far. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Kokkonen* is clarified beyond settlement enforcement. Ancillary jurisdiction may implement an existing federal decree, but it does not create adjudicatory power over a new defendant and a new liability controversy. The decision does not limit garnishment, receivership, contempt, or other process directed to property legally belonging to the original debtor.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated 8-1 judgment, Thomas authorship, complete majority lineup, and Stevens dissent match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the Chief Justice’s historical join. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/349/))

Research sources: Officially reported materials for the original ERISA judgment, later veil-piercing action, jurisdictional holding, historical judgment, lineup, and dissent. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/349/))

### *Voting Rights Coalition v. Wilson*, 60 F.3d 1411

Decided February 26, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: Governor Wilson directed California officials not to implement the National Voter Registration Act on constitutional grounds. The Ninth Circuit affirmed an injunction requiring state motor-vehicle, public-assistance, rehabilitation, and election officials to perform the Act’s registration duties. California’s federal and state election systems used substantially integrated administrative machinery. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/60/1411/565929/))

Question presented: Whether the NVRA validly requires California officials and agencies to provide registration opportunities for federal elections, and whether the compliance injunction extends beyond the federal-election duty.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *U.S. Term Limits, Inc. v. Thornton* distinguishes permissible regulation of election administration from forbidden addition of substantive candidate qualifications. No simulated anti-commandeering rule displaces the Elections Clause’s specific allocation of federal-election administration.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

California officials shall implement the NVRA for federal elections. The injunction reaches state-election registration only where California law integrates the rolls or where that incidental operation is reasonably necessary to administer the federal-election duties. It does not independently prescribe qualifications or registration rules for state-only elections.

Justice Kennedy delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Federal registration duties administered through state offices

Holding: The Elections Clause and Necessary and Proper Clause authorize Congress to require state election officials and designated state offices to administer registration procedures for federal elections. A State’s executive officials may not nullify those enacted duties by directing noncompliance.

Operative rule: Congress may alter state rules governing the times, places, and manner of congressional elections and may require state administrative machinery to carry out reasonably related federal-election procedures. Incidental operation on an integrated state registration system is permissible when inseparable from or reasonably necessary to federal compliance. The authority does not independently extend to severable state-only elections or state voter qualifications.

Reasoning: Registration determines which eligible voters appear on the rolls and how applications for federal elections are received and maintained. It is therefore part of election administration rather than a new qualification for office or a general exercise of federal police power. The Constitution itself contemplates state administration subject to congressional alteration. Related recordkeeping and agency-registration requirements are reasonably tied to making that federal scheme effective. Accountability is not obscured: Congress enacted the duties, federal law supplies their content, and the injunction identifies the federal source.

Application: California already administered federal and state elections through overlapping offices and rolls. The district court could require those officials to process federal-election registrations and maintain the records necessary to make that system work. It could not treat the NVRA as an independent federal code for a hypothetical severable state-only election.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not authorize Congress to prescribe state voter qualifications, regulate an election having no federal component, or compel consolidation of otherwise severable state and federal rolls.

Legal basis: Article I, § 4 and the Necessary and Proper Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s specific Elections Clause allocation and its limitation of the injunction to federal-election administration and necessary integrated operation.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*U.S. Term Limits* is applied and clarified. Registration procedures are part of the manner of conducting federal elections, while candidate and voter qualifications remain distinct. The decision establishes that a general anti-commandeering objection does not override the Constitution’s express federal authority to alter state-administered congressional election machinery.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court did not historically decide the case on the merits. The simulation affirms the Ninth Circuit’s historical judgment and makes its central Elections Clause conclusion controlling Supreme Court law, while expressly limiting the injunction’s treatment of state-only elections. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/60/1411/565929/))

Research sources: The published Ninth Circuit opinion for the Governor’s directive, covered offices, injunction, federalism arguments, lower-court judgment, and historical procedural anchor. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/60/1411/565929/))

### *Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein*, 516 U.S. 367

Argued November 27, 1995 | Decided February 27, 1996

From: A Delaware shareholder class action settled claims arising from Matsushita’s acquisition of MCA and released related state and federal claims, including Exchange Act claims within exclusive federal jurisdiction. The Delaware court approved the settlement after notice, opt-out procedures, and a fairness hearing. The Ninth Circuit declined to give the judgment preclusive effect over the federal securities claims. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/367/))

Questions presented: Whether 28 U.S.C. § 1738 applies to the Delaware class judgment, whether Delaware law gives the release preclusive effect, whether exclusive federal jurisdiction displaces § 1738, and what due-process objections remain open.

Controlling simulated law before decision: No prior simulated holding addresses a state class settlement releasing claims within exclusive federal jurisdiction. *Marrese* and *Shutts* supply the surviving statutory sequence and constitutional boundary.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens and Ginsburg.

The Ninth Circuit’s categorical refusal to apply § 1738 is reversed. The judgment is remanded for proceedings consistent with the controlling propositions below, including any preserved collateral due-process challenge not foreclosed by prior adjudication.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer in full; Stone and Stevens as to the facts, § 1738 framework, and no-implied-repeal holding | Supports reversal and remand for the six full joiners and Stone; Stevens does not join the judgment | Full opinion for six Justices; partial joins as stated |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone | None | Supplies the seventh vote to reverse and remand | Would leave the content of Delaware preclusion law and any preserved due-process challenge for the lower federal courts |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | None | Opposes the Court’s reversal while supporting remand for lower-court analysis | Joins the § 1738 framework and no-implied-repeal portions; rejects the Court’s Delaware-law determination |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Ginsburg | Stevens; Souter as to the due-process discussion | Opposes the Court’s reversal while supporting remand | Would leave Delaware law to the lower courts and emphasizes collateral review of adequate representation |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Full faith and credit for a settlement releasing exclusively federal claims

Holding: A federal court may not withhold the effect required by § 1738 from a state-court class judgment merely because the settlement releases claims that could have been adjudicated on the merits only in federal court.

Operative rule: The federal court first determines the effect the rendering State gives its judgment. It then applies that effect unless Congress expressly or by irreconcilable implication displaced § 1738, or the judgment is not entitled to recognition because the rendering court lacked jurisdiction over the action before it or the judgment failed constitutional due process.

Reasoning: The Delaware court exercised jurisdiction over the state-law shareholder action and approved a negotiated release. Approval of a release does not amount to adjudication on the merits of the exclusively federal claim. Section 1738 governs judgments, not merely claims a state court could independently try, and an exception cannot be created from a judicial preference for preserving a separate federal forum.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. § 1738 and the Full Faith and Credit Clause’s implementing legislation.

Authority of the proposition: Thomas’s opinion is joined on this proposition by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer, and Stevens, an eight-Justice majority.

##### Delaware preclusion law

Holding: Delaware would give the approved settlement judgment preclusive effect over the released Exchange Act claims because they arose from the covered transaction, were expressly included in the release, and were held subject to the class judgment.

Reasoning: Delaware’s class-action law permits a judicially approved settlement to release transaction-related claims that could not themselves have been adjudicated in the Court of Chancery, provided the court possessed jurisdiction over the underlying action and the class judgment satisfied the governing procedural requirements.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide a preserved collateral claim that the class was inadequately represented or that the judgment otherwise failed due process.

Authority of the proposition: Thomas’s opinion is joined at this scope by O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer, producing an actual six-Justice majority. Stone, Stevens, and Ginsburg would leave the state-law determination to the lower federal courts.

##### No implied repeal by exclusive Exchange Act jurisdiction

Holding: The Exchange Act’s exclusive federal-jurisdiction provision does not impliedly repeal § 1738 for a state judgment approving the release of Exchange Act claims.

Reasoning: Exclusive jurisdiction prevents a state court from adjudicating an Exchange Act cause of action. It does not forbid litigants from releasing such a claim while settling a controversy properly before the state court. The statutes therefore operate together without irreconcilable conflict.

Authority of the proposition: Thomas’s opinion is joined on this proposition by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer, and Stevens, an eight-Justice majority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined the Court’s factual, § 1738, and no-implied-repeal portions; concurred separately in the judgment.

Announced ground: Stone agrees that the Ninth Circuit used the wrong categorical rule and that exclusive federal jurisdiction does not displace § 1738. He would not decide Delaware preclusion law in the first instance. He would direct the Ninth Circuit to determine that law and then resolve any preserved objection concerning notice, opt-out opportunity, representation, or settlement fairness. Stone’s vote does not approve preclusion by a constitutionally defective class judgment.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens agrees with the Court’s statutory framework but would not decide Delaware law or reverse the judgment on that basis. Ginsburg, joined by Stevens and by Souter on the due-process discussion, emphasizes that adequate representation is a continuing constitutional prerequisite and remains open to collateral examination when properly preserved. Those propositions are noncontrolling except for the general due-process boundary already recognized by the Court.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for state class judgments releasing exclusively federal claims. Section 1738 remains the starting point; exclusive federal jurisdiction alone creates no exception. A constitutionally adequate class process remains a prerequisite to binding absent members.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court also reversed and remanded 7-2. Historically, Justice Thomas’s opinion received six additional full joins, including Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justice Scalia, while Justice Stevens joined specified parts and Justice Ginsburg wrote separately. In the simulation, Stone replaces the Chief Justice’s full historical join with a partial join and concurrence preserving lower-court determination of Delaware law, but still supplies the seventh vote for reversal. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/367/))

Research sources: Officially reported materials for the Delaware settlement, federal securities action, statutory holdings, historical 7-2 judgment, partial joins, due-process reservation, and separate writings. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/367/))

### *Norfolk & Western Railway Co. v. Hiles*, 516 U.S. 400

Argued January 8, 1996 | Decided February 27, 1996

From: Hiles was injured while manually realigning an off-center drawbar. Drawbars move laterally during ordinary railroad operations and may remain off center after uncoupling. The record did not establish that the coupler failed after proper alignment or that its automatic mechanism was defective. The Illinois Appellate Court nevertheless sustained absolute statutory liability. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/400/))

Question presented: Whether ordinary drawbar misalignment, without failure of a properly set coupler, establishes a violation of the Safety Appliance Act.

Controlling simulated law before decision: No prior simulated holding construes the automatic-coupler requirement.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

The statutory liability judgment is reversed. Any independently preserved negligence or FELA claim remains for ordinary lower-court treatment without direction from this Court.

Justice Thomas delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Ordinary drawbar misalignment

Holding: Ordinary lateral misalignment of a drawbar does not itself violate the Safety Appliance Act when the coupler performs its required automatic function once the drawbars are properly positioned.

Operative rule: The Act imposes an absolute duty to provide and maintain couplers that couple automatically by impact when properly set for coupling. Liability follows from failure of that required function, not merely from the need to realign a drawbar that moved laterally during ordinary operation.

Reasoning: The statute addresses the performance of the automatic coupling appliance. Lateral movement is part of the drawbar’s ordinary mechanical design and permits railcars to negotiate curves. Treating every off-center position as a statutory defect would convert the Act from an absolute performance duty into a guarantee that an ordinary operational condition will never occur.

Application: No evidence showed that the coupler failed after proper positioning or that a defect caused the misalignment. Hiles therefore was not entitled to judgment as a matter of law on the statutory claim.

Limits and questions left open: A recurrent misalignment caused by defective maintenance, improper setup, damaged equipment, or failure after proper positioning may present a different case.

Legal basis: The Safety Appliance Act.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse.

Public role: Joined Thomas’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision supplies the first controlling simulated distinction between failure of an automatic coupler’s required function and ordinary operational misalignment. The Act’s absolute-duty character remains intact within the former category.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment, Thomas authorship, statutory construction, and reversal of the Illinois Appellate Court match the historical decision. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/400/))

Research sources: Officially reported materials for the mechanical record, Illinois appellate posture, argument date, statutory holding, historical judgment, and unanimous authorship. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/400/))

### *Action for Children’s Television v. FCC*, 58 F.3d 654

Decided March 4, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: Congress and the FCC barred indecent broadcasts from 6 a.m. to midnight for most stations but permitted certain public stations that ended broadcasting by midnight to begin airing such material at 10 p.m. The en banc D.C. Circuit concluded that the differential treatment bore no demonstrated relationship to protecting children and directed a uniform 10 p.m. to 6 a.m. safe harbor. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/58/654/574688/))

Question presented: Whether the record constitutionally supports the midnight boundary and differential treatment of broadcasters, and what severable channeling rule may remain in force.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Sable Communications* supplies demanding review of a content-based restriction on protected indecent speech. *Pacifica* permits narrow regulation in the distinctive broadcast setting. *Turner Broadcasting* contributes record discipline and independent review but not the governing tier, while *Madsen* remains confined to injunctions.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

The FCC may enforce a uniform prohibition from 6 a.m. to 10 p.m. under the present statute and record. A more restrictive boundary requires new lawful authority and a record demonstrating why materially less restrictive channeling is inadequate.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse the uniform 10 p.m. judgment | Would sustain the midnight boundary or sever the favorable public-station exception rather than enlarge the safe harbor |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Time channeling of protected broadcast indecency

Holding: The 6 a.m. to midnight prohibition is not narrowly tailored on the present record because Congress allowed similarly situated public stations to broadcast after 10 p.m. without identifying a corresponding difference in the risk of exposing children. A uniform 10 p.m. to 6 a.m. safe harbor is the maximum restriction supported by the severable statute and existing evidence.

Operative rule: Government may channel indecent but nonobscene broadcast programming to protect children only when adults retain a realistic opportunity to receive the material and the selected hours are supported by evidence that materially less restrictive channeling would inadequately protect the governmental interest. Comparable broadcasters may not receive materially different safe harbors without a justification connected to the asserted harm.

Reasoning: Broadcast’s accessibility and presence in the home permit greater time regulation than would be constitutional for print or ordinary private communications. That reduced-protection principle does not eliminate narrow tailoring. Substantial adult audiences remain during the late-evening hours, while child audiences decline. Congress’s own 10 p.m. exception shows that broadcasting after that hour is not inherently incompatible with the asserted interest, and the Government supplied no risk-based explanation for treating equivalent content differently according to the station’s public or commercial status.

Application: Removing the unsupported additional two-hour restriction, rather than extending it to the favored public stations, preserves the greatest amount of protected speech while leaving a substantial and administrable channeling rule in force.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not freeze the safe harbor permanently, decide every application of the indecency definition, or bar a later rule supported by materially different audience evidence, technology, or parental-control mechanisms. Settled-core enforcement remains subject to ordinary as-applied notice review.

Legal basis: The First Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone publicly joins the Court’s requirement that adults retain a realistic channel and that differential burdens on comparable broadcasters be connected to the demonstrated risk. He announces no additional ground.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would not infer from the public-station exception that all broadcasters must receive the earlier safe harbor. The dissent would preserve Congress’s midnight rule by severing or restricting the exceptional treatment rather than enlarging access to indecent programming. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Pacifica* is confined to its narrow broadcast rationale and does not authorize an effectively all-day prohibition without adequate fit. *Sable* supplies the controlling narrow-tailoring requirement. *Turner Broadcasting* is applied only for evidence-based review of burden and fit; its intermediate-scrutiny classification is not extended to this content-based rule.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court did not historically issue a merits decision. The simulation affirms the en banc D.C. Circuit’s historical remedial result and establishes a controlling Supreme Court rule requiring a uniform 10 p.m. safe harbor on the present record. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/58/654/574688/))

Research sources: The published en banc opinion for the statutory hours, public-station exception, audience evidence, governing First Amendment analysis, lower-court judgment, and historical procedural anchor. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/58/654/574688/))

### *Dalton v. Little Rock Family Planning Services*, 516 U.S. 474

Decided March 18, 1996

From: Arkansas Amendment 68 prohibited public funding of abortions except when necessary to save the mother’s life. During the relevant period, federal Medicaid law required participating States to fund additional Hyde Amendment categories. The lower courts enjoined Amendment 68 in its entirety for as long as Arkansas participated in Medicaid. The Court granted review only of that blanket remedial scope. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/95-1025.ZPC.html))

Question presented: Whether federal preemption extends beyond the applications of Amendment 68 that conflict with federal Medicaid funding requirements.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick* requires an identified impossibility or obstruction conflict and does not permit preemption to extend beyond the operative federal command.

#### Judgment

Certiorari granted; judgment vacated and case remanded, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

The district court shall enjoin Arkansas officials from denying Medicaid funding in every category federal law requires while Arkansas participates in the program. It shall not disable Amendment 68 in nonconflicting applications.

The Court issued a unanimous per curiam opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Conflict-specific preemption and relief

Holding: Amendment 68 is preempted only insofar as it prevents Arkansas from paying for abortions that federal Medicaid law requires the State to fund during the relevant period. The Supremacy Clause does not authorize blanket invalidation of the state provision’s nonconflicting operation.

Operative rule: When a state law has both conflicting and nonconflicting applications, federal equitable relief must reach the actual impossibility or obstruction and no further, unless governing severability law makes narrower operation unavailable.

Reasoning: Arkansas may not accept Medicaid participation while refusing a mandatory federal condition. That direct conflict requires complete relief for eligible patients and providers. Federal law, however, does not require every public funding source or every abortion category covered by Amendment 68. Disabling the entire provision would extend federal supremacy beyond the enacted obligation.

Limits and questions left open: The Court accepts without deciding the lower courts’ interpretation of the operative Hyde Amendment. It does not decide future appropriations language, non-Medicaid state funding, or an independently presented substantive constitutional challenge.

Legal basis: The Supremacy Clause and federal equitable-remedy principles.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand.

Public role: Joined the per curiam Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s conflict-specific preemption and remedial-scope analysis.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Freightliner* is applied without alteration. The decision confirms that identifying a federal conflict and determining the breadth of equitable relief are separate inquiries.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court unanimously reversed and remanded after granting review only of the blanket-invalidation question. The simulation uses vacatur and remand, preserving Stone’s controlled formal disposition, while adopting the same narrow conflict-preemption rule and remedial boundary. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/95-1025.ZPC.html))

Research sources: The official per curiam opinion for the corrected docket, amendment, Medicaid conflict, limited grant, historical disposition, and narrow remedial holding. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/95-1025.ZPC.html))
