### *Varity Corp. v. Howe*, 516 U.S. 489

Argued November 1, 1995 | Decided March 19, 1996

From: Varity, acting both as employer and plan administrator, convened a meeting to induce employees to transfer to a financially troubled affiliate and represented that their benefits would remain secure. The affiliate was insolvent from inception, and the transferring employees later lost coverage. The lower courts found deliberate fiduciary deception and ordered individualized equitable restoration. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/489/))

Questions presented: Whether Varity acted as an ERISA fiduciary when making the representations, whether it breached the duty of loyalty, and whether § 502(a)(3) authorizes individualized equitable relief when no more specific ERISA subsection adequately remedies the injury.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust* applies ERISA’s functional “to the extent” fiduciary rule. Earlier the same term, *Peacock v. Thomas* held that § 502(a)(3) does not create jurisdiction over a new nonparty-liability action unsupported by an actual ERISA violation.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas.

The individualized equitable relief may stand to the extent it restores the employees to the benefits position lost through the fiduciary breach and avoids duplicative recovery.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | O’Connor, Scalia | Would reverse | Rejects individualized recovery under § 502(a)(3) and would confine the relevant fiduciary remedies more narrowly |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Fiduciary status in communications about plan benefits

Holding: Varity acted as an ERISA fiduciary when it deliberately misled plan beneficiaries about the security of their benefits while speaking in an authorized plan-administration capacity.

Operative rule: An employer does not act as a fiduciary merely because a business decision affects a plan. It does act as a fiduciary when, exercising plan-administration authority, it communicates about benefits for the purpose of guiding beneficiaries’ plan choices. Deliberate material deception in that capacity violates the duty of loyalty.

Reasoning: Varity did more than announce a corporate restructuring. It explained plan consequences, answered benefit questions as administrator, invoked the employees’ reliance on its plan authority, and concealed known information about the affiliate’s financial condition. Functional capacity, not corporate title or the meeting’s mixed business context, controls.

Legal basis: ERISA §§ 3(21)(A) and 404.

##### Individualized equitable relief under § 502(a)(3)

Holding: Section 502(a)(3) authorizes appropriate individualized equitable relief to redress an actual ERISA violation when no more specific remedial subsection adequately reaches the injury.

Operative rule: The claimant must establish an ERISA violation and seek relief traditionally available in equity. Section 502(a)(3) remains unavailable for duplicative recovery, legal damages relabeled as equity, or circumvention of a more specific provision that fully remedies the same injury.

Reasoning: The employees lost coverage because Varity used fiduciary deception to induce surrender of their existing plan position. Reinstatement or equivalent equitable restoration directly addresses that breach. Treating the statute’s other remedial provisions as categorically exclusive even when they provide no remedy to these plaintiffs would deprive the enacted catchall of practical operation.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not convert every employer statement into fiduciary conduct, authorize compensatory or punitive damages, or permit double recovery.

Legal basis: ERISA § 502(a)(3).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Breyer’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone publicly joins the functional fiduciary-capacity rule, the conclusion that deliberate deception breached loyalty, and the limited availability of traditional equitable relief when no more specific ERISA remedy is adequate.

#### Material Separate Writings

Thomas, joined by O’Connor and Scalia, would read ERISA’s remedial structure to deny the individualized relief ordered here. The dissent warns that the Court’s construction may duplicate plan-focused fiduciary remedies and expand employer exposure beyond the statutory design. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*John Hancock* is applied and clarified: fiduciary status follows the function exercised, including plan-administration communications, and does not attach to all employer conduct. *Peacock* is distinguished. *Peacock* involved a new action against a person not liable for an ERISA violation; *Varity* involves equitable relief against the fiduciary that committed the proved breach.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated 6-3 judgment, Breyer authorship, majority lineup, and Thomas dissent joined by O’Connor and Scalia match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the Chief Justice’s historical majority position. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/489/))

Research sources: Officially reported materials for the meeting, deception findings, fiduciary analysis, equitable remedy, historical judgment, author, joins, and dissent. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/516/489/))

### *Wisconsin v. City of New York; Oklahoma v. City of New York; Department of Commerce v. City of New York*, 517 U.S. 1

Argued January 10, 1996 | Decided March 20, 1996

From: The 1990 census produced an acknowledged differential undercount. The Census Bureau developed a post-enumeration survey adjustment, but the Secretary declined to substitute the adjusted figures after considering technical uncertainty, distributive accuracy among States, and historical census practice. The district court sustained that decision; the Second Circuit applied heightened scrutiny and reversed. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/1/))

Question presented: Whether the Secretary’s decision not to use the PES adjustment was subject to heightened scrutiny or instead fell within the constitutional discretion governing the actual enumeration.

Controlling simulated law before decision: No census-specific simulated standard controls. The Census Clause, 13 U.S.C. § 141(a), and surviving principles from *Department of Commerce v. Montana* and *Franklin v. Massachusetts* govern the allocation of methodological authority.

#### Judgment

Reversed in all three consolidated cases, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

The Second Circuit’s judgment is reversed, and the district court’s judgment sustaining the Secretary’s no-adjustment decision remains in force. No statutory or Administrative Procedure Act issue outside the controlled constitutional question is decided.

Justice Stone delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Constitutional review of census methodology

Holding: The Secretary’s decision not to use the post-enumeration survey adjustment was within the constitutional discretion governing the census because it remained within the enacted delegation and bore a reasonable relationship to accomplishing the actual enumeration for apportionment.

Operative rule: A federal census methodology satisfies the Census Clause when it is authorized by Congress and bears a reasonable relationship to accomplishing an actual enumeration in light of the Constitution’s apportionment purpose. A differential effect on racial or socioeconomic groups does not by itself trigger heightened scrutiny or constitutionally compel a competing statistical method; intentional discrimination remains independently reviewable.

Reasoning: The Constitution assigns Congress broad authority to direct the manner of the census, and Congress delegated substantial methodological judgment to the Secretary. The constitutional command does not identify numerical accuracy as the sole permissible measure of a census method. The Secretary could consider distributive accuracy among States, technical uncertainty, historical practice, and the risk that an adjustment improving some population estimates might distort apportionment. Intrastate one-person, one-vote doctrine does not supply a judicial formula for choosing among those nationwide census methodologies.

Application: The Secretary reviewed extensive technical work, acknowledged the differential undercount, considered competing recommendations, and reasonably concluded that the PES adjustment had not been shown to improve the interstate distribution used for apportionment. No preserved finding establishes discriminatory purpose.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not immunize intentional discrimination, a method outside delegated authority, refusal to consider material evidence, or a properly presented statutory or Administrative Procedure Act violation. It does not hold that statistical adjustment is constitutionally forbidden in a later census supported by a materially different record.

Legal basis: Article I, § 2; Fourteenth Amendment, § 2; and 13 U.S.C. § 141(a).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse in all consolidated cases.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone states that the Constitution fixes the duty to conduct an actual enumeration but leaves substantial methodological judgment to Congress and the Secretary. The acknowledged differential undercount, without discriminatory purpose, does not authorize heightened scrutiny or judicial selection of the preferred statistical technique.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for constitutional review of federal census methodology. *Department of Commerce v. Montana* and *Franklin v. Massachusetts* are applied and clarified: delegated census choices receive review for constitutional authorization and reasonable relation to the enumeration and apportionment function, not heightened scrutiny borrowed from intrastate districting.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court also reversed unanimously and sustained the Secretary’s no-adjustment decision under a reasonable-relationship standard. Chief Justice Rehnquist wrote the historical opinion. In the simulation, Stone occupies the Chief Justice’s institutional role, authors the unanimous opinion, and adopts the same principal constitutional boundary. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/1/))

Research sources: Officially reported materials for the consolidated dockets, argument and decision dates, differential-undercount record, Secretary’s stated considerations, lower-court standard, historical unanimous judgment, and opinion authorship. ([Supreme Court Case Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/1/))

Fordice for section 2 not the one in the doc: ## Section II: Entering law and Stone's position

**Approval Status:** Draft for express approval; this Section II is not controlling or frozen until approved.

### A. Law entering the case

* **Current Through:** March 20, 1996. The controlling baseline includes *Brown v. Board of Education*, *Swann v. Charlotte-Mecklenburg Board of Education*, *Milliken v. Bradley*, *Freeman v. Pitts*, *United States v. Fordice*, 505 U.S. 717 (1992), controlling simulated *Missouri v. Jenkins*, and, where a remedial provision itself allocates an individualized governmental benefit by race, *City of Richmond v. J.A. Croson Co.* and controlling simulated *Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña*.
* **Governing Sequence:** Mississippi's constitutional duty begins with the prohibition on state-maintained racial segregation recognized in *Brown*. *Swann* supplies broad equitable power to dismantle an established de jure system. *Milliken* confines the scope of relief to the nature and extent of the proved constitutional violation. *Freeman* permits incremental withdrawal of judicial supervision as separable vestiges are eliminated. *United States v. Fordice* supplies the case-specific higher-education rule: Mississippi must eliminate or reform a policy traceable to the former de jure university system when that policy continues to foster segregation and can practicably be eliminated consistent with sound educational practice. Simulated *Jenkins* further requires every compulsory remedial component to remain tied to an identified surviving vestige and prohibits racial balance, demographic attraction, institutional prestige, or general educational improvement from becoming independent constitutional objectives. 
* **Role of Adarand:** *Adarand* does not govern the ordinary structural desegregation components merely because the underlying violation involved race. Race-neutral admissions reform, mission correction, program changes, institutional studies, funding, and monitoring remain governed by the native desegregation-remedy doctrine. *Adarand*, together with *Croson*, becomes independently applicable when the decree itself makes race determinative of an individual's scholarship or comparable discrete governmental benefit. Controlling simulated *Adarand* requires strict scrutiny for such race-determinative governmental benefits. 
* **Earlier Same-Term Decisions:** No completed OT1995 decision before March 20, 1996 materially changes the controlling desegregation-remedy rules.
* **Unresolved Same-Term Dependencies:** None identified.
* **General Remedial Boundary:** Enrollment patterns and racial identifiability may be evidence that a traceable policy continues to operate segregatively. They are not themselves the constitutional violation, and increasing enrollment of a particular race is not an independent remedial objective. The constitutional object is elimination of the traceable policy and its continuing segregative effect.
* **General Supervisory Boundary:** Federal supervision may continue only over surviving constitutional violations and valid remedies. It must contract as separable components are brought into compliance. Stone's own remedial jurisprudence likewise requires structural obligations to remain tied to the violation and to end when the legally relevant effects have been cured. 

#### Issue 1: Undergraduate admissions and related admissions policies

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** *United States v. Fordice* directly governs Mississippi's admissions structure. Simulated *Jenkins* requires the remedy imposed for that vestige to remain specifically fitted to eliminating it rather than serving a freestanding racial-composition objective.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** **Violation-remedy nexus and interdistrict attraction** and **Achievement benchmarks and incremental withdrawal of control.** 
* **Other Governing Law:** Equal Protection Clause; *Brown*; *Swann*; *Milliken*; *Freeman*; *Green v. County School Board*; *Bazemore v. Friday*; *United States v. Fordice*.
* **Record Application:** The former differential undergraduate admissions standards were traceable to Mississippi's de jure system and continued to influence student choice. The district court separately found that graduate-admissions practices were not proved to be continuing de jure vestiges and that the challenged preexisting ACT-based scholarship practices did not supply the same *Fordice* predicate.
* **Open Point:** None preventing disposition of the undergraduate admissions provisions. Separate constitutional or statutory challenges to practices not remediable under *Fordice* remain outside this judgment.

#### Issue 2: Missions, academic programs, duplication, institutional structure, and related studies

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** *Fordice* identifies mission assignments, unnecessary program duplication, and continued institutional structure as subjects of the State's dismantling duty when they remain traceable, continue to foster segregation, and can practicably be reformed. *Milliken* and simulated *Jenkins* require each ordered programmatic or structural measure to correspond to that identified violation.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** **Violation-remedy nexus and interdistrict attraction** and **Achievement benchmarks and incremental withdrawal of control.** 
* **Other Governing Law:** *Brown*; *Swann*; *Milliken*; *Freeman*; *Fordice*.
* **Record Application:** The district court found that historically restricted HBI missions and certain forms of proximate program duplication remained traceable to the dual system. It also found educational uncertainty concerning actual DSU/MVSU consolidation and insufficient desegregative necessity for the proposed MUW/MSU merger.
* **Critical Limitation:** A program addition or institutional study may be ordered because it corrects a traceably restricted mission or unnecessary duplication. It may not be ordered merely because it might attract more white students, improve institutional prestige, or produce a preferred racial composition.
* **Open Point:** A DSU/MVSU study may proceed to determine whether an educationally sound and practicable remedy exists. The present judgment does not authorize consolidation.

#### Issue 3: Funding, facilities, land-grant measures, endowments, and race-specific scholarships

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** *Milliken*, *Fordice*, and simulated *Jenkins* permit expenditures necessary to eliminate an identified continuing vestige but do not authorize compensatory institutional equalization for its own sake. Historical underinvestment is relevant to traceability and present effects but does not itself authorize any particular present expenditure.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** **Violation-remedy nexus and interdistrict attraction** and **Achievement benchmarks and incremental withdrawal of control.** 
* **Independent Equality Rule:** An express state racial classification determining eligibility for an individual scholarship receives strict scrutiny under *Croson*. Controlling simulated *Adarand* confirms that when race determines or effectively determines receipt of a particular governmental contract, payment, or comparable discrete benefit, government must establish a compelling interest and narrow tailoring, including necessity, workable race-neutral alternatives, duration, and fit to the identified injury. 
* **Record Application:** The district court found no present traceable defect in the general funding formula or ordinary capital-facilities allocation. It found the current land-grant arrangement educationally sound and did not establish that its current operation prevented cross-racial access, while separately finding mission-related underdevelopment at JSU and ASU.
* **Open Point:** The present record does not adequately tie the general JSU capital package, unrestricted JSU or ASU endowment funding, or the ASU Small Farm Development Center matching-fund mandate to sufficiently identified present vestiges. Those provisions therefore require either removal or more specific findings, depending on the component.
* **Race-Specific Scholarship Point:** Mississippi's own de jure segregation provides a substantial remedial predicate capable of supporting a compelling-interest argument. But the white-only scholarship provisions cannot survive merely because they are labeled desegregative. The present findings do not establish narrow tailoring sufficiently to sustain them without further proceedings.

#### Issue 4: Monitoring, studies, general injunction, and continuing federal supervision

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** *Freeman* and simulated *Jenkins* require incremental withdrawal of federal control from separable components once the attributable vestige has been eliminated to the extent practicable. 
* **Other Governing Law:** *Brown*; *Swann*; *Milliken*; *Green*; *Fordice*; ordinary federal equitable-remedy principles.
* **Record Application:** The Monitoring Committee, reporting requirements, studies, and retained jurisdiction may operate only in connection with surviving constitutional obligations. The district court expressly found no *Fordice* predicate for graduate admissions and did not establish the necessary university-system traceability for the community-college system.
* **General-Injunction Problem:** An injunction against maintaining identified remnants and vestiges of the former de jure system is valid. A broader prohibition against "any practice" having the effect of impeding desegregation cannot stand without limitation because *Fordice* does not convert every racially consequential policy into a constitutional violation. A policy outside the continuing de jure vestige must satisfy whatever independent constitutional or statutory rule governs it.
* **Open Point:** No fixed date for termination is constitutionally required. The relevant rule is component-specific relinquishment of control as the constitutional work is completed.

---

### B. Stone's position

* **Stone's Controlled Judgment or Disposition:** **Affirm in part, vacate in part, and remand.**

More specifically:

1. **Affirm** the uniform undergraduate admissions remedy.
2. **Affirm** the identified JSU program additions as remedies for the traceably restricted mission.
3. **Affirm as narrowed** the JSU institutional and program-duplication study. The study may investigate lawful correction of identified mission and duplication vestiges; producing a specified amount of white enrollment may not itself be its constitutional objective.
4. **Vacate and remand** the community-college articulation requirement insofar as it is compelled as a *Fordice* remedy because the record did not establish the necessary traceability.
5. **Affirm as narrowed** the Universities Center component where it is tied to correction of JSU's mission and programmatic vestiges.
6. **Vacate and remand** the general JSU capital-enhancement package for component-specific violation-remedy findings.
7. **Vacate and remand** the non-race-specific portions of the JSU and ASU endowments for the same reason.
8. **Vacate and remand under strict scrutiny** the white-applicant scholarship provisions.
9. **Vacate and remand** the ASU Small Farm Development Center matching-fund mandate because the existing findings do not adequately connect that particular expenditure to a continuing vestige rather than to other-race attraction or general institutional enhancement.
10. **Affirm as narrowed** the ASU MBA program and funding reasonably necessary to implement that valid mission-related programmatic remedy.
11. **Vacate** the facility-maintenance study because the district court found no traceable present defect in ordinary facilities allocation.
12. **Affirm** the DSU/MVSU consolidation study as a study only; no consolidation is authorized.
13. **Vacate** the graduate-catalog monitoring requirement.
14. **Vacate from this decree** the systemwide community-college coordination study.
15. **Affirm as narrowed** the Monitoring Committee, administrative-funding provisions, retained jurisdiction, and general injunction only insofar as they administer surviving *Fordice* violations and lawful remedies.
16. **Vacate or narrow** the separate injunction against "any practice" impeding desegregation so that federal supervision does not extend to nontraceable conduct absent an independently established legal violation.

* **Stone's Remedy and Remand Instruction:** The district court shall revise the decree component by component. It may retain provisions affirmed outright or as narrowed. It shall remove provisions for which the existing findings establish no remediable *Fordice* predicate. It may reconsider provisions vacated for inadequate violation-remedy findings only if the existing procedural posture permits further findings and the court identifies the particular surviving vestige, explains how the proposed measure materially remedies that vestige, determines that the measure is educationally sound and practicable where *Fordice* requires that inquiry, and limits the obligation and supervision to the period constitutionally necessary. Any individual scholarship or comparable benefit expressly allocated by race must independently satisfy strict scrutiny. The Monitoring Committee and retained jurisdiction may continue only for surviving obligations, and federal control shall terminate incrementally as each separable vestige has been eliminated to the extent practicable.

### Issue 1: Undergraduate admissions and related admissions policies

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone reaches the admissions provisions because they are operative components of the decree and directly implement the earlier *Fordice* mandate.
* **Stone's Legal Position:** The former differential undergraduate admissions standards are the clearest continuing *Fordice* vestige in the record. They originated in the de jure system and continued to channel student choice according to the structure that system created. Replacing them with an educationally sound uniform statewide standard directly reforms the identified policy.
* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** The decree's uniform undergraduate admissions standards are affirmed because they directly eliminate a traceable admissions structure that continued to foster segregation. No additional *Fordice* remedy is authorized for the preexisting ACT-based scholarship practices or graduate-admissions policies on the findings presently made.
* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** This is the straightforward application of *Fordice*. Traceability, continuing effect, and a practicable educationally sound alternative are all established for the differential undergraduate standards. Simulated *Jenkins* adds no obstacle because the remedy directly removes the vestige rather than using admissions reform as a means of pursuing racial balance independently.
* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone does not constitutionalize open admissions, prohibit educationally sound standardized testing, prescribe a racial enrollment outcome, or decide an independent equal-protection or statutory challenge to a materially different admissions or scholarship policy.

### Issue 2: Missions, academic programs, duplication, institutional structure, and related studies

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone reaches each separable mission, program, duplication, institutional-structure, and study provision because each independently exercises federal remedial authority.
* **Stone's Legal Position:** Mississippi's de jure system deliberately limited the missions and programmatic development of the HBIs. Where that policy remains operative and continues to channel student choice, specific educationally sound program changes may repair the vestige. Proximate duplication created or maintained by the dual system may similarly be corrected. But neither *Fordice* nor *Jenkins* authorizes restructuring merely to create a preferred racial composition.
* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** The identified JSU program additions and the ASU MBA program are affirmed as mission-related remedies. The JSU institutional and program-duplication study is affirmed only insofar as it examines remedies for identified mission and duplication vestiges. The DSU/MVSU study is affirmed solely to determine whether a practicable, educationally sound correction exists; this judgment does not authorize consolidation. The district court's refusal to compel the MUW/MSU merger and wholesale elimination or transfer of nonproximate duplicative programs is affirmed.
* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** The validity of the JSU and ASU measures depends on correction of the historically restricted mission and resulting program structure, not on their predicted ability to attract white students. Enrollment effects remain relevant evidence of whether the old structure continues to influence student choice. They do not become the remedy's constitutional objective. The DSU/MVSU duplication findings justify continued inquiry because traceability and present segregative operation have record support, but the necessary finding that consolidation itself is practicable and educationally sound has not yet been made.
* **Community-College Articulation:** The compelled articulation requirement is vacated and remanded because the district court did not establish that the relevant community-college policy was a vestige of the university system's de jure segregation. Desirable coordination and potential improvement in racial diversity do not substitute for that missing predicate.
* **Universities Center:** This provision may remain only insofar as the district court ties it to correction of the identified JSU mission or program-access vestige rather than to demographic attraction standing alone.
* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone does not hold that every HBI must receive a unique professional school, every duplication is unconstitutional, every racially identifiable institution must disappear, or consolidation is categorically forbidden. A later record may support closure, transfer, or merger where *Fordice*'s traceability, continuing-effect, practicability, and educational-soundness requirements are actually satisfied.

### Issue 3: Funding, facilities, land-grant measures, endowments, and race-specific scholarships

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone reaches each expenditure because compulsory spending is itself an exercise of federal remedial authority and must possess its own lawful connection to the constitutional violation.
* **Stone's Legal Position:** The historical underfunding of Mississippi's HBIs is relevant and severe, but history alone does not authorize present equalization. *Milliken*, *Fordice*, and simulated *Jenkins* require the court to identify what surviving policy or consequence the expenditure corrects. Program-specific funding necessary to make an otherwise valid mission remedy effective may therefore be compelled. General improvement, beautification, prestige, image, or racial attractiveness does not independently authorize federal expenditure.
* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** The refusal to rewrite Mississippi's general funding formula, require wholesale land-grant restructuring, or compel general facilities equalization is affirmed. Necessary funding for the valid ASU MBA program is affirmed. The general JSU capital package, non-race-specific portions of the JSU and ASU endowments, and ASU Small Farm Development Center matching-fund mandate are vacated and remanded for the more specific findings described above. The facility-maintenance study is vacated.
* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** The district court found that ordinary capital and facility allocation itself was not a continuing traceable policy and found the existing land-grant arrangement educationally sound. Those findings prevent the court from treating capital improvement or land-grant equalization as constitutionally required merely because the HBIs suffered profound historic deprivation. A particular expenditure can nonetheless survive if the court connects it to a separately established mission, program, or other continuing vestige and demonstrates why the expenditure is needed to make that corrective measure effective.
* **White-Applicant Scholarships:** The scholarship provisions are separately vacated and remanded under strict scrutiny. Mississippi has a powerful remedial interest in curing its own established de jure segregation, but these provisions make race expressly determinative of individual eligibility. *Croson* and controlling simulated *Adarand* therefore require the government to establish compelling interest and narrow tailoring for that particular use of race. The existing decree does not adequately establish why white-only scholarships are necessary, whether workable race-neutral alternatives would be inadequate, how many scholarships are required, their amount and duration, or the point at which the classification terminates. 
* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone does not prevent Mississippi from voluntarily funding JSU or ASU beyond the federal constitutional minimum, improving facilities, expanding land-grant services, creating race-neutral scholarships or recruitment programs, establishing endowments, or otherwise pursuing educational excellence. The judgment determines only what the federal court may compel as a constitutional remedy.

### Issue 4: Monitoring, studies, general injunction, and continuing federal supervision

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone reaches monitoring and retained jurisdiction because they impose continuing judicial control and therefore require continuing remedial authority.
* **Stone's Legal Position:** Monitoring is permissible as an administrative means of enforcing a valid remedy. It is not an independent source of federal power. *Freeman* and simulated *Jenkins* require supervision to track the particular surviving vestige and end when that vestige has been eliminated to the extent practicable.
* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** The Monitoring Committee, its necessary administrative funding, and retained jurisdiction are affirmed only as narrowed to surviving remedial obligations. The graduate-catalog monitoring requirement and systemwide community-college coordination study are vacated because the district court identified no corresponding *Fordice* vestige. The JSU mission/duplication study and DSU/MVSU study may continue within the limits stated above.
* **General Injunction:** The prohibition against Mississippi maintaining identified remnants or vestiges of its former de jure system is affirmed. The broader prohibition against "any practice" that impedes desegregation is vacated or narrowed. *Fordice* does not authorize continuing federal control over every policy producing a racially consequential effect. A nontraceable policy must be challenged under whatever independent constitutional or statutory source governs it.
* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** Structural jurisdiction is justified by unfinished constitutional work, not by the court's continuing interest in the quality or racial composition of the university system. Once a component contains no surviving traceable vestige or the vestige has been eliminated to the extent practicable, federal control over that component ends.
* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone imposes no fixed sunset date and does not require simultaneous termination of the decree. He requires component-specific termination under *Freeman* and *Jenkins* as the State completes the constitutional work.

### Stone's Proposed Holding

Mississippi remains constitutionally obligated to eliminate policies traceable to its former de jure public-university system that continue to foster segregation when those policies can practicably be eliminated or reformed consistent with sound educational practice. Federal remedial authority proceeds component by component. Each compulsory institutional change, program, expenditure, study, or supervisory requirement must remain reasonably directed to eliminating an identified surviving vestige, and control over a separable component must terminate once that vestige has been eliminated to the extent practicable. Racial enrollment patterns may demonstrate that a traceable policy continues to influence student choice, but racial balance, attraction of students of a particular race, institutional prestige, and general educational improvement are not independent constitutional ends. Race-neutral structural remediation remains governed by the desegregation-remedy cases. A provision that makes race determinative of an individual's scholarship or comparable governmental benefit is independently subject to strict scrutiny.

**Doctrinal Effect:** Application and reconciliation of existing law. No new Stone-specific Fordice test is created. The principal work is performed by *United States v. Fordice*, *Milliken*, *Freeman*, simulated *Jenkins*, and, solely for race-determinative individual benefits, *Croson* and simulated *Adarand*.

## Decisions and Dispositions

### *National Association for the Advancement of Colored People v. Fordice*, 879 F. Supp. 1419

Decided March 25, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

#### Entering Posture and Questions

From: Following the Supreme Court’s earlier decision in *United States v. Fordice*, the Northern District of Mississippi conducted a lengthy remedial proceeding and entered a decree addressing undergraduate admissions, university missions, programs, duplication, institutional structure, funding, scholarships, studies, and continuing supervision. The district court identified several surviving vestiges of Mississippi’s former de jure university system but rejected or declined other proposed measures as unsupported or educationally unsound. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/district-courts/FSupp/879/1419/2264344/))

Questions presented: Whether each component of the decree remains tied to a policy traceable to the former dual system that continues to foster segregation and can practicably be reformed consistent with sound educational practice; whether race-determinative scholarships require independent constitutional review; and how continuing supervision must contract as separable constitutional obligations are completed.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *United States v. Fordice* supplies the higher-education dismantling duty. Controlling simulated *Missouri v. Jenkins* requires each compulsory remedy to remain connected to an identified surviving vestige and forbids racial balance, demographic attraction, institutional prestige, or general educational improvement from becoming independent constitutional objectives. Controlling simulated *Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña* requires strict scrutiny when race determines receipt of an individual governmental benefit.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Uniform undergraduate admissions standards | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The uniform statewide standards remain in force. |
| Additional *Fordice* relief for the preexisting ACT scholarship rules and graduate admissions | District court affirmed, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | No additional desegregation remedy may be imposed on the findings presently made. Independent legal challenges remain open. |
| Identified JSU program additions and ASU MBA program, including necessary program funding | Affirmed, 8-1 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia | The measures remain as remedies for traceably restricted institutional missions. |
| JSU institutional and program-duplication study and Universities Center | Affirmed as narrowed, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Each measure may address identified mission, duplication, or program-access vestiges; attracting a specified number of white students is not an independent remedial objective. |
| Community-college articulation requirement and systemwide community-college coordination study | Vacated, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | The articulation provision is remanded for any further treatment supported by a separately established legal basis. The systemwide study is removed from this decree. |
| General JSU capital package, non-race-specific portions of the JSU and ASU endowments, and ASU Small Farm Development Center matching-fund mandate | Vacated and remanded, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Each expenditure requires component-specific findings identifying the present vestige, remedial mechanism, necessity, practicability, and lawful duration. |
| White-applicant scholarship provisions | Vacated and remanded, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Any reimposed race-determinative scholarship must satisfy strict scrutiny. |
| Facility-maintenance study and graduate-catalog monitoring | Vacated, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | The district court found no corresponding current traceable defect. |
| DSU/MVSU consolidation study | Affirmed as a study only, 8-1 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia | The study may examine educationally sound, practicable correction of identified duplication. Consolidation is not authorized. |
| Refusal to compel a general funding-formula rewrite, wholesale land-grant restructuring, a MUW/MSU merger, or systemwide elimination of nonproximate duplication | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | No additional compulsory remedy is entered. |
| Monitoring Committee, administrative funding, retained jurisdiction, and general injunction | Affirmed as narrowed, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Oversight extends only to surviving *Fordice* violations and lawful remedies. Control must end incrementally as each separable obligation is completed. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas in full; Scalia as to the remedial-nexus limits, contested vacaturs, scholarship scrutiny, and refusal of broader restructuring; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer as to the record, uniform admissions, targeted JSU and ASU program remedies, DSU/MVSU study, and refusal of broader unproved restructuring | Supports the component-specific judgment | Joins vary by the exact propositions stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | None | Supports the narrowing and vacatur components but opposes several programmatic affirmances | Would confine compulsory relief principally to removal of discriminatory admissions barriers |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports the admissions and targeted-program affirmances but opposes the contested narrowing and vacaturs | Would sustain more of the district court’s decree and would not subject the scholarships to a separate strict-scrutiny track |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Undergraduate admissions and related policies

Holding: Mississippi’s uniform undergraduate admissions standards are affirmed because they directly replace a differential policy traceable to the de jure system that continued to influence student choice. On the present findings, the preexisting ACT-based scholarship rules and graduate-admissions practices do not support additional relief under *Fordice*.

Reasoning: Differential admissions were an operative remnant identified in the earlier Supreme Court mandate. Replacing them with educationally sound uniform standards removes the policy itself rather than pursuing a preferred enrollment result. The separate scholarship and graduate-admissions findings do not establish the same combination of traceability and continuing segregative operation.

Limits and questions left open: The judgment neither constitutionalizes open admissions nor forecloses an independent equal-protection or statutory challenge to a materially different scholarship or graduate-admissions practice.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support the uniform-admissions holding. Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas support the refusal to add further *Fordice* relief on the present findings.

##### Missions, programs, duplication, and institutional structure

Holding: The identified JSU program additions and ASU MBA program are valid mission-correction remedies. The JSU study, Universities Center, and DSU/MVSU study may continue only to identify or implement practicable, educationally sound correction of specified mission, duplication, or program-access vestiges.

Reasoning: Mississippi’s former system deliberately restricted the missions and professional development of its historically Black institutions. A specific program may remedy that surviving institutional structure when the record identifies the traceable restriction and explains how the program corrects it. Predicted enrollment change may be evidence that the old structure still influences choice, but attracting students of a particular race is not itself the constitutional objective.

Application: The JSU additions and ASU MBA possess the required mission connection. The JSU and DSU/MVSU studies may investigate lawful structural correction, but this judgment authorizes no consolidation. The record does not justify compelling the MUW/MSU merger or wholesale elimination of nonproximate duplication.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not require every historically Black institution to receive a unique professional school, treat every duplication as unconstitutional, or prohibit a later closure, transfer, or merger supported by the findings *Fordice* requires.

Authority of the proposition: Eight Justices support the targeted program remedies and DSU/MVSU study. The five-Justice Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas coalition controls the limitations placed on the JSU study and Universities Center.

##### Funding, facilities, endowments, and scholarships

Holding: A federal desegregation court may compel expenditures necessary to eliminate an identified surviving vestige, but it may not order general institutional equalization, prestige enhancement, or demographic attraction without the required violation-remedy connection. The general JSU capital package, non-race-specific endowment provisions, and Small Farm Development Center matching mandate must be reconsidered component by component. The facility-maintenance study cannot remain.

Holding: The white-applicant scholarship provisions make race determinative of an individual governmental benefit and therefore receive strict scrutiny independently of the structural desegregation analysis.

Operative rule: A race-determinative scholarship must serve a compelling remedial interest and be narrowly tailored in necessity, amount, duration, termination, and treatment of workable race-neutral alternatives. Mississippi’s responsibility for its own de jure system supplies a substantial remedial predicate, but the label “desegregative” does not itself establish narrow tailoring.

Reasoning: The district court found no present traceable defect in the ordinary funding formula, general capital allocation, or current land-grant structure. Historical underinvestment remains relevant to identifying the origin and effects of a particular mission or program vestige, but it does not authorize unrestricted compensatory equalization. The scholarship findings likewise do not establish why white-only eligibility is necessary, how many awards are required, why race-neutral recruitment or need-based alternatives are inadequate, or when the preference must end.

Limits and questions left open: Mississippi may voluntarily improve institutions, create endowments, expand land-grant services, or use race-neutral recruitment and scholarships beyond the federal constitutional minimum. The decision concerns what a federal decree may compel.

Authority of the proposition: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas support both holdings.

##### Monitoring, injunctions, and withdrawal of control

Holding: The Monitoring Committee, retained jurisdiction, administrative funding, and general injunction may operate only in relation to surviving traceable vestiges and valid remedial measures. Supervision must terminate incrementally when the constitutional work in a separable component is complete.

Reasoning: Monitoring administers lawful relief; it does not create remedial authority. Graduate-catalog review and systemwide community-college supervision cannot continue after the district court found no corresponding university-system vestige. An injunction against maintaining identified remnants of the former de jure system is proper. A prohibition against any practice that impedes desegregation is overbroad unless confined to traceable conduct or supported by an independently established legal violation.

Limits and questions left open: No fixed termination date is required. Different components may remain under supervision for different periods.

Authority of the proposition: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas support the controlling supervisory limits.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm in part, vacate in part, and remand as stated in the component table.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone applies *Fordice* component by component, reconciles its higher-education duty with *Jenkins*’s violation-remedy and incremental-withdrawal requirements, and applies strict scrutiny only to provisions making race determinative of an individual scholarship.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia would sustain the uniform admissions remedy but would vacate the JSU program additions, ASU MBA remedy, Universities Center, and DSU/MVSU study. He regards most measures beyond removal of discriminatory access barriers as exceeding the constitutional duty applicable to voluntary higher education.

Souter, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer, would give greater weight to the district court’s findings concerning historically restricted missions, institutional underdevelopment, community-college linkages, and the practical operation of the decree. The dissenting coalition would sustain the broader funding, study, and monitoring provisions and would review the scholarships as part of the desegregation remedy rather than through a separate strict-scrutiny inquiry.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*United States v. Fordice* is applied without creation of a new Stone-specific test. Controlling simulated *Missouri v. Jenkins* clarifies the permissible object and duration of each higher-education remedy. Controlling simulated *Adarand* applies independently when a decree makes race determinative of an individual scholarship, while race-neutral admissions, mission, program, study, funding, and monitoring measures remain governed by the native desegregation cases.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court did not historically review the March 1995 decree. The Fifth Circuit later affirmed substantial portions of the district court’s work while requiring additional or revised treatment of several components. The simulation reaches the remedial decree directly, narrows multiple expenditure and supervisory provisions, and subjects the white-only scholarships to independent strict scrutiny. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/district-courts/FSupp/879/1419/2264344/))

Research sources: The 1995 district-court findings and decree, the later Fifth Circuit review, and the earlier Supreme Court mandate in *United States v. Fordice*. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/district-courts/FSupp/879/1419/2264344/))

### *Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida*, 517 U.S. 44

Argued October 11, 1995 | Decided March 27, 1996

From: IGRA requires States and tribes to negotiate gaming compacts in good faith and expressly authorizes a tribe to sue a State in federal court for failure to perform that duty. Florida invoked sovereign immunity, and the Eleventh Circuit dismissed both the statutory action against the State and the alternative officer claim. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/44/))

Questions presented: Whether Congress unmistakably abrogated state immunity, whether the Indian Commerce Clause authorized that abrogation under the still-controlling rule of *Pennsylvania v. Union Gas Co.*, and whether the alternative officer ruling must be reached.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Union Gas* remains in force. No simulated decision has overruled its conclusion that Congress may abrogate state immunity through an Article I commerce power when it speaks unmistakably.

#### Judgment

Reversed in part, vacated in part, and remanded, 5-4.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

The sovereign-immunity dismissal of the statutory action against Florida is reversed. The separate ruling concerning an officer action is vacated as unnecessary. The district court shall determine whether Florida failed to negotiate in good faith and, if so, administer only IGRA’s enacted negotiation, mediation, and Secretarial sequence.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal, vacatur, and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Souter | Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports the judgment | States a broader historical objection to constitutionalized state sovereign immunity |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm | Would overrule *Union Gas* and hold that Article I cannot authorize private federal suits against nonconsenting States |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Article I abrogation under IGRA

Holding: IGRA unmistakably authorizes a tribe to sue a State for failure to negotiate in good faith. Under controlling *Union Gas*, Congress may provide that prospective federal action as an incident of its Indian Commerce authority.

Operative rule: When Congress unmistakably identifies the State as defendant, acts within an enumerated commerce power, and expressly supplies the cause of action and prospective remedy, *Union Gas* permits abrogation of state sovereign immunity. Article I does not itself create a cause of action, and statutory clarity remains indispensable.

Reasoning: IGRA specifies the regulated duty, the State defendant, the federal forum, and a staged prospective remedy. The rationale supporting abrogation under the Interstate Commerce Clause supplies no principled distinction for the Indian Commerce Clause, which transfers at least as much relevant regulatory authority to the Federal Government. The Court declines Florida’s request to overrule controlling precedent in a case where Congress legislated in direct reliance on an express federal enforcement route.

Limits and questions left open: The holding does not authorize damages, an implied cause of action, a judicially drafted compact, or abrogation outside an enumerated congressional power.

Legal basis: The Indian Commerce Clause, Article III, the Eleventh Amendment, and 25 U.S.C. § 2710(d).

##### Alternative officer action

Holding: Because Florida is a proper defendant under IGRA, the Court does not decide whether *Ex parte Young* independently permits an officer suit or whether IGRA’s remedial detail would displace one.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse the sovereign-immunity dismissal, vacate the officer-action ruling, and remand.

Public role: Joined Stevens’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone publicly joins the application of *Union Gas* to IGRA’s express prospective action and announces no position on the unnecessary officer question.

#### Material Separate Writings

Souter, joined by Ginsburg and Breyer, would rest the judgment on a broader historical understanding of Article III and state sovereign immunity. That rationale is noncontrolling.

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, would overrule *Union Gas*, hold that Article I cannot expand federal jurisdiction over an unconsenting State, and affirm both parts of the Eleventh Circuit judgment.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Union Gas* is followed and clarified. Its Article I commerce-power rule applies to the Indian Commerce Clause when Congress unmistakably authorizes a statutory action against a State. The decision does not address abrogation under every Article I power.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court affirmed 5-4, overruled *Union Gas*, and held that IGRA’s remedial structure also foreclosed an *Ex parte Young* action. The simulation preserves *Union Gas* and reverses 5-4, with Stone joining the four Justices who historically dissented from the immunity holding. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/44/))

Research sources: The official report for IGRA’s remedial structure, lower-court judgment, historical vote, opinion topology, and treatment of *Union Gas*. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/44/))

---

### *Morse v. Republican Party of Virginia*, 517 U.S. 186

Argued October 2, 1995 | Decided March 27, 1996

From: Virginia authorized its major parties to choose nominees by primary, convention, or another approved method and placed the resulting nominees automatically on the general-election ballot. The Republican Party required convention delegates to pay a $35 or $45 registration fee. Two otherwise qualified voters declined to pay, while Morse paid using funds advanced by supporters. A three-judge district court dismissed their Voting Rights Act claims. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/186/))

Questions presented: Whether the fee was a change affecting voting subject to § 5 preclearance, whether affected voters may enforce § 5’s no-enforcement command, and whether the Court must decide a separate private action under § 10.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

The fee may not be imposed unless and until it receives § 5 preclearance. The district court shall enter appropriate prospective relief and address any remaining request for repayment through a separately authorized remedial source.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stevens | Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Would also recognize private enforcement under § 10, an issue the Court reserves |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Scalia, Thomas | Would affirm | Would treat the convention fee as an internal party rule outside § 5 and emphasizes associational concerns |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Section 5 coverage

Holding: A major political party must preclear a new delegate-registration fee when state law authorizes the party convention to select a nominee entitled to automatic general-election ballot placement and the fee changes who may participate in that official nomination process.

Operative rule: Section 5 reaches a party rule when the party acts under authority granted by a covered jurisdiction to perform a legally operative nomination function and the rule alters participation in that function. Coverage does not extend to internal membership, platform, fundraising, or governance decisions lacking that electoral operation.

Reasoning: The convention did more than endorse a candidate. Virginia allowed it to substitute for a primary and gave the certified nominee automatic legal effect on the general-election ballot. Conditioning delegate participation on payment therefore altered a prerequisite to action necessary to make votes effective. Section 5 requires preclearance before enforcement; proof of discriminatory purpose or effect belongs to the preclearance process.

Legal basis: Voting Rights Act §§ 5 and 14(c)(1).

##### Private enforcement and statutory sequencing

Holding: Voters directly excluded or charged under an unprecleared covered change may seek an injunction enforcing § 5’s prohibition on implementation before preclearance.

Reasoning: *Allen v. State Board of Elections* supplies the private enforcement route necessary to preserve the statutory sequence. The district court may prevent use of the fee but may not itself decide whether the change has a discriminatory purpose or effect.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide whether § 10 creates an independent private cause of action or authorizes repayment of a fee already collected.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Breyer’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone publicly joins the state-authorized nomination and private § 5 enforcement grounds and reserves § 10.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens, joined by Ginsburg, would recognize a private § 10 action as well as the § 5 route. That proposition is noncontrolling.

Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas, would hold that automatic ballot access does not transform the Party’s convention rules into changes enacted or administered by the State. The dissent also warns against reading § 5 to create an unnecessary federal preclearance system for internal associational choices.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule for § 5 coverage of a state-authorized party nominating convention. *Allen* is applied without expanding the district court’s role into preclearance adjudication.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court also reversed, but the 5-4 judgment rested on fragmented opinions. Stevens and Ginsburg supplied one rationale, while Breyer, O’Connor, and Souter supplied the narrower additional votes for § 5 coverage. The simulation produces a unified six-Justice Opinion of the Court on § 5 and reserves § 10. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/186/))

Research sources: The official report for the convention’s legal operation, fee, standing, historical judgment, and fragmented opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/186/))

### *Rutledge v. United States*, 517 U.S. 292

Argued November 27, 1995 | Decided March 27, 1996

From: The jury convicted Rutledge under both 21 U.S.C. § 846 and § 848, using the same agreement to establish the conspiracy and the continuing-criminal-enterprise offense’s “in concert” element. The district court entered two convictions, concurrent life sentences, and separate $50 assessments. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/292/))

Question presented: Whether both convictions and punishments may stand.

#### Judgment

Reversed in part and remanded, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The district court shall vacate the § 846 conviction, its concurrent sentence, and its separate special assessment. The § 848 conviction and sentence remain undisturbed.

Justice Stevens delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Lesser included offense and multiple punishment

Holding: When the same agreement establishes a § 846 conspiracy and the “in concert” element of a § 848 continuing criminal enterprise, the conspiracy is a lesser included offense and a separate conviction, sentence, and assessment may not stand.

Reasoning: The CCE offense requires the agreement incorporated in the conspiracy count plus additional facts concerning a continuing series, five or more persons, supervision, and substantial income. The conspiracy count requires no fact absent from the CCE count as charged and proved here. Congress supplied no clear authorization for two convictions based on that same agreement.

Application: Concurrent prison terms do not cure the error. The second assessment is immediate additional punishment, and the additional conviction carries possible collateral consequences.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not govern separately charged conspiracies based on different agreements or authorize vacation of the greater CCE conviction.

Legal basis: The Double Jeopardy Clause, 21 U.S.C. §§ 846 and 848, and 18 U.S.C. § 3013.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse in part and remand to vacate the § 846 judgment.

Public role: Joined Stevens’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The ordinary *Blockburger* presumption and *Ball v. United States* remedy are applied. The current simulated rule recognizing expressly authorized cumulative punishment under § 924(c) remains unchanged because § 848 contains no comparable additional-and-consecutive command.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment, Stevens authorship, lesser-included-offense holding, and requirement to vacate the second conviction match the historical decision. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/292/))

Research sources: The official report for the charges, jury findings, sentences, assessments, statutory analysis, judgment, and authorship. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/292/))

### *O’Connor v. Consolidated Coin Caterers Corp.*, 517 U.S. 308

Argued February 27, 1996 | Decided April 1, 1996

From: Consolidated Coin discharged O’Connor at age fifty-six and replaced him with a forty-year-old employee. The Fourth Circuit affirmed summary judgment because the replacement was not outside the ADEA’s age-forty-and-over protected group. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/308/))

Question presented: Whether replacement by a person under forty is a required element of an ADEA prima facie case.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The case shall be reconsidered without an outside-the-protected-class requirement. No finding of discrimination or entitlement to relief is entered.

Justice Scalia delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Substantially younger replacement

Holding: An ADEA plaintiff need not show replacement by a person under forty. Replacement by a substantially younger person, or other circumstances reasonably supporting an inference that age caused the challenged action, may satisfy the prima facie stage.

Operative rule: The age-forty threshold identifies who may invoke the statute; it does not define whom an employer may prefer without regard to age. No fixed minimum age gap is adopted.

Reasoning: The ADEA prohibits discrimination because of age, not discrimination against a protected class as an undifferentiated group. A rule focused on whether the replacement crossed the age-forty line would disregard a large age disparity while giving special significance to a negligible difference at the statutory boundary.

Application: O’Connor’s sixteen-year age difference prevents summary judgment on the ground used below. The employer remains free to offer a lawful explanation, contest pretext, and dispute ultimate causation.

Legal basis: 29 U.S.C. §§ 623(a)(1) and 631(a).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Scalia’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment, Scalia authorship, and substantially-younger formulation match the historical decision. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/308/))

Research sources: The official report for the employment record, lower-court rule, statutory holding, argument date, and judgment. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/308/))

### *Lonchar v. Thomas*, 517 U.S. 314

Argued December 4, 1995 | Decided April 1, 1996

From: Lonchar filed his first federal habeas petition on the day scheduled for execution after years of state and next-friend litigation. The district court concluded that the petition could not be summarily dismissed under the governing rules and stayed execution while considering the State’s formal defenses. The Eleventh Circuit vacated the stay based on broader equitable concerns about delay. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/314/))

Questions presented: Whether a first federal petition may be dismissed for ad hoc equitable reasons outside the statutes, Habeas Corpus Rules, and settled doctrines; and whether a stay is required when execution would prevent lawful adjudication of a petition not otherwise dismissible before the execution date.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The district court’s stay shall remain or be reinstated for the period reasonably necessary to adjudicate the State’s formal defenses and, if required, the merits. Proceedings shall be conducted expeditiously.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Would preserve broader equitable consideration of manipulative filing conduct within the stay inquiry |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Rule-governed disposition of a first federal petition

Holding: A federal court may not dismiss a prisoner’s first federal habeas petition for free-floating equitable reasons outside the habeas statutes, rules, and settled procedural doctrines.

Operative rule: Delay may have the consequences assigned by Rule 9, exhaustion, procedural default, adverse evidentiary rules, or another applicable doctrine. A court must identify and apply the governing source rather than convert general disapproval of filing conduct into an independent ground of dismissal.

Reasoning: The Eleventh Circuit treated timing as dispositive without identifying the legal elements, burdens, or consequences of an applicable rule. Habeas has equitable origins, but the enacted statutes and promulgated rules organize that authority and cannot be displaced by case-specific moral judgment. The State remains entitled to every formal defense supported by the record.

Limits and questions left open: The Court decides no habeas claim, exhaustion issue, procedural default, Rule 9 defense, or abuse question not resolved below.

##### Stay preserving adjudication

Holding: When a first federal petition cannot lawfully be dismissed before execution, a court abuses its discretion by denying a stay to produce the same terminal result indirectly.

Reasoning: Execution would eliminate the pending case before the court performed the adjudication federal law assigns. The stay preserves jurisdiction; it does not predict entitlement to relief. It lasts only as long as reasonably necessary for prompt decision and may be dissolved after a lawful dispositive ruling.

Authority of the proposition: Breyer’s opinion is joined on both holdings by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, and Ginsburg, a six-Justice majority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Breyer’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone publicly joins the rule-based treatment of the first petition and the adjudication-preserving stay requirement.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, agrees that the particular stay cannot be vacated on the grounds used below. The concurrence would not adopt a categorical account of the stay inquiry that prevents consideration of deliberate manipulation or other serious misconduct under lawful equitable principles.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The current rule governing capital habeas counsel and prepetition stay authority is clarified. A stay remains neither automatic nor indefinite, but a court cannot permit execution to moot a first petition that has not been lawfully dismissed.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court vacated and remanded unanimously. Breyer’s five-Justice opinion stated the rule-based dismissal and stay principles, while Chief Justice Rehnquist concurred in the judgment with Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas. In the simulation, Stone joins Breyer, producing a six-Justice Opinion of the Court, and Scalia authors the narrower three-Justice concurrence. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/314/))

Research sources: The official report for the procedural history, first-petition posture, district-court stay, historical opinion topology, and governing rationale. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/314/))

---

### *United States v. Bishop*, 66 F.3d 569

Decided April 8, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: Bishop and Edward Stokes forcibly took a recently purchased Dodge Shadow from its occupants in East Orange, New Jersey. Bishop pleaded guilty after the district court rejected his Commerce Clause challenge. The conviction required proof that the particular vehicle had been transported in interstate commerce. The Third Circuit affirmed using substantial-effects, jurisdictional-element, and instrumentality reasoning. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/66/569/488093/))

Question presented: Whether § 2119 is constitutional as applied to Bishop after *Lopez*.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *United States v. Harris* holds that § 2119 is constitutional as applied to the direct forcible taking of a particular vehicle proved to have moved in interstate commerce. It rejects generalized reliance on insurance costs, productivity, or the aggregate consequences of local violence.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Bishop’s conviction remains in force. Questions outside the limited Commerce Clause grant are unaffected.

Justice Kennedy delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Direct protection of the covered vehicle

Holding: Section 2119 is constitutional as applied to Bishop because the offense directly and forcibly seized a covered motor vehicle and required proof that the particular vehicle had been transported in interstate commerce.

Reasoning: Bishop’s conduct matches *Harris*. The vehicle was the object of the forceful taking, not merely an item incidentally present during an ordinary local crime. The vehicle-specific interstate element therefore performs constitutionally relevant work. The Court does not adopt the Third Circuit’s characterization of carjacking as an economic transaction or its reliance on generalized aggregate consequences.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not authorize federal regulation of every crime involving property that once crossed state lines, every act occurring in or near an automobile, or every jurisdictional element unrelated to the regulated conduct.

Legal basis: The Commerce Clause and 18 U.S.C. § 2119.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the narrow *Harris* instrumentality ground and rejects the Third Circuit’s broader substantial-effects rationale.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*United States v. Harris* is applied without alteration. Its direct-instrumentality and vehicle-specific interstate-connection requirements control.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court did not historically decide Bishop’s case on the merits. The simulation affirms the Third Circuit’s judgment but replaces its broader Commerce Clause rationale with the narrower rule already established in simulated *Harris*. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/66/569/488093/))

Research sources: The published Third Circuit opinion for the offense facts, plea posture, interstate element, lower-court judgment, and historical reasoning. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/66/569/488093/))
