### *Planned Parenthood v. Miller*, 63 F.3d 1452

Decided April 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: South Dakota required personal or certified-mail notice to a parent at least forty-eight hours before an unemancipated minor’s abortion. The statute contained medical-emergency, parental-certification, and reported-abuse exceptions, but no independent procedure for determining maturity or whether proceeding without notice served the minor’s best interests. The Eighth Circuit held the provision facially invalid. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/63/1452/613941/))

Questions presented: Whether simulated *Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer* and *Casey* supply the facial standard, and whether the absence of a maturity or best-interests route creates an undue burden.

Controlling simulated law before decision: Simulated *Fargo* holds that *Casey*’s large-fraction formulation, not *Salerno*, governs facial challenges to previability abortion regulations and requires operational review of the persons for whom the provision functions as a restriction.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

The injunction against South Dakota Codified Laws § 34-23A-7 remains in force. The lower-court rulings concerning separate penalty, mandatory-information, and severability provisions remain undisturbed without becoming holdings of this Court.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Would apply a more demanding facial standard and would not require a Bellotti-type route for a notice-only provision |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Facial review and parental notification

Holding: *Casey*’s large-fraction undue-burden standard governs. South Dakota’s parental-notification provision is facially invalid because, in a large fraction of the cases in which notice functions as a restriction, it supplies no independent, confidential, and expeditious route for a minor to establish maturity or that proceeding without notice is in her best interests.

Operative rule: A State may encourage parental consultation and may require notice for a minor who cannot establish maturity or a contrary best interest. When notification is otherwise unavoidable, the State must provide an effective independent decisionmaker capable of authorizing the abortion without notice under criteria addressing maturity and best interests. The decisionmaker need not be a court.

Reasoning: The abuse-reporting exception does not address mature minors, nonabusive family circumstances in which disclosure would be harmful, or abused minors who cannot safely initiate a public report. The record showed that these circumstances were real within the affected class rather than remote hypotheticals. The statute therefore imposed a substantial obstacle in a large fraction of the cases in which its notice requirement mattered.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not invalidate parental consultation, require a particular hearing format, or decide the separate penalty, information, and severability questions outside the grant.

Legal basis: The Fourteenth Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm the invalidation of § 34-23A-7.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone publicly joins *Fargo*’s large-fraction standard and the requirement of an effective maturity or best-interests route.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would decline to extend the bypass requirements developed for parental consent to a notice-only statute and would require a facial challenger to establish invalidity across a broader range of applications.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer* is applied and clarified for parental-notification laws. The decision does not alter *Fargo*’s denominator, cumulative-operation, or medical-emergency rules.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court historically denied review, leaving the Eighth Circuit judgment in place; Justice Scalia filed a dissent from denial. The simulation reaches the merits and affirms 7-2 under the same coalition that decided simulated *Fargo*. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/95-856.ZA1.html))

Research sources: The Eighth Circuit opinion for the statute, exceptions, record, judgment, and historical proceedings; the historical denial materials for the comparator. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/95-856.ZA1.html))

### *Cooper v. Oklahoma*, 517 U.S. 348

Argued January 17, 1996 | Decided April 16, 1996

From: Oklahoma presumed criminal defendants competent and required proof of incompetence by clear and convincing evidence. Cooper displayed bizarre behavior, and the experts disagreed. The trial judge expressed uncertainty but ruled him competent because the heightened burden had not been met. Cooper was convicted of murder and sentenced to death. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/348/))

Question presented: Whether the clear-and-convincing burden creates an unconstitutional risk of trying a person who is more likely than not incompetent.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Oklahoma courts shall determine the lawful consequence of the unconstitutional burden. If state law permits and a reliable retrospective inquiry remains possible, they may determine Cooper’s competence at the original trial under a constitutionally valid burden. Otherwise, the conviction and death sentence must be set aside, and Oklahoma may retry Cooper only when he is competent.

Justice Stevens delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Burden for proving incompetence

Holding: Due process forbids requiring a criminal defendant to prove incompetence by clear and convincing evidence. A State may presume competence and place a preponderance burden on the defendant.

Operative rule: A competency procedure may not permit trial after the defendant has shown that incompetence is more probable than competence. The rule does not require the prosecution to prove competence beyond a reasonable doubt.

Reasoning: Competence is the condition that makes counsel, confrontation, testimony, waiver, and defense choice meaningful. An erroneous competency determination cannot be treated as an ordinary evidentiary error because an incompetent defendant cannot reliably participate in preventing or correcting the error. Oklahoma identified no sufficient interest justifying the increased risk created by its heightened burden.

Application: The trial judge’s expressed uncertainty demonstrates that the unconstitutional burden may have controlled the result. The Supreme Court cannot supply the missing historical competence finding.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not prohibit a rebuttable presumption of competence, decide Cooper’s actual competence, or establish standards for civil commitment.

Legal basis: The Due Process Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Stevens’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the preponderance boundary and the remand preserving any reliable retrospective procedure authorized by state law.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment, Stevens authorship, constitutional holding, and refusal to determine historical competence match the historical decision. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/348/))

Research sources: The official report for Oklahoma’s burden, the competency record, judgment, and unanimous opinion. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/348/))

### *Markman v. Westview Instruments, Inc.*, 517 U.S. 370

Argued January 8, 1996 | Decided April 23, 1996

From: Markman’s patent concerned an inventory-control system for dry-cleaning stores. A jury found infringement after hearing evidence concerning the meaning of “inventory,” but the district court independently construed the claim to require tracking articles of clothing and entered judgment for Westview. The en banc Federal Circuit affirmed the court’s authority to construe the claim. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/370/))

Question presented: Whether the Seventh Amendment requires a jury rather than a judge to construe disputed patent-claim terms, including technical terms of art.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The judgment for Westview remains in force.

Justice Souter delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Allocation of patent-claim construction

Holding: Construction of a patent claim, including the meaning of technical terms of art within the claim, is a question for the court. The jury retains its role in deciding infringement and other factual issues under the claim as construed.

Operative rule: Expert testimony may inform the court’s interpretation of the patent instrument, but the presence of technical evidence does not transfer ultimate claim construction to the jury.

Reasoning: No clear founding-era practice assigns modern claim construction to juries. The closest historical analogues placed interpretation of patent specifications with judges. Existing precedent likewise separates judicial construction of the patent from the factfinder’s application of that construction. Judicial allocation also promotes consistent public notice of a patent’s scope.

Application: The district court could construe “inventory” independently and enter judgment when Westview’s system did not track articles of clothing as the claim required.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not remove infringement from the jury, classify every expert-supported dispute as legal, or decide the appellate standard for every possible subsidiary factual determination.

Legal basis: The Seventh Amendment and the Patent Act.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Souter’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s historical, precedential, and functional allocation of claim construction to judges.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment, Souter authorship, judge-jury allocation, and principal boundaries match the historical decision. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/370/))

Research sources: The official report for the patent, trial posture, historical analysis, functional reasoning, judgment, and unanimous lineup. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/517/370/))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### *Sheets v. Salt Lake County*, 45 F.3d 1383

Decided April 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: Gary Sheets voluntarily supplied his murdered wife’s diary to investigators under evidence supporting an assurance of confidentiality. After the criminal investigation ended, investigator Michael George allowed a favored author access to notes and possible copies of intimate diary material. The resulting book directly quoted the diary. A jury awarded Sheets $650,000, and the Tenth Circuit affirmed that judgment and the related attorney-fee award. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/45/1383/634441/))

Question presented: Whether the Fourteenth Amendment protects highly intimate personal information obtained by government for a limited criminal investigation from deliberate public disclosure serving no legitimate governmental function.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Hodge v. Jones* permits confidential retention of an accurate investigation record that produces no public dissemination, status change, or concrete disability. It expressly leaves materially disseminated information outside its holding. The specific-source rule from *Albright v. Oliver* does not displace the claim because no more specific constitutional guarantee directly governs the publication alleged here.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

The compensatory judgment and fee award remain in force. The Court decides only the granted constitutional-confidentiality question and enters no separate holding on municipal policy, the amount of damages, or attorney fees.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Rejects a freestanding substantive-due-process restriction on disclosure unsupported by a more specific constitutional source |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Deliberate publication of intimate investigative information

Holding: The Fourteenth Amendment protects against deliberate governmental publication of intimate marital and financial information entrusted to police for a limited criminal investigation when the investigative purpose has ended and the disclosure serves no law-enforcement purpose, public-record duty, or comparably sufficient governmental function.

Operative rule: The claimant must establish that the information concerned the claimant personally, was highly intimate, entered governmental possession for a limited official purpose under circumstances supporting confidentiality, and was intentionally disclosed for materially broader public dissemination without a legitimate governmental function connected to the acquisition or use of the information. Negligent disclosure, already public information, and lawful access required by public-record law remain outside the rule.

Reasoning: Sheets did not place his marriage, finances, character, or business affairs before the public by giving the diary to investigators examining his wife’s murder. Disclosure within the investigative team served the purpose for which the information was supplied. Access by a private author after the case ended served a different function. The supported verdict findings establish both the intimate character of the information concerning Sheets himself and intentional disclosure outside the limited investigative use.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not create a general constitutional right to secrecy, bar lawful public-record access, constitutionalize every breach of confidence, protect facts already broadly public, or establish liability for negligent handling.

Legal basis: The Fourteenth Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s narrow distinction between safeguarded investigative use and deliberate public dissemination serving no legitimate governmental function.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would leave the alleged breach to state privacy, tort, evidence, and public-record law. The dissent concludes that *Whalen* and *Nixon* permit regulated governmental information systems but do not establish the asserted substantive-due-process cause of action. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Hodge v. Jones* is distinguished. Its confidential-retention rule remains intact, while the present decision resolves the materially different circumstance it reserved: deliberate public dissemination of highly intimate information that entered governmental custody for a limited official purpose. *Albright*’s specific-source rule is applied without alteration.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court did not historically decide the case on the merits. The simulation affirms the Tenth Circuit’s historical judgment but states a narrower Supreme Court rule tied to highly intimate information, limited-purpose governmental acquisition, intentional public dissemination, and the absence of a legitimate governmental function. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/45/1383/634441/))

Research sources: The published Tenth Circuit opinion for the diary’s submission, confidentiality evidence, post-investigation disclosure, jury verdict, fee award, and historical judgment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/45/1383/634441/))

### *Holly Farms Corp. v. NLRB*, 517 U.S. 392

Argued February 21, 1996 | Decided May 6, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: Holly Farms employed chicken catchers, forklift operators, and truckdrivers who collected mature broilers raised by independent growers and transported them to the company’s processing plant. The Board treated all three categories as covered employees rather than excluded agricultural laborers, and the Fourth Circuit enforced the Board’s order. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-392.pdf))

Question presented: Whether the Board reasonably classified the live-haul workers as employees aligned with poultry processing rather than secondary agriculture.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *NLRB v. Town & Country Electric, Inc.* and *NLRB v. Health Care & Retirement Corp. of America* require labor-law status to follow enacted functions and actual operational relationships rather than labels or assumptions about whose general interests the work serves.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Live-haul truckdrivers | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The drivers remain included in the protected bargaining unit. |
| Chicken catchers and forklift operators | Affirmed, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | The catchers and loaders remain included in the protected bargaining unit. |

The Board’s order may be enforced.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports complete affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | O’Connor | Scalia, Thomas | Supports the judgment as to truckdrivers; opposes it as to catchers and forklift operators | Would classify the on-farm catching and loading work as secondary agriculture |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Live-haul truckdrivers

Holding: Holly Farms’s truckdrivers are not agricultural laborers because their work is neither performed by a farmer nor performed on a farm. Their transportation of completed broilers to the processor falls within the company’s processing operation.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support this classification.

##### Chicken catchers and forklift operators

Holding: The Board reasonably classified the catchers and forklift operators as covered employees rather than agricultural laborers.

Operative rule: Work performed on a farm does not become secondary agriculture merely because it concerns an agricultural product. When the governing statute is reasonably susceptible to more than one construction, the classification may consider whose operation the task serves, the stage at which the task begins, the employer and supervision, the grower’s role, and the task’s functional integration with processing.

Reasoning: The independent growers’ contractual raising obligations ended before the crews arrived. The growers did not employ, supervise, or assist the crews. The employees reported to and used equipment supplied through Holly Farms’s processing operation, and their work began the movement of finished broilers into slaughter and processing. The Board’s distinction between the completed raising operation and the processor’s collection operation therefore lay within the statute’s permissible meaning.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not classify every contract harvester or transporter as a covered employee. Work performed by the farmer, by the farmer’s employees, or genuinely incident to the farmer’s own operation may remain agricultural.

Legal basis: NLRA § 2(3) and the incorporated definition in FLSA § 3(f).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm as to all three categories of live-haul workers.

Public role: Joined Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s functional separation of the growers’ completed raising operation from Holly Farms’s collection and processing operation.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor, joined by Scalia and Thomas, agrees that the truckdrivers are covered employees because their transportation work is not performed on a farm. The dissenting portion treats catching and loading as work incident to poultry raising because it occurs on the farm and prepares the birds for market. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Town & Country* and *Health Care & Retirement Corp.* are applied without alteration. The decision adds a specific rule for the secondary-agriculture exclusion: physical presence on a farm is relevant but does not displace the inquiry into the operation, production stage, and employment relationship the work actually serves.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court decided the case on April 23, 1996. Historically, the judgment was 5-4 as to the catchers and forklift operators, while all nine Justices agreed about the truckdrivers. Stone replaces the Chief Justice’s historical partial dissent and joins Ginsburg’s complete opinion, producing a 6-3 simulated majority for the disputed worker categories. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-392.pdf))

Research sources: The official report for the worker categories, operational record, statutory classification, historical partial concurrence and dissent, lineup, and historical decision date. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-392.pdf))

### *United States v. Armstrong*, 517 U.S. 456

Argued February 26, 1996 | Decided May 13, 1996

From: Respondents supported a selective-prosecution discovery request with an affidavit reporting that all twenty-four identified federal crack cases handled by the local federal defender’s office in the relevant year involved Black defendants, together with materials indicating that non-Black persons committed crack offenses and were prosecuted in state court. The district court ordered production of charging information; after the Government refused, the indictments were dismissed, and the en banc Ninth Circuit affirmed. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-456.pdf))

Question presented: What showing a defendant must make to obtain discovery supporting a claim that federal prosecutors selected defendants for crack-cocaine prosecution because of race.

Controlling simulated law before decision: No prior simulated holding fixes the selective-prosecution discovery threshold. Ultimate liability continues to require discriminatory effect and discriminatory purpose, while prosecutorial decisions remain presumptively regular but subject to equal protection.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.

Supporting: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stone and Stevens.

The dismissal of the indictments is reversed. Respondents are not entitled to the ordered discovery on the present showing. The prosecution may resume, without prejudice to a later discovery request supported by the threshold stated below.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg in full; Breyer except the broader Rule 16 discussion | Supports reversal and remand | Seven Justices support the selective-prosecution discovery threshold; six support the Court’s complete Rule 16 analysis |
| Concurrence | Souter | None | Supports reversal and remand | Limits the Rule 16 discussion to selective-prosecution claims |
| Concurrence | Ginsburg | None | Supports reversal and remand | Emphasizes that the Rule 16 holding does not resolve unrelated affirmative defenses |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Breyer | None | Supports reversal and remand | Joins the constitutional discovery threshold but not the Court’s broader construction of Rule 16 |
| Dissent | Stevens | Stone | Would preserve a narrowed discovery inquiry | Would permit limited judicial inquiry on the office-specific showing and protect confidential prosecutorial material |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Source of discovery authority

Holding: Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 16(a)(1)(C) does not itself authorize discovery of prosecutorial charging files to prepare a selective-prosecution claim.

Reasoning: A selective-prosecution claim challenges the Government’s decision to prosecute rather than refuting its proof of the charged offense. Rule 16’s work-product protection also forecloses reading the provision as a general right to inspect prosecutorial deliberations.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide Rule 16’s treatment of every affirmative or collateral defense.

##### Threshold for selective-prosecution discovery

Holding: A defendant seeking discovery on a race-selective-prosecution claim must produce credible evidence that similarly situated persons of another race could have been prosecuted for comparable conduct but were not.

Operative rule: The showing must address both differential treatment and factual similarity in legally relevant charging characteristics. Statistical concentration may contribute to the showing but does not alone establish entitlement when it fails to identify or reliably describe comparable conduct handled differently. The ultimate burden to prove discriminatory effect and purpose remains on the defendant.

Reasoning: Discovery itself imposes institutional costs, risks exposing charging strategy, and may interfere with a core executive function. Those concerns justify a demanding threshold related to the elements of the ultimate claim. Respondents’ study described the race of persons federally prosecuted but did not establish that non-Black suspects with materially comparable conduct, evidence, criminal histories, firearms involvement, and federal referral circumstances were known to federal authorities and left unprosecuted.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not foreclose discovery on a properly supported claim, require a completed merits case before discovery, or decide the remedy for an ultimately proved constitutional violation.

Legal basis: The equal-protection component of the Fifth Amendment’s Due Process Clause and federal judicial authority over criminal proceedings.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Preserve the discovery ruling, with a remand solely to narrow production and impose protective safeguards.

Public role: Joined Stevens’s dissent.

Announced ground: Stone would hold that the office-specific all-Black federal docket, together with evidence of comparable non-Black offending handled outside federal court, justified a confined inquiry. The dissent would limit production to objective criteria, referrals, declinations, and anonymized comparator information while protecting work product, unrelated investigations, and sensitive identities.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens, joined by Stone, agrees that the evidence does not prove selective prosecution and that Rule 16 is not the source of the requested authority. The dissent rejects a threshold that effectively requires defendants to obtain the Government’s undisclosed comparator information before discovery and would permit a narrowed exercise of the district court’s inherent authority. This proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated selective-prosecution discovery rule. It leaves the ultimate discriminatory-effect, purpose, causation, and persuasion requirements unchanged.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 8-1, with Chief Justice Rehnquist writing and Justice Stevens dissenting. In the simulation, Stone joins Stevens’s position and the remaining historical majority becomes a 7-2 coalition. O’Connor authors the Court’s opinion as the senior Justice in that judgment majority. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-456.pdf))

Research sources: The official report for the affidavits, discovery order, dismissal, historical threshold, opinion topology, and Stevens’s historical objection to a categorical refusal of inquiry. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-456.pdf))

### *44 Liquormart, Inc. v. Rhode Island*, 517 U.S. 484

Argued November 1, 1995 | Decided May 13, 1996

From: Licensed retailers brought a federal action challenging Rhode Island’s prohibition on advertising retail liquor prices except at the place of sale. The district court held the ban unconstitutional, but the First Circuit reversed, accepting the State’s temperance theory and giving the prohibition an added presumption of validity under the Twenty-first Amendment. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-484.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether the ban satisfies the First Amendment and whether the Twenty-first Amendment alters the governing protection for truthful commercial information.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Rubin v. Coors Brewing Co.* requires a restriction on truthful commercial information to advance a substantial interest materially and possess a reasonable fit. *Florida Bar v. Went For It, Inc.* likewise requires concrete support for claimed harm and fit.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The First Circuit judgment is reversed. The lower courts shall enter or reinstate judgment barring enforcement of the challenged price-advertising prohibition. Rhode Island remains free to regulate alcohol prices, taxation, sales conditions, misleading advertising, and unlawful transactions directly.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion announcing the judgment and Opinion of the Court in part | Stevens | Parts I, II, and VII: Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg; Part VIII: Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg; Parts III and V: Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg; Part IV: Kennedy, Ginsburg; Part VI: Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports reversal | Court and plurality portions as stated |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | None | Supports reversal | Applies existing commercial-speech precedent without endorsing the principal opinion’s broader theory |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Thomas | None | Supports reversal | Treats governmental maintenance of consumer ignorance as an illegitimate interest |
| Concurrence in the judgment | O’Connor | Stone, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal | Applies the existing *Central Hudson* framework and finds the ban insufficiently fitted |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Effect of the Twenty-first Amendment

Holding: The Twenty-first Amendment does not exempt state alcohol regulation from the First Amendment or create an added presumption favoring suppression of protected speech.

Reasoning: The Amendment gives States substantial authority over alcohol distribution and use. It does not displace independently applicable constitutional guarantees.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support this proposition across the controlling and concurring writings.

##### Reasonable fit of the price-advertising ban

Holding: Rhode Island’s complete prohibition on truthful, nonmisleading liquor-price advertising fails the First Amendment because the State has not established a reasonable fit between suppressing price information and promoting temperance.

Operative rule: Under *Central Hudson*, government may regulate truthful commercial speech concerning lawful activity only in service of a substantial interest, through a measure that materially advances that interest and is reasonably fitted to it. A complete information ban cannot survive where direct regulation of prices, taxes, quantities, hours, or sales conditions would address the asserted harm more directly and the State supplies no adequate reason for suppressing consumer information instead.

Reasoning: Rhode Island sought to keep prices high by preventing price competition. The record did not establish that suppression would materially reduce consumption. More fundamentally, the State could regulate the price or conditions of liquor sales directly without preventing retailers from communicating truthful prices. The indirect paternalistic mechanism therefore lacked the required fit.

Limits and questions left open: False or misleading advertising, promotion of illegal transactions, sales to minors, and supported regulation of harmful marketing remain subject to separate analysis.

Authority of the proposition: Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, O’Connor, Stone, and Breyer support the reasonable-fit proposition at the same operative level of generality, producing an actual seven-Justice majority. No *Marks* synthesis is required for that holding.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s concurrence in the judgment.

Announced ground: Stone applies the existing *Central Hudson* framework without adopting a new commercial-speech tier. He joins the conclusion that the ban fails reasonable-fit review because direct nonspeech regulation is available and the Twenty-first Amendment supplies no speech exception.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens’s plurality portions would review complete bans on truthful, nonmisleading commercial information with special care and reject deference to a legislative objective of keeping consumers ignorant. Thomas would treat that objective as categorically illegitimate. Scalia adheres to existing precedent while reserving his preferred historical approach. Those broader propositions are noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Rubin v. Coors* is applied and reaffirmed. The decision does not replace *Central Hudson* with a new test. It confirms that the Twenty-first Amendment does not alter the existing material-advancement and reasonable-fit requirements.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment and fragmented opinion structure track the historical decision. Stone occupies the Chief Justice’s historical place in O’Connor’s narrower concurrence, which also includes Souter and Breyer. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-484.pdf))

Research sources: The official report for the First Circuit posture, statutory ban, record, Twenty-first Amendment holding, fragmented joins, and historical judgment. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-484.pdf))

### *United States v. Noland*, 517 U.S. 535

Argued March 25, 1996 | Decided May 13, 1996

From: The IRS filed postpetition tax and penalty claims that ordinarily possessed administrative-expense priority. The bankruptcy court subordinated the penalty claim to general unsecured claims, not because of inequitable governmental conduct, but because tax penalties as a class were punitive and reduced recovery by innocent creditors. The Sixth Circuit affirmed. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-535.pdf))

Question presented: Whether § 510(c) permits categorical equitable subordination of an otherwise valid federal tax-penalty claim.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The penalty claim may not be subordinated solely because it is punitive. The bankruptcy court may consider any preserved case-specific ground permitted by § 510(c).

Justice Souter delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Categorical equitable subordination

Holding: Section 510(c) does not authorize a bankruptcy court to subordinate a category of claims in derogation of Congress’s enacted priority scheme.

Operative rule: Equitable subordination must rest on the circumstances of the particular claim and recognized equitable principles. A court may not reorder an entire statutory class because it disagrees with the distributive consequences Congress selected.

Reasoning: Congress adopted the preexisting doctrine of equitable subordination while preserving the Code’s general hierarchy. A categorical rule placing tax penalties below general unsecured claims operates at the same legislative level as Congress’s priority judgment. The punitive character of every claim in the class and its effect on innocent creditors are general policy considerations, not case-specific equities.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide whether creditor misconduct is invariably required or foreclose subordination based on fraud, insider abuse, inequitable conduct, or another recognized case-specific ground.

Legal basis: 11 U.S.C. §§ 510(c), 503, and 507.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Souter’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s distinction between a case-specific equitable adjustment and judicial revision of a statutory priority class.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule governing categorical equitable subordination. It leaves the content of recognized case-specific equitable grounds open.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment, Souter authorship, statutory rule, and reservation concerning a universal misconduct requirement match the historical decision. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-535.pdf))

Research sources: The official report for the claim’s priority, categorical rationale, absence of governmental misconduct, argument date, historical judgment, and reserved question. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-535.pdf))

### *United Food & Commercial Workers Union Local 751 v. Brown Group, Inc.*, 517 U.S. 544

Argued February 20, 1996 | Decided May 13, 1996

From: The union alleged that Brown Group began layoffs associated with a plant closing without giving the notice required by the WARN Act. It sought backpay and benefits for affected members. The Eighth Circuit dismissed because individual participation would be needed to establish each worker’s damages. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-544.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether *Hunt*’s individual-participation requirement is constitutional or prudential, and whether Congress authorized representative union litigation for WARN Act damages.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The union may litigate the common WARN Act claim and seek relief for injured members. The district court may use individual proofs, claims procedures, subclasses, or distribution safeguards as necessary. No WARN Act liability finding is entered.

Justice Souter delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Representative standing for statutory damages

Holding: The third *Hunt* requirement, concerning the need for individual member participation, is prudential rather than an irreducible Article III limitation. Congress authorized unions to sue for members’ WARN Act damages notwithstanding the need for individualized calculations.

Operative rule: Article III requires at least one represented member to possess injury, causation, and redressability. Germaneness assures a genuine representative stake. Once those requirements are met, Congress may authorize an association to litigate common liability and seek member relief even though individual participation is needed to establish the amount due.

Reasoning: The asserted violation arose from one plant closing and one alleged failure to provide notice. Payroll records and ordinary claims procedures can determine individual backpay and benefits. Requiring every employee to file a separate action would defeat the representative enforcement mechanism Congress expressly selected without serving an additional case-or-controversy function.

Limits and questions left open: The association may not recover for persons who are not members or injured, eliminate individual defenses, or dispense with proof of each claimant’s compensable loss.

Legal basis: Article III and the WARN Act.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Souter’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision clarifies *Hunt*. Member standing remains constitutional; the individual-participation prong is an administrability rule subject to congressional alteration. The Court creates no general rule permitting representative damages suits without statutory authorization.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment, Souter authorship, distinction between Article III and prudential standing, and WARN Act result match the historical decision. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-544.pdf))

Research sources: The official report for the closing and notice claim, statutory authorization, standing analysis, argument date, judgment, and unanimous lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-544.pdf))

---

### *BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore*, 517 U.S. 559

Argued October 11, 1995 | Decided May 20, 1996

From: BMW sold Gore a new automobile that had been repainted after minor transit damage. The jury awarded $4,000 in compensatory damages and $4 million in punitive damages based in substantial part on BMW’s nationwide nondisclosure policy. The Alabama Supreme Court reduced the punitive award to $2 million after concluding that the jury had improperly used similar transactions outside Alabama in setting the amount. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-559.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether the remitted award is grossly excessive under the Due Process Clause and whether Alabama may punish conduct occurring lawfully in other States.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Honda Motor Co. v. Oberg* requires meaningful judicial review capable of removing legally impermissible considerations and correcting a punitive award outside the rational range supported by the evidence. It establishes no numerical ratio or mandatory guidepost formula.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.

Supporting: Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer. Opposing: Stone, Scalia, Thomas, and Ginsburg.

The Alabama Supreme Court shall determine whether state law requires a new punitive-damages trial or permits it independently to fix an award consistent with the constitutional limits stated below. The $4,000 compensatory award remains undisturbed.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Breyer | O’Connor, Souter | Supports reversal and remand | Emphasizes the need for legal standards that constrain punitive-damages review |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Rejects a substantive-due-process ceiling on the amount of a punitive award |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | Stone | Would affirm | Concludes that Alabama supplied meaningful review and that the remitted amount should remain |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Territorial limits on punitive authority

Holding: A State may punish conduct occurring within its lawful regulatory interest, but it may not use punitive damages to impose its policy throughout the Nation or punish transactions lawful in other States as independent offenses.

Reasoning: State sovereignty limits Alabama’s punitive authority to protection of its own consumers and economy. The constitutional assessment must therefore examine the penalty justified by BMW’s Alabama conduct rather than use every nationwide transaction as a separate multiplier.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not prevent consideration of other conduct when independently relevant under lawful evidentiary rules, but the State may not punish the defendant for lawful out-of-state transactions themselves.

##### Gross excessiveness of the remitted award

Holding: The $2 million punitive award is grossly excessive in relation to Alabama’s legitimate punitive and deterrent interests.

Operative rule: Constitutional review considers three principal, nonexclusive guideposts: the reprehensibility of the defendant’s conduct; the relationship between punitive damages and actual or potential harm; and the difference between the award and authorized civil or criminal sanctions for comparable misconduct. No fixed numerical ratio governs every case.

Reasoning: Gore suffered a $4,000 economic loss involving no threat to health or safety. The remitted award was five hundred times the compensatory damages, and the comparable statutory sanctions were modest. The record did not show recidivism after an adjudication of unlawfulness, deliberate endangerment, or another circumstance capable of supporting the multimillion-dollar penalty.

Limits and questions left open: High ratios may remain permissible when actual damages are difficult to detect, the potential harm is substantially greater than the realized injury, or nominal damages conceal serious misconduct. The guideposts do not establish a mathematical cap.

Legal basis: The Fourteenth Amendment’s Due Process Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm the Alabama Supreme Court’s remitted $2 million judgment.

Public role: Joined Ginsburg’s dissent.

Announced ground: Stone concludes that Alabama performed the meaningful review required by *Honda*, reduced the verdict by half, and removed punishment resting on transactions outside its lawful authority. Evidence of BMW’s national policy could demonstrate deliberateness, knowledge, profitability, and the need for deterrence without treating each out-of-state sale as a separately punishable offense. Stone would not replace the state court’s rational-range judgment with a new federally selected amount.

#### Material Separate Writings

Breyer, joined by O’Connor and Souter, stresses that the guideposts must function as genuine legal constraints rather than labels for case-specific intuition. Scalia, joined by Thomas, would confine due process to procedures for contesting the award and rejects substantive federal review of the amount. Those propositions are noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Honda Motor Co. v. Oberg* is extended. Meaningful state judicial review remains necessary but is no longer always sufficient: a reviewed award may still violate the substantive constitutional ceiling when it is grossly excessive under the guideposts adopted here. *Honda*’s rejection of a fixed ratio remains unchanged.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, 5-4 vote, Stevens majority, Breyer concurrence, and two dissents track the historical decision. Stone occupies the Chief Justice’s historical place in Ginsburg’s dissent and preserves the simulation’s broader *Honda*-based rational-range position. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-559.pdf))

Research sources: The official report for the repainting, compensatory and punitive awards, Alabama remittitur, territorial analysis, guideposts, historical lineup, and separate writings. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-559.pdf))

### *Romer v. Evans*, 517 U.S. 620

Argued October 10, 1995 | Decided May 20, 1996

From: Colorado’s Amendment 2 repealed existing state and local protections against discrimination based on homosexual, lesbian, or bisexual orientation and prohibited any state or local governmental body from adopting comparable protections in the future unless the state constitution was amended again. The Colorado Supreme Court affirmed an injunction against enforcement. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-620.pdf))

Question presented: Whether Amendment 2’s breadth, purpose, and restructuring of ordinary legal protection satisfy the Equal Protection Clause.

Controlling simulated law before decision: No prior simulated decision assigns a level of scrutiny to sexual-orientation classifications. *Department of Agriculture v. Moreno* and *City of Cleburne v. Cleburne Living Center* establish that bare hostility and a desire to disadvantage a group for its own sake are not legitimate governmental interests.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

Amendment 2 remains unenforceable. Colorado retains authority to regulate conduct and define ordinary antidiscrimination policy through laws satisfying constitutional requirements.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stone | None | Supports affirmance | Would also apply intermediate scrutiny to the express classification and statewide political disability |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Treats Amendment 2 as a permissible refusal to grant special legal protection |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Status-based withdrawal of ordinary legal protection

Holding: Amendment 2 violates equal protection because its extraordinary breadth bears no rational relationship to an independent legitimate governmental purpose.

Operative rule: A law that identifies a class by status and broadly withdraws access to ordinary legal protection must bear a rational relationship to a legitimate objective distinct from hostility, civic subordination, or a desire to make the class unequal. The breadth and operation of the classification may demonstrate that the asserted objectives do not explain the law.

Reasoning: Amendment 2 did not regulate a defined act, preserve one associational exemption, prohibit quotas, or allocate one governmental benefit. It disabled every state and local governmental channel from protecting one class against discrimination across unrelated fields including housing, employment, education, public accommodations, and public services. The asserted interests in preserving associational choice and conserving enforcement resources were far narrower than the constitutional disability imposed. Its breadth therefore revealed that disadvantaging the class was the operative object.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not require Colorado to enact any particular antidiscrimination law, create a right to preferred political outcomes, invalidate neutral generally applicable rules, or decide a substantive-liberty claim.

Legal basis: The Equal Protection Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full and filed a concurrence.

Announced ground: Stone agrees that Amendment 2 fails ordinary rational-basis review. He would additionally hold that an express classification withdrawing ordinary legal protection because of sexual orientation receives intermediate scrutiny, requiring an actual important objective and a substantial, nonstereotyped relationship between the classification and that objective.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stone’s proposed intermediate-scrutiny classification is noncontrolling. Scalia, joined by Thomas, would sustain Amendment 2 as a lawful decision to prevent preferred legal treatment and rejects the Court’s inference of unconstitutional animus.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Moreno* and *Cleburne* are applied and clarified. A class-wide legal disability whose breadth cannot be explained by the asserted interests fails rational-basis review when hostility or civic subordination supplies the remaining explanation. The Court does not assign sexual-orientation classifications to a heightened-scrutiny tier.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court affirmed 6-3, with Kennedy writing for Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, and Scalia dissenting with the Chief Justice and Thomas. Stone joins Kennedy’s opinion, producing a 7-2 simulated judgment, and separately states his controlled intermediate-scrutiny position. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-620.pdf))

Research sources: The official report for Amendment 2’s text and operation, the Colorado judgment, rational-basis holding, historical lineup, and dissent. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-620.pdf))

### *Henderson v. United States*, 517 U.S. 654

Argued March 19, 1996 | Decided May 20, 1996

From: Henderson timely filed a seaman’s personal-injury action under the Suits in Admiralty Act and completed service within Rule 4’s extendable timetable, but not within the older statute’s undefined requirement that service occur “forthwith.” The Fifth Circuit treated that requirement as jurisdictional and affirmed dismissal. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-654.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether Rule 4 supersedes the statutory service instruction and whether noncompliance with the superseded prescription is jurisdictional.

Controlling simulated law before decision: The existing simulated rule of jurisdictional characterization distinguishes the source of subject-matter jurisdiction and sovereign consent from procedural or claim-processing prescriptions governing how an action proceeds.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor and Thomas.

The district court shall apply Rule 4’s service timetable and extension provisions and adjudicate any remaining defense. The “forthwith” prescription supplies no independent ground for dismissal.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | Kennedy | Supports reversal and remand | Agrees that the Rules Enabling Act gives Rule 4 controlling effect |
| Dissent | Thomas | O’Connor | Would affirm | Treats the service instruction as part of the conditions Congress attached to the sovereign-immunity waiver |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Governing service rule and jurisdiction

Holding: Rule 4 supersedes the Suits in Admiralty Act’s inconsistent “forthwith” service requirement, and failure to satisfy that superseded requirement is not jurisdictional.

Operative rule: When a federal action is timely commenced under the governing statute, directions concerning the manner and timing of service ordinarily concern practice and procedure unless Congress clearly makes them conditions defining the cause, waiver, or adjudicatory authority. A later valid Federal Rule displaces an inconsistent earlier procedural prescription under the Rules Enabling Act.

Reasoning: The Act’s first sentence supplies the waiver and cause. The service direction governs what happens after filing and appears alongside venue and transfer provisions with a procedural character. Rule 4 supplies a comprehensive and extendable service regime. Treating the older direction as jurisdictional would collapse filing, consent to suit, service, and subject-matter jurisdiction into one inquiry and defeat the uniform Rules.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not alter the Act’s limitations period, excuse compliance with Rule 4, or decide any remaining defense under that Rule.

Legal basis: The Suits in Admiralty Act, Federal Rules of Civil Procedure 4 and 82, and 28 U.S.C. § 2072.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s separation of the statutory waiver and timely filing from nonjurisdictional postfiling service procedure.

#### Material Separate Writings

Thomas, joined by O’Connor, would treat every requirement Congress placed in the waiver provision as a condition on suit and would affirm the jurisdictional dismissal. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The existing rule of jurisdictional characterization is applied and extended to statutory service requirements. Placement inside a waiver statute does not, without more, convert a case-processing instruction into a limitation on subject-matter jurisdiction.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 6-3. Ginsburg’s opinion was joined by Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer; Thomas dissented with the Chief Justice and O’Connor. Stone rejects the Chief Justice’s historical position and joins Ginsburg, producing a 7-2 simulated judgment. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-654.pdf))

Research sources: The official report for the filing and service chronology, Fifth Circuit judgment, Rules Enabling Act analysis, argument date, historical joins, concurrence, and dissent. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-654.pdf))

### *Doctor’s Associates, Inc. v. Casarotto*, 517 U.S. 681

Argued April 16, 1996 | Decided May 20, 1996

From: A Subway franchise agreement contained an arbitration provision in ordinary type on page nine. Montana law made an arbitration clause unenforceable unless the contract’s first page contained a prescribed warning in underlined capital letters. The Montana Supreme Court refused to enforce the clause because the agreement lacked that arbitration-specific legend. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-681.pdf))

Question presented: Whether FAA § 2 preempts the arbitration-specific notice requirement.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Allied-Bruce Terminix Cos. v. Dobson* applies FAA § 2 throughout its lawful commerce reach while preserving contract defenses applicable generally. *Mastrobuono v. Shearson Lehman Hutton, Inc.* and *First Options of Chicago, Inc. v. Kaplan* preserve ordinary contract interpretation, formation, and actual assent.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-1.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

Montana courts shall disregard the arbitration-specific notice statute and decide enforceability under ordinary contract principles applicable to comparable provisions. Arbitration may not be compelled if a preserved neutral defense is established.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | None | Would affirm | Rejects application of the FAA in state-court proceedings |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Arbitration-specific contract notice

Holding: FAA § 2 preempts Montana’s special first-page notice requirement because the requirement applies solely to arbitration agreements and conditions their enforceability on a formality not imposed on contracts generally.

Operative rule: States may apply generally applicable defenses such as fraud, duress, unconscionability, lack of assent, or neutral conspicuousness rules. They may not single out arbitration clauses for special legends, placement, form, or enforceability burdens.

Reasoning: The Montana statute did not determine whether the Casarottos actually assented or were misled in this transaction. It rendered the clause unenforceable categorically because arbitration, and only arbitration, lacked a prescribed warning. That unequal treatment conflicts directly with § 2’s command that arbitration provisions stand on the same footing as other contracts.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not approve hidden terms, waive ordinary formation requirements, displace generally applicable unconscionability law, or require arbitration of a dispute the parties did not agree to submit.

Legal basis: FAA § 2 and the Supremacy Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground. His join preserves ordinary state-law inquiry into assent, fraud, surprise, and unconscionability while rejecting the arbitration-only formality.

#### Material Separate Writings

Thomas would adhere to his position that the FAA does not govern state-court proceedings and would affirm the Montana judgment. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Allied-Bruce*, *Mastrobuono*, and *First Options* are applied without alteration. The decision adds a recurring equal-treatment application: a State may regulate contract notice neutrally, but it may not impose a special notice condition on arbitration alone.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated 8-1 judgment, Ginsburg authorship, complete majority lineup, and Thomas dissent match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the Chief Justice’s historical join. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-681.pdf))

Research sources: The official report for the franchise agreement, Montana notice statute, remand history, argument date, statutory holding, historical judgment, lineup, and dissent. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-681.pdf))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### *Pinder v. Johnson*, 54 F.3d 1169

Decided May 21, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: The en banc Fourth Circuit reversed the denial of qualified immunity to Officer Donald Johnson. On the facts assumed at summary judgment, Johnson knew that Don Pittman had threatened Pinder and her children, assured Pinder that Pittman would remain detained overnight, then charged only minor offenses permitting Pittman’s prompt release. Pittman returned to the house and set the fire that killed Pinder’s three children. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-4th-circuit/1336802.html))

Questions presented: Whether Johnson’s affirmative assurance and use of official authority created or intensified a specific private danger within the Due Process Clause, and whether any resulting right was clearly established on March 10, 1989.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *DeShaney v. Winnebago County Department of Social Services* supplies the no-general-duty rule. *Elder v. Holloway* requires consideration of the complete legal landscape for qualified immunity, while *Johnson v. Jones* and *Behrens v. Pelletier* confine the interlocutory appeal to separable legal questions on the assumed facts.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

The federal individual-capacity damages claim against Johnson ends. The judgment does not dispose of municipal claims, state-law claims, or claims against any defendant outside this appeal.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Scalia and Thomas as to qualified immunity and the judgment | Supports affirmance | Seven Justices join the constitutional and immunity analysis; all nine join the immunity disposition |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Supports affirmance | Would resolve the case on qualified immunity without announcing a new substantive-due-process rule |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Affirmative official creation or intensification of a private danger

Holding: On the assumed facts, a due-process violation is stated when an official knows of a specific and imminent violent threat, gives a concrete assurance that foreseeably induces the threatened person to abandon available self-protection, then affirmatively uses official authority with deliberate indifference to make that person materially more vulnerable to the identified violence, thereby causing the harm.

Operative rule: The claimant must establish a specific imminent threat, affirmative official inducement, reasonable reliance that changes available self-protection, deliberate use of governmental authority, a material increase in vulnerability, deliberate indifference, and causation. Knowledge of danger, a broken promise, negligent charging, or failure to provide protection is insufficient without that affirmative sequence.

Reasoning: *DeShaney* rejects liability for failing to rescue a person from private violence. It does not authorize an official affirmatively to induce a person to surrender available precautions and then use governmental authority in a way that materially increases the very danger addressed by the assurance. The assumed sequence differs from passive nonprotection because Johnson allegedly affected Pinder’s conduct while using his charging authority in a manner that made Pittman’s immediate return foreseeable.

Limits and questions left open: The decision creates no general police duty to protect, no constitutional claim for every mistaken charging decision, and no liability for an assurance standing alone. Whether Pinder could prove reliance, deliberate indifference, material increased vulnerability, and causation would ordinarily be factual questions, but personal damages are barred here by qualified immunity.

Legal basis: The Fourteenth Amendment.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s opinion is joined on this proposition by Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing a seven-Justice majority.

##### Qualified immunity

Holding: Johnson is entitled to qualified immunity because the narrow state-created-danger rule was not clearly established in March 1989.

Reasoning: *DeShaney* had been decided eighteen days earlier and emphasized the absence of a general duty outside custody. Earlier lower-court authority concerning affirmative danger creation did not clearly establish that an assurance, reliance, and a later charging decision produced the constitutional violation recognized today. Johnson therefore lacked the fair notice required for personal damages.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support qualified immunity and affirmance.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins both the narrow state-created-danger holding and the separate conclusion that the rule was not sufficiently established in March 1989 to support personal liability.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would reserve the constitutional question and affirm solely because no materially specific precedent gave Johnson fair notice. That reservation is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*DeShaney* is clarified rather than narrowed. The no-general-duty rule remains controlling for private violence, but it does not foreclose liability for an official’s deliberate affirmative creation or material intensification of a specific danger under the conditions stated here. *Elder*, *Johnson v. Jones*, and *Behrens* are applied without alteration.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court historically denied certiorari, leaving the Fourth Circuit’s qualified-immunity judgment in place without Supreme Court merits precedent. The simulation unanimously affirms the same personal-immunity result but establishes, by seven votes, a narrow state-created-danger rule for future conduct.

Research sources: The published en banc Fourth Circuit opinion for the record, posture, historical judgment, and lower-court divisions. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-4th-circuit/1336802.html))
