### *Ornelas v. United States*, 517 U.S. 690

Argued March 26, 1996 | Decided May 28, 1996

From: The Seventh Circuit affirmed petitioners’ convictions after reviewing deferentially the district court’s determinations that officers possessed reasonable suspicion and probable cause during a motel investigation and warrantless automobile search. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-690.pdf))

Question presented: Whether historical facts receive clear-error review while the ultimate Fourth Amendment determinations receive independent appellate review.

Controlling simulated law before decision: Earlier the same term, *Thompson v. Keohane* separated deferential review of historical facts from independent application of an objective constitutional standard. No prior simulated decision had applied that division to reasonable suspicion and probable cause.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 8-1.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia.

The Seventh Circuit shall accept supported historical and credibility findings unless clearly erroneous, then determine independently whether the circumstances established reasonable suspicion and probable cause. It may affirm or reverse the suppression ruling without reopening supported facts.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | None | Would affirm | Would treat the ultimate determinations as sufficiently factbound to receive deferential review |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Appellate review of reasonable suspicion and probable cause

Holding: Historical facts underlying a stop or search are reviewed for clear error. Whether those facts amount to reasonable suspicion or probable cause is reviewed de novo, with respectful consideration of record-supported inferences drawn by trial judges and experienced local officers.

Operative rule: The trial court determines what occurred, resolves credibility, and identifies the immediate factual setting. The appellate court independently applies the constitutional standard to those facts. Practical consideration of local knowledge and police experience does not transfer final authority over the constitutional boundary.

Reasoning: Independent review promotes uniform Fourth Amendment law and supplies guidance to police and lower courts. Clear-error review preserves the trial court’s superior position concerning testimony, credibility, and immediate context. The two functions can be respected without turning experiential inferences into conclusive legal determinations.

Application: The Seventh Circuit reviewed the ultimate constitutional characterizations only for clear error. Its judgment does not establish that the independent review required today was performed.

Limits and questions left open: Independent review does not authorize appellate factfinding, disregard of supported credibility determinations, or use of a mechanical checklist of suspicious circumstances. The Court does not decide whether suppression is ultimately required.

Legal basis: The Fourth Amendment and federal appellate standards of review.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone separates factfinding from constitutional characterization and requires independent appellate responsibility for the nationwide boundaries of reasonable suspicion and probable cause.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia would affirm because he regards the trial judge’s appraisal of contextual and experiential inferences as inseparable from the probable-cause and reasonable-suspicion determinations. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Thompson v. Keohane* is applied and clarified. Its distinction between historical facts and independent application of an objective constitutional rule now governs reasonable suspicion and probable cause.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court also vacated and remanded 8-1, with Justice Scalia dissenting. Chief Justice Rehnquist wrote the historical opinion. Stone authors the simulated Court’s opinion and adopts the same basic division between fact review and independent constitutional judgment. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-690.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the suppression record, standard-of-review question, historical judgment, and lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-690.pdf))

---

### *Love v. Pepersack*, 47 F.3d 120, No. 94-1582

Decided May 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: Maryland officials denied Love’s handgun-purchase application after an incomplete records check disclosed arrests whose dispositions were not immediately known, even though arrest history alone was not a statutory disqualification. Love obtained a state-court order requiring approval and then sought personal damages under § 1983. The Fourth Circuit affirmed dismissal. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/us-4th-circuit/1036155.html))

Questions presented: Whether the corrected licensing denial states a substantive-due-process claim; whether the Second Amendment protects an individual right to acquire a common handgun for home defense and applies to the States; and whether the officers are protected by qualified immunity.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Albright v. Oliver* requires assignment of an injury to the specific constitutional source that governs it and rejects substantive due process as a generalized federal tort. No controlling simulated decision resolves the individual scope or incorporation of the Second Amendment. *Elder v. Holloway* governs clearly established law.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The federal damages action is dismissed. The state judgment ordering approval of Love’s application remains unaffected.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas as to substantive due process, qualified immunity, and the judgment | Supports affirmance | Five Justices join the complete opinion; all nine join the two controlling grounds |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Reaches the Second Amendment merits, recognizes an incorporated individual right, and then applies qualified immunity |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Corrected licensing error and substantive due process

Holding: An erroneous or unauthorized application of state licensing law does not become a substantive-due-process violation merely because the decision was arbitrary under state law, particularly when the State’s established review process supplied complete correction. Any procedural-due-process theory was not pleaded and is not decided.

Reasoning: State courts exist in part to correct misapplications of state law. Love obtained the license-related relief she sought through that process. The Fourteenth Amendment does not convert every overturned licensing decision into a generalized substantive constitutional tort.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide a properly pleaded claim alleging denial of meaningful procedure, retaliation, a forbidden classification, or a systematic policy incapable of correction through available process.

Legal basis: The Fourteenth Amendment.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support the substantive-due-process holding.

##### Qualified immunity and the unresolved Second Amendment question

Holding: The defendant officers are entitled to qualified immunity because, in September 1990, no sufficiently specific body of law established that the Second and Fourteenth Amendments protected acquisition of a handgun from denial based solely on an unresolved arrest record.

Reasoning: The received Supreme Court and circuit authorities pointed against an incorporated individual right, and no controlling authority established that arrest history could never support temporary denial while the record was investigated. Whatever the correct constitutional rule, the officers lacked the fair notice necessary for personal damages.

Questions not reached by the Court: The Court does not decide whether the Second Amendment protects an individual right, whether that right applies to the States, or whether lawful acquisition is part of its protected core.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support qualified immunity. Only the separate four-Justice concurrence reaches the underlying arms question.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined the substantive-due-process and qualified-immunity portions of O’Connor’s opinion and filed a concurrence joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

Announced ground: Stone would hold that the Second and Fourteenth Amendments protect a law-abiding person’s acquisition and possession of a common handgun in the home for self-defense. An unadjudicated arrest, without a disqualifying conviction or adequate dangerousness finding, cannot alone bar that acquisition. Stone nevertheless agrees that the rule was not clearly established in September 1990 and therefore supports dismissal of the damages action.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Albright* is applied without alteration. The Court creates no controlling Second Amendment rule. Stone’s concurrence, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, is a material but noncontrolling same-term proposition concerning an incorporated individual right and the acquisition of a common handgun.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court did not historically decide the case on the merits. The Fourth Circuit affirmed dismissal and rejected an incorporated individual-right theory. The simulation affirms unanimously but does not adopt the lower court’s collective-right rationale; four Justices expressly state the opposite constitutional position. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/us-4th-circuit/1036155.html))

Research sources: The published Fourth Circuit opinion for the corrected docket, licensing procedure, state-court relief, constitutional theories, and historical judgment. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/us-4th-circuit/1036155.html))

### *Quackenbush v. Allstate Insurance Co.*, 517 U.S. 706

Argued February 20, 1996 | Decided June 3, 1996

From: The California Insurance Commissioner brought a removed diversity action seeking contract and tort damages arising from reinsurance agreements. The district court remanded under *Burford* abstention without deciding Allstate’s motion to compel arbitration. The Ninth Circuit held the remand reviewable, vacated it, and directed the case to arbitration. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-706.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether the abstention remand was appealable, whether *Burford* authorized dismissal or remand of a damages action, and what portion of the Ninth Circuit’s remedial direction could lawfully remain.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Digital Equipment Corp. v. Desktop Direct, Inc.* preserves narrow collateral-order discipline. Existing jurisdictional law distinguishes a § 1447(c) remand from a discretionary surrender of concededly existing federal jurisdiction.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Reviewability and rejection of the abstention remand | Ninth Circuit affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The diversity action returns to federal district court. |
| Direction compelling arbitration | Vacated, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | Arbitrability shall be addressed by the district court under ordinary law because it was outside the granted abstention questions. |

Overall disposition: Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded, 9-0.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports the complete judgment | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | None | Supports the complete judgment | Treats the prohibition on dismissal or remand of a legal damages action as categorical |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | None | Supports the complete judgment | Emphasizes federalism and comity as independent foundations of abstention, while regarding a retained-jurisdiction stay as the ordinary available response |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Appealability of an abstention remand

Holding: A remand based on *Burford* abstention rather than a jurisdictional or removal-procedure defect under § 1447(c) is reviewable under § 1291.

Operative rule: Section 1447(d) bars review only of remands authorized by § 1447(c). An abstention remand that conclusively surrenders the federal action to a state court, determines a question separate from the merits, and cannot later be reviewed in the federal case is immediately appealable.

Reasoning: The district court did not find jurisdiction absent or removal defective. It chose not to exercise existing jurisdiction. The remand completely ended the federal proceeding and placed the parties effectively out of federal court.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. §§ 1291, 1447(c), and 1447(d).

##### Burford abstention in a damages action

Holding: *Burford* concerns may support a bounded stay of a federal damages action, but they do not authorize dismissal or remand of that action.

Operative rule: A federal court may decline equitable or otherwise discretionary relief in an appropriate abstention case. When legal damages are sought within existing jurisdiction, the court may postpone adjudication through a necessary and proportionate stay while a material state-law issue is resolved, but it ordinarily must retain the action.

Reasoning: Abstention coordinates federal adjudication with important state interests; it does not erase Congress’s jurisdictional grant. A stay can protect California’s interest in coherent insurer liquidation while preserving the federal forum. Remand surrenders that forum entirely.

Application: The district court’s remand was impermissible. The Court does not decide whether a temporary stay is warranted on the present record.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not prevent remand on an independently valid statutory ground, require a stay, or decide arbitrability.

Legal basis: Federal abstention doctrine and the statutory grant of diversity jurisdiction.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm the Ninth Circuit as to reviewability and rejection of the remand; vacate its arbitration direction; remand for further federal proceedings.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the distinction between a retained-jurisdiction stay and outright surrender of a legal damages action. He announces no additional ground.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia reads the Court’s legal-damages boundary as foreclosing dismissal or remand even in a case presenting unusually serious federalism concerns. Kennedy would reserve whether an otherwise unavoidable affront to federalism could justify dismissal in a materially different case. The reservation is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Digital Equipment* is clarified rather than enlarged generally. The immediate appeal rests on the special finality of an order surrendering the entire federal action to a state court. The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule distinguishing a permissible abstention stay from impermissible dismissal or remand of a damages action.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court unanimously affirmed the Ninth Circuit’s judgment and preserved its arbitration direction. The simulation agrees unanimously on appellate jurisdiction and the *Burford* rule but vacates the arbitration directive because that issue was outside the controlled grant. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-706.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the removed action, remand, Ninth Circuit disposition, statutory analysis, historical judgment, and concurrences. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-706.pdf))

### *Smiley v. Citibank (South Dakota), N.A.*, 517 U.S. 735

Argued April 24, 1996 | Decided June 3, 1996

From: Citibank, a national bank located in South Dakota, charged California cardholders late-payment fees authorized by South Dakota law. The California Supreme Court held that the fees were “interest” under 12 U.S.C. § 85 and rejected the application of California’s contrary restrictions. During the litigation, the Comptroller adopted a regulation expressly including late fees within “interest.” ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-735.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether the statutory term includes credit-card late fees, what weight the Comptroller’s interpretation receives, and whether California’s contrary regulation is preempted.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *NationsBank of North Carolina, N.A. v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co.* gives controlling weight to the Comptroller’s permissible construction of an unresolved National Bank Act term, while Stone’s separate position in that case relied on independent construction and persuasive agency expertise. *MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. AT&T* continues to require that an agency remain within the statutory design.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Citibank may charge the late fee permitted by the law of the State in which it is located. No deceptive-practices or contract claim outside § 85’s preemptive operation is decided.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone | None | Supports affirmance | Independently construes § 85 and treats the Comptroller’s expertise as substantially persuasive rather than controlling |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Late-payment fees as interest

Holding: A credit-card late-payment fee charged as part of the price of extending and carrying credit is “interest” within § 85. A national bank may impose the amount permitted by the law of the State in which the bank is located.

Reasoning: Interest need not be stated solely as a percentage of principal over time. A late fee compensates for delayed repayment, additional servicing, and increased credit risk. Section 85’s national rule would be defeated if each borrower State could relabel a credit charge and apply a different ceiling.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not classify every penalty, service fee, or collateral charge as interest. It does not authorize deception, excuse failure to disclose a contractual fee, or displace neutral state law outside the field governed by § 85.

Legal basis: 12 U.S.C. §§ 85 and 86 and the Supremacy Clause.

Authority of the proposition: Scalia’s opinion and Stone’s concurrence support the same statutory and preemption conclusion, producing a unanimous holding.

##### Weight of the Comptroller’s interpretation

Holding: The term “interest” is sufficiently open at the relevant boundary that the Comptroller’s reasonable and authoritative construction, adopted within the agency’s assigned banking domain, receives controlling weight.

Reasoning: The interpretation fits accepted usage, the functional pricing of credit, and the National Bank Act’s structure. It does not manufacture a new banking power or eliminate a statutory limitation.

Authority of the proposition: Scalia’s opinion is joined on this rationale by Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing an eight-Justice majority. Stone reaches the same judgment through independent statutory interpretation.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Concurred in the judgment.

Announced ground: Stone independently construes § 85 to include the late fee. He gives the Comptroller’s reasoned expertise substantial persuasive weight but does not treat the agency’s interpretation as controlling merely because the statutory boundary is open.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*NationsBank* is applied at its controlling eight-Justice scope. *MCI* is distinguished because the Comptroller’s interpretation remains within the enacted banking field and does not transform or eliminate a statutory requirement. Stone’s separate agency-weight position remains noncontrolling.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court unanimously affirmed in an opinion by Justice Scalia. The simulation preserves the unanimous statutory result, but Stone follows his established simulated agency-law position and concurs only in the judgment. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-735.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the statutory text, regulation, lower-court judgment, historical disposition, and authorship. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-735.pdf))

### *Loving v. United States*, 517 U.S. 748

Argued January 9, 1996 | Decided June 3, 1996

From: A general court-martial convicted Army Private Dwight Loving of two murders and related offenses and imposed death after finding aggravating factors contained in Rule for Courts-Martial 1004. The President had prescribed those factors under authority asserted from the Uniform Code of Military Justice. The Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces affirmed. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-748.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether Congress constitutionally authorized the President to prescribe military capital aggravating factors, whether the UCMJ supplied that authorization, and whether the factors lawfully narrowed the class eligible for death.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Tuilaepa v. California / Proctor v. California* requires genuine eligibility narrowing and understandable selection considerations. No prior simulated decision addresses delegation of military capital criteria.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Loving’s convictions and death sentence remain in force. Claims outside the controlled grant concerning evidence, counsel, proportionality, and other capital procedures are not decided.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; O’Connor and Scalia as to Parts I, II, III, IV-B, and IV-C | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for six Justices; core narrowing, statutory-authorization, and delegation holdings for eight |
| Concurrence | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Preserves the possible requirement of a service connection for military capital jurisdiction |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | O’Connor | Supports affirmance | Rejects reliance on English governmental history and describes the arrangement as assignment of executive responsibility rather than a lawful transfer of legislative power |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Thomas | None | Supports affirmance | Relies on military-specific constitutional deference and reserves whether civilian Eighth Amendment narrowing doctrine applies to courts-martial |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Military capital narrowing

Holding: Assuming the civilian capital-narrowing requirements apply to courts-martial, the aggravating factors found in Loving’s case genuinely narrowed death eligibility and possessed understandable culpability-based cores.

Reasoning: The factors identified murder during robbery, Loving’s personal lethal action, and multiple murder. Each confined death eligibility by concrete conduct rather than making every military murder automatically capital. The procedure also preserved individualized consideration rather than compelling death after eligibility.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide whether every civilian capital doctrine applies identically in the military, whether a service connection is constitutionally required for a materially different capital offense, or any penalty issue outside the grant.

Legal basis: The Eighth Amendment, applied on the assumption stated, and the UCMJ.

Authority of the proposition: Kennedy’s opinion is joined on this proposition by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing an eight-Justice majority.

##### Congressional authorization and presidential implementation

Holding: Congress constitutionally authorized the President to prescribe aggravating factors channeling military capital sentencing for offenses Congress made punishable by death.

Operative rule: Congress must make the fundamental legislative choices by defining the offense, authorizing the available punishment, and confining military rulemaking to the enacted system. Within that boundary, the President may prescribe criteria that narrow and implement capital sentencing. The President may not create a new capital offense or authorize a punishment Congress withheld.

Reasoning: Articles 18 and 56 authorize death for specified military offenses, while Article 36 assigns the President responsibility for procedural implementation. Prescribing aggravating factors narrows the punishment Congress authorized rather than enlarging it. The assignment also falls within a military system over which Congress and the President possess express constitutional responsibilities.

Legal basis: Article I, § 8, clause 14; Article II; and UCMJ Articles 18, 36, and 56.

Authority of the proposition: Kennedy’s statutory and delegation analysis in Parts IV-B and IV-C is joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing an eight-Justice majority. The broader historical account of shared military authority is joined by Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing a six-Justice majority with Kennedy.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the bounded military-implementation rule and the conclusion that the specified aggravators lawfully narrowed eligibility and supported individualized selection.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, states that a service-connection requirement may remain necessary in a future military capital case. Scalia, joined by O’Connor, rejects the Court’s extended English-history discussion while agreeing that Congress assigned lawful implementation duties to the President. Thomas would sustain the scheme through military-specific deference without deciding the broader delegation or Eighth Amendment questions. These propositions are noncontrolling except where incorporated into the majority holdings stated above.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Tuilaepa / Proctor* is applied without alteration to the aggravating factors on the assumption that its narrowing requirements govern. The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule allowing bounded presidential implementation of military capital sentencing within offenses and punishments enacted by Congress.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment and opinion topology match the historical decision, with Stone occupying Chief Justice Rehnquist’s complete historical join. The same reservations concerning service connection, English history, delegation terminology, and military-specific capital doctrine remain in the separate writings. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-748.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the court-martial, UCMJ provisions, aggravating factors, historical judgment, partial joins, and separate writings. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-748.pdf))

### *Auciello Iron Works, Inc. v. NLRB*, 517 U.S. 781

Argued April 22, 1996 | Decided June 3, 1996

From: Auciello possessed information causing it to doubt the union’s majority status while its collective-bargaining offer remained open. The union accepted the offer, and Auciello repudiated the agreement the next day based entirely on the previously known information. The First Circuit enforced the Board’s order requiring the company to honor the agreement. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-781.pdf))

Question presented: Whether the Board reasonably treated the repudiation as an unfair labor practice.

Controlling simulated law before decision: No prior simulated holding addresses this sequence. Existing labor law gives the Board interpretive responsibility within the NLRA’s statutory boundaries and recognizes rules supporting stable bargaining relationships.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Board’s order requiring execution and performance of the agreement remains enforceable, subject to ordinary expiration and later lawful bargaining rules.

Justice Souter delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Repudiation based on concealed preacceptance doubt

Holding: The Board reasonably treats repudiation of a newly formed collective-bargaining agreement as an unfair labor practice when the employer’s asserted good-faith doubt rests entirely on facts known before the union accepted the employer’s outstanding offer.

Operative rule: An employer possessing the facts supporting its asserted doubt before contract formation must raise the issue through lawful procedures before acceptance, withdraw the offer, or remain bound after the union accepts. The rule does not govern genuinely new postacceptance evidence.

Reasoning: Permitting repudiation would give the employer a concealed unilateral option. It could leave an offer open, obtain the stabilizing benefit of possible acceptance, and then invoke already known information if the agreement later appeared undesirable. The Board could reasonably conclude that this sequence undermines reliable bargaining and the statutory presumption supporting an accepted agreement.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not govern a new postcontract loss of majority support, fraud in obtaining acceptance, expiration of the agreement, or a timely authorized representation proceeding.

Legal basis: NLRA §§ 8(a)(1), 8(a)(5), and 9(a).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Souter’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision establishes the first controlling simulated rule concerning an employer’s use of concealed preacceptance doubt to repudiate an accepted collective-bargaining agreement. *Town & Country Electric* and *Holly Farms* remain unchanged because they concern employee classification rather than bargaining stability and contract formation.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment, Souter authorship, and preacceptance-knowledge rule match the historical decision. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-781.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the bargaining history, Board rule, lower-court judgment, historical disposition, and authorship. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-781.pdf))

### *United States v. Hale*, 978 F.2d 1016

Decided June 5, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: The Eighth Circuit affirmed Hale’s federal firearms convictions, including convictions for possession of post-1986 machineguns under 18 U.S.C. § 922(o). Hale’s collection included submachineguns, rifles converted to fully automatic fire, and machinegun conversion components. The lower court rejected his Commerce Clause and Second Amendment objections. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F2/978/1016/183710/))

Questions presented: Whether Congress may prohibit purely intrastate possession of post-1986 machineguns as part of its regulation of the interstate firearms market, and whether the Second Amendment protects Hale’s possession.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *United States v. Lopez* permits regulation of an actual economic market and local conduct reasonably necessary to prevent evasion or impairment of that regime, while rejecting generalized reliance on crime costs and productivity. No controlling simulated decision defines the Second Amendment’s individual scope. Two days earlier, *Love v. Pepersack* left that merits question open, although Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas stated a noncontrolling individual-right position.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

The § 922(o) judgment remains in force. Registration, mens rea, evidentiary, confrontation, and other convictions or issues outside the controlled grant are unaffected.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Thomas as to the Second Amendment issue | Supports affirmance | Eight Justices support the Commerce Clause holding; all nine support the Second Amendment disposition |
| Concurrence | Stone | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports the Second Amendment portion of the judgment | Recognizes an individual right centered on common arms for home self-defense while concluding that machineguns fall outside the protected core |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Thomas | None | Supports the Second Amendment disposition; opposes the Commerce Clause holding and overall affirmance | Would invalidate § 922(o) as applied to intrastate possession |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Regulation of the post-1986 civilian machinegun market

Holding: Section 922(o) is within Congress’s commerce authority because it directly closes the post-1986 civilian machinegun transfer market and includes possession as a necessary anti-evasion component of that market regulation.

Operative rule: Congress may include local possession within a valid regulation of a defined interstate product market when possession and transfer are functionally inseparable and excluding possession would permit concealment, retention, or acquisition that defeats the market prohibition. Generalized effects of violent crime, insurance costs, fear, or lost productivity remain insufficient.

Reasoning: Section 922(o) does not create a federal offense for possession of every firearm. It targets a defined product, prohibits post-ban transfer and possession together, and preserves lawfully possessed pre-ban weapons. Requiring the Government to prove a new interstate movement in every prosecution would leave the transfer prohibition readily avoidable through concealed local possession.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not establish a general federal police power over firearms, validate every possession statute, or hold that any object remains federally regulable forever merely because it once crossed a state line. The anti-evasion relationship to a direct market regime must perform real work.

Legal basis: The Commerce Clause and Necessary and Proper Clause.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s opinion is joined on this issue by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing an eight-Justice majority.

##### Second Amendment challenge to a machinegun prohibition

Holding: Section 922(o) does not violate the Second Amendment as applied to Hale’s post-1986 possession of fully automatic machineguns.

Reasoning: *United States v. Miller* does not establish that every weapon having military utility is constitutionally protected. A weapon-specific restriction on fully automatic machineguns leaves ordinary handguns, rifles, and shotguns generally available for lawful defense. The Court therefore need not choose between every competing account of the Amendment’s broader scope.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide the constitutional status of ordinary handguns, public carriage, every automatic-weapon restriction, or every category of arm.

Legal basis: The Second Amendment.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support the as-applied Second Amendment holding. The individual-right rationale stated by Stone and three other Justices does not command a majority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full and filed a concurrence joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

Announced ground: Stone agrees that § 922(o) is a necessary anti-evasion component of direct regulation of the post-1986 machinegun market. He also states that the Second Amendment protects a law-abiding person’s individual right to possess common firearms in the home for self-defense, but that a prohibition confined to fully automatic machineguns does not burden that core.

#### Material Separate Writings

Thomas agrees that the Second Amendment does not invalidate the machinegun prohibition and joins Stone’s broader individual-right analysis. He dissents from the Commerce Clause judgment because intrastate possession is noneconomic conduct, § 922(o) contains no case-specific interstate element, and he would not treat possession as sufficiently necessary to the transfer prohibition. That commerce proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Lopez* is clarified. Its rejection of attenuated aggregate-effects reasoning remains intact, while a direct product-market prohibition may include possession that is reasonably necessary to prevent evasion of the regulated transfer market.

*Love* remains unchanged at the Court level. The individual and incorporated scope of the Second Amendment still lacks a controlling majority rule. Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas repeat their noncontrolling position that common arms for home self-defense fall within an individual right.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court historically denied certiorari, leaving the Eighth Circuit judgment in place without Supreme Court merits precedent. The simulation affirms 8-1 under a narrower direct-market and anti-evasion commerce rationale and unanimously rejects the machinegun-specific Second Amendment claim without adopting the lower court’s collective-right-only reasoning. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F2/978/1016/183710/))

Research sources: The published Eighth Circuit opinion for the weapons, charges, constitutional arguments, and historical judgment; the historical certiorari disposition for the comparator. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F2/978/1016/183710/))

### *Richards v. Jefferson County*, 517 U.S. 793

Argued March 26, 1996 | Decided June 10, 1996

From: The Alabama Supreme Court held that an earlier taxpayer action barred Richards and other taxpayers from challenging Jefferson County’s occupation tax. The petitioners received no notice of the earlier case, exercised no control over it, and were not represented through a certified class or fiduciary relationship. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-793.pdf))

Question presented: Whether applying res judicata in those circumstances violates due process.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein* recognizes that absent persons may be bound by a class judgment only through constitutionally adequate representation and process. No prior simulated holding addresses an ordinary taxpayer suit lacking those safeguards.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Alabama courts shall permit petitioners to litigate claims not independently barred by another lawful rule. The Supreme Court expresses no view on the validity of the occupation tax.

Justice Stevens delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Due-process limits on nonparty preclusion

Holding: A prior taxpayer action cannot bind different taxpayers who received no notice, exercised no control, and were not represented through a certified class, fiduciary relationship, authorized public representation, or comparable legally accountable arrangement.

Operative rule: Shared taxpayer status, a common public issue, and similar litigation interests do not themselves create representation. Binding representation requires a recognized legal relationship, genuinely aligned interests, responsibility to protect the absent person, and procedure adequate to make that responsibility effective. Notice is ordinarily required when practicable.

Reasoning: Allowing any taxpayer to bind every other taxpayer would permit the first litigant to extinguish an entire population’s claims without authority or accountability. The earlier plaintiffs chose their claims and strategy for themselves and had no legal obligation to represent petitioners’ distinct federal objections.

Limits and questions left open: The decision leaves intact ordinary privity, actual control of prior litigation, adequately constituted class actions, fiduciary representation, and suits brought by legally authorized public representatives.

Legal basis: The Fourteenth Amendment’s Due Process Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Stevens’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Matsushita* is applied and clarified. A judgment may bind absent persons only when the rendering process satisfies the constitutional prerequisites of notice and adequate, legally accountable representation. A generalized identity of public interest is insufficient.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment, Stevens authorship, due-process rule, and remand match the historical decision. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-793.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the earlier taxpayer litigation, absence of representation, Alabama judgment, constitutional holding, and historical lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-793.pdf))

### *Whren v. United States*, 517 U.S. 806

Argued April 17, 1996 | Decided June 10, 1996

From: Plainclothes vice officers observed petitioners’ vehicle remain at a stop sign for an unusually long period, turn without signaling, and accelerate at an unreasonable speed. After stopping the vehicle, an officer observed crack cocaine. Petitioners argued that the traffic violations were used as a pretext for a drug investigation. The District of Columbia Circuit affirmed the convictions. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-806.pdf))

Question presented: Whether a traffic stop supported by objective probable cause violates the Fourth Amendment because of the officers’ subjective enforcement motives.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Ornelas v. United States* requires objective legal application to supported historical facts. *United States v. Armstrong* separately governs discovery on a race-selective-prosecution claim and does not convert subjective motive into a Fourth Amendment element.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The convictions remain in force. No equal-protection selective-enforcement claim is adjudicated.

Justice Scalia delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Objective reasonableness of a traffic stop

Holding: A traffic stop supported by objective probable cause that a traffic violation occurred is reasonable under the Fourth Amendment notwithstanding the officers’ subjective interest in investigating another offense.

Operative rule: The constitutional inquiry ordinarily asks whether the facts objectively authorized the seizure actually performed. A court does not suppress evidence because an officer also possessed an unexpressed investigative motive or because another officer might have exercised enforcement discretion differently.

Reasoning: Fourth Amendment reasonableness generally does not turn on subjective intent. Probable cause supplied lawful authority to stop the vehicle, and the stop was not conducted through an unusually harmful method requiring a separate balancing rule. A reasonable-officer test based on ordinary enforcement practice would vary across departments and invite inquiry into internal policy rather than constitutional authority.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not authorize a stop lacking objective legal support, fabricated violations, prolongation beyond the traffic mission without independent justification, or discriminatory enforcement. A claim that officers selected motorists because of race remains governed by equal protection and its own proof and remedial rules.

Legal basis: The Fourth Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Scalia’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s objective-probable-cause rule and its preservation of separately grounded equal-protection claims.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Ornelas* is applied without alteration. That decision governs appellate review of the objective constitutional determination; *Whren* establishes that subjective enforcement purpose ordinarily does not enter the Fourth Amendment merits. *Armstrong* remains a separate equal-protection and discovery rule.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment, Scalia authorship, objective-probable-cause rule, and separation of Fourth Amendment and equal-protection inquiries match the historical decision. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-806.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the traffic observations, suppression posture, objective-motive holding, historical judgment, and unanimous lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-806.pdf))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### *Degen v. United States*, 517 U.S. 820

Argued April 22, 1996 | Decided June 10, 1996

From: The Ninth Circuit affirmed a civil-forfeiture judgment entered after the district court struck Degen’s claims solely because he remained in Switzerland and did not submit to related federal criminal proceedings. The court possessed secure jurisdiction over the properties, and Degen appeared through counsel to contest forfeiture. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-820.pdf))

Question presented: Whether inherent judicial authority permitted complete disentitlement from the civil property action.

Controlling simulated law before decision: No prior simulated holding authorized fugitive disentitlement in a separate civil proceeding. Existing procedural law limited inherent authority to responses reasonably necessary to protect the court’s actual proceedings and judgments. `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

Degen’s answer and claims shall be restored. The district court shall adjudicate the forfeiture action on its lawful merits while retaining authority to manage discovery, protect the criminal prosecution, and impose sanctions tied to actual noncompliance.

Justice Kennedy delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Inherent authority and civil fugitive disentitlement

Holding: A court with secure jurisdiction over the property may not strike a claimant’s filings and enter forfeiture judgment solely because the claimant remains outside the country and beyond the reach of a related criminal prosecution.

Operative rule: An exercise of inherent judicial authority must be a reasonable and proportionate response to an actual problem affecting the proceeding in which the sanction is imposed. Complete loss of the right to defend is unavailable when narrower measures can protect enforcement, evidence, discovery, and the integrity of related litigation.

Reasoning: Degen’s absence did not prevent the court from adjudicating title to the properties or enforcing the eventual in rem judgment. Concerns that civil discovery might reveal criminal-prosecution information could be addressed through protective orders, sequencing, restrictions on proof, or stays. If Degen disobeyed a lawful discovery or evidentiary order, ordinary sanctions remained available. Categorical disentitlement therefore exceeded any demonstrated necessity.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not protect disobedience of a court order, require unrestricted civil discovery, decide ownership or traceability, or resolve whether Congress may enact a constitutionally adequate rule addressing a specifically defined form of fugitive abuse.

Legal basis: The limits of federal inherent judicial authority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s requirement that inherent sanctions remain necessary and fitted to the proceeding they protect.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule governing fugitive disentitlement in a separate civil-forfeiture action. *United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property* is applied for the proposition that forfeiture does not suspend ordinary procedural protection, but its distinct pre-seizure hearing rule is not altered.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment, Kennedy authorship, and rejection of categorical civil disentitlement match the historical decision. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-820.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the record, judgment, historical lineup, narrower available sanctions, and inherent-authority analysis. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-820.pdf))

### *United States v. International Business Machines Corp.*, 517 U.S. 843

Argued March 18, 1996 | Decided June 10, 1996

From: IBM paid the federal tax imposed by 26 U.S.C. § 4371 on premiums paid to foreign insurers for policies covering goods during export transit. The Federal Circuit ordered refunds under the Export Clause and the longstanding rule of *Thames & Mersey Marine Insurance Co. v. United States*. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-843.pdf))

Question presented: Whether the generally applicable insurance-premium tax is a prohibited tax on exports and whether *Thames & Mersey* should be retained.

Participation: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Historical nonparticipation by Justice Stevens does not establish a separate nonparticipation fact in the simulation. All nine seated Justices participate.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Kennedy, and Ginsburg.

The refund judgment remains in force.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Stevens, Ginsburg | Would reverse | Would distinguish a tax on insurance services from a tax laid on the exported goods |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Tax on insurance inseparable from export transit

Holding: Section 4371 may not be applied to premiums paid for insurance covering goods in export transit because that application imposes a tax within the Export Clause’s categorical prohibition.

Reasoning: The tax falls on a transaction whose relevant existence and measure are inseparable from the export movement of the goods. The Clause prohibits a tax or duty on articles exported, and nondiscrimination does not cure a tax that falls within that express prohibition. The Government’s analogies to differently worded dormant Commerce Clause and Import-Export Clause doctrine do not justify abandoning the settled Export Clause rule.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not exempt exporters from income, property, payroll, or other generally applicable taxes imposed on independent events unrelated to export transit. The Court does not decide every possible tax on a service connected more remotely to exports.

Legal basis: Article I, § 9, clause 5.

##### Retention of *Thames & Mersey*

Holding: *Thames & Mersey Marine Insurance Co. v. United States* is retained.

Reasoning: The Government did not establish that the century-old construction was unworkable, textually indefensible, or displaced by later Export Clause precedent. The rule had also become embedded in commercial arrangements concerning export insurance.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Thomas’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone applies the Export Clause’s categorical text and the settled *Thames & Mersey* rule. He announces no additional ground.

#### Material Separate Writings

Kennedy, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg, would hold that the tax falls on insurance services rather than on the goods exported. The dissent would overrule *Thames & Mersey* and sustain the assessment. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated Export Clause rule. A tax inseparably incident to the export transit of goods remains forbidden even when generally applicable and nondiscriminatory; taxes on legally independent events remain outside the holding.

#### Historical Comparison

Historically, the Court affirmed 6-2. Thomas wrote for the Chief Justice, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, and Breyer; Kennedy dissented with Ginsburg, and Stevens did not participate. In the simulation, Stone occupies the Chief Justice’s majority position, Stevens participates and joins the dissent, and the judgment remains 6-3. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-843.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the tax, Export Clause question, treatment of *Thames & Mersey*, historical participation, and opinion topology. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-843.pdf))

### *Lockheed Corp. v. Spink*, 517 U.S. 882

Argued April 22, 1996 | Decided June 10, 1996

From: Lockheed amended its pension plan to offer additional early-retirement benefits to employees who released employment-related claims. Spink rejected the offer and also sought credit for service completed while he was lawfully excluded from the plan before the effective date of 1986 statutory amendments. The Ninth Circuit reinstated his prohibited-transaction theory and required earlier service credit. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-882.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether Lockheed acted as an ERISA fiduciary in adopting the amendment; whether the release arrangement was a prohibited transaction; and whether the 1986 amendments required retroactive service credit.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust* makes fiduciary status functional and attaches it only to the extent a person exercises authority over plan assets or administration. Earlier the same term, *Varity Corp. v. Howe* applied that rule to plan-administration communications, not to plan design. `HOLDINGS.md` `OT_1995CHUNK3.md`

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

The § 406 claim based on adoption of the amendment and the demand for pre-effective-date service credit shall be dismissed. Any independently preserved claim concerning unlawful waiver, misrepresentation, coercion, or administration contrary to plan terms remains for ordinary treatment.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Ginsburg in full; Souter and Breyer except Part III-B | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion for seven Justices; all except the release-transaction conclusion for Souter and Breyer |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | Souter | Supports the judgment but disputes the release ruling | Would leave open whether payment of plan benefits for releases could constitute a prohibited transaction on a more developed theory |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Plan amendment as settlor conduct

Holding: An employer does not act as an ERISA fiduciary when deciding whether and how to amend a pension plan.

Operative rule: Fiduciary status follows the function performed. Plan design and amendment are settlor functions; implementation and administration of the adopted plan may separately involve fiduciary duties.

Reasoning: ERISA’s functional definition does not make every employer decision affecting benefits an act of plan administration. Lockheed and its directors were designing the terms on which additional benefits would be offered, not exercising fiduciary authority over an existing entitlement.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support the settlor-function holding.

##### Release-conditioned benefits

Holding: Payment of additional benefits under the amended plan in exchange for an employee’s release of employment-related claims does not state a prohibited-transaction claim under § 406(a)(1)(D) on the pleaded facts.

Reasoning: The participant remained free to reject the enhancement and retain the claims. The pleaded arrangement did not establish that a fiduciary transferred plan assets for the employer’s benefit in violation of § 406. A release procured through fraud, coercion, unlawful waiver, or administration inconsistent with plan terms would present a different question.

Authority of the proposition: Thomas’s opinion is joined on this issue by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Ginsburg, producing a seven-Justice majority.

##### Temporal reach of the 1986 amendments

Holding: The 1986 amendments do not require Lockheed to credit service completed during Spink’s lawful pre-effective-date exclusion from the plan.

Reasoning: Retroactive service credit would attach a new benefit obligation to completed conduct. Congress supplied effective dates but no clear command requiring plans to reconstruct prior benefit accrual. The ordinary rule against attaching new legal consequences therefore controls.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support the temporal holding.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Thomas’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the functional separation between plan design and fiduciary administration, the rejection of the pleaded § 406 theory, and the prospective application of the 1986 amendments.

#### Material Separate Writings

Breyer, joined by Souter, would not decide the release issue categorically. The separate opinion agrees that adoption of the amendment was settlor conduct and that the statutory amendments do not require retroactive service credit.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*John Hancock* is applied and clarified: functional fiduciary status does not attach to plan-sponsor amendment decisions. *Varity* is distinguished because Varity exercised plan-administration authority when communicating deceptively with beneficiaries. *Landgraf* and *Rivers* are applied without alteration to the service-credit question.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimous judgment, Thomas authorship, partial joins, and Breyer-Souter separate writing match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the Chief Justice’s complete historical join. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-882.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the plan amendment, release arrangement, statutory effective dates, judgment, and partial opinion joins. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-882.pdf))

### *United States v. Myers*, 46 F.3d 668

Decided June 11, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: Police used an Agema 210 thermal imager from outside Myers’s residence to detect unusual heat patterns associated with indoor grow lights. The warrantless scan and other evidence were then used to obtain a search warrant. Myers entered a conditional guilty plea after the district court denied suppression, and the Seventh Circuit held that the scan was not a search. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-7th-circuit/1353634.html))

Question presented: Whether warrantless use of thermal-imaging technology to infer activity inside a home is a Fourth Amendment search.

Controlling simulated law before decision: Simulated *United States v. Pinson* holds that police conduct a search when they use sense-enhancing technology outside a home to obtain information about interior activity unavailable through ordinary public observation. *Ornelas v. United States* governs appellate review of probable cause but does not alter that threshold rule. `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `OT_1995CHUNK6.md`

#### Judgment

Reversed, conviction vacated, and case remanded, 6-3.

Supporting: Stone, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, O’Connor, and Kennedy.

The district court shall treat the thermal scan as a warrantless search and determine whether the warrant and resulting evidence survive after proper treatment of the scan under independent-source, attenuation, or objectively reasonable reliance principles. If suppression is required, Myers may obtain the relief governing his conditional plea.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal, vacatur, and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | O’Connor, Kennedy | Would affirm | Would classify the device as observing heat exposed outside the home rather than obtaining constitutionally protected interior information |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Technological acquisition of information from inside a home

Holding: The warrantless Agema 210 scan was a search because officers deliberately used sense-enhancing technology to obtain information about activity inside Myers’s home that ordinary public observation could not otherwise acquire.

Reasoning: The constitutional question turns on the information police deliberately obtained, not on whether the instrument emitted energy, physically penetrated the wall, or detected a signal labeled waste heat. Officers used a patterned thermal image to infer operation of grow lights behind the walls. The home’s constitutional protection cannot depend on the physical carrier through which otherwise unavailable interior information becomes perceptible.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not govern ordinary visual observation from a lawful vantage, perceptible smoke or odor, utility records obtained through lawful process, or technology that reveals no information about interior activity. The Court does not decide suppression, good faith, or the remaining warrant’s sufficiency.

Legal basis: The Fourth Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse, vacate the conviction, and remand.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone applies the existing simulated rule protecting otherwise unavailable information about activity inside a home from warrantless technological acquisition.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens, joined by O’Connor and Kennedy, would treat the emitted heat as exposed to public detection. The dissent would distinguish technology that merely depicts exterior heat from a device that penetrates walls or produces a detailed image of interior activity. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Simulated *United States v. Pinson* is applied without alteration. The decision confirms that the search inquiry concerns the deliberate acquisition of otherwise unavailable interior information, not whether the device physically penetrates the structure. *Ornelas* remains the separate rule governing appellate review of a later probable-cause determination.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court did not historically decide Myers’s case. The Seventh Circuit historically affirmed, joining other circuits that treated thermal imaging of emitted heat as outside the Fourth Amendment. The simulation reverses because the earlier controlling simulated decision in *Pinson* displaced that premise. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-7th-circuit/1353634.html))

Research sources: The published Seventh Circuit opinion for the corrected docket, thermal-imaging procedure, warrant sequence, conditional plea, and historical judgment. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-7th-circuit/1353634.html))

### *Shaw v. Hunt; Pope v. Hunt*, 517 U.S. 899

Argued December 5, 1995 | Decided June 13, 1996

From: A three-judge district court upheld North Carolina’s Twelfth Congressional District after finding that race predominated but concluding that voting-rights interests justified the district. The district connected separated population concentrations through a narrow interstate corridor and substantially departed from ordinary political geography. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-899.pdf))

Question presented: Whether District 12 survives the district-specific predominance and remedial-classification sequence established by simulated *Miller*.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *United States v. Hays* requires residence in the challenged district or another concrete personal classification. Simulated *Miller* asks first whether race materially subordinated traditional race-neutral districting principles, then classifies a race-predominant district as a genuinely fitted district-specific remedy or unsupported racial sorting before assigning the corresponding scrutiny. `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

| Docket | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| *Shaw v. Hunt*, No. 94-923 | Vacated and remanded, 5-4 | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | District 12 may not be used in another election unless sustained under the controlling sequence. |
| *Pope v. Hunt*, No. 94-924 | Vacated and remanded, 5-4 | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | The same district-specific findings and remedial limitation govern. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would reverse outright | Would apply strict scrutiny directly and hold District 12 insufficiently justified |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Standing and racial predominance

Holding: Residents of District 12 have standing, and race predominated in the district’s design. A nonresident lacking another concrete personal classification may not challenge the district.

Reasoning: The boundary choices, racial targets, connectors, and departures from ordinary political geography show that race materially controlled the design. Shape is probative evidence rather than an independent constitutional element.

##### Remedial classification and scrutiny

Holding: The district court used the wrong legal sequence by moving from racial predominance directly to a generalized justification. It must determine whether District 12 is a genuinely fitted, district-specific remedy for a demonstrated constitutional or Voting Rights Act violation or instead constitutes unsupported racial sorting, then apply the corresponding scrutiny under simulated *Miller*.

Operative rule: A remedial classification requires an actual district-specific injury, lawful corrective authority or obligation, and substantial fit between that injury and the racial design. Unsupported maximization, unnecessary packing, racial separation, or reliance on a remedial label without those findings receives strict scrutiny.

Reasoning: The record contains evidence concerning compactness, bloc voting, prior exclusion, alternative designs, and remedial fit. The lower court did not make the findings needed to place the district in either controlling lane, and the Supreme Court may not supply them through appellate inference.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not determine District 12’s ultimate validity, approve maximization, presume Voting Rights Act compliance from invocation alone, or direct a replacement map.

Legal basis: The Equal Protection Clause and the Voting Rights Act as relevant to the asserted remedial predicate.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand in both dockets.

Public role: Joined Souter’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s district-specific predominance, remedial-classification, and scrutiny sequence.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, would reverse outright. The dissent concludes that the record already establishes unsupported racial sorting and that the district cannot survive strict scrutiny.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Hays* and simulated *Miller* are applied without alteration. The decision confirms that a lower court may not omit the classification step that determines whether remedial or strict scrutiny governs a race-predominant district.

#### Historical Comparison

Historically, the Court reversed 5-4 and held District 12 invalid under strict scrutiny. The simulation instead vacates and remands 5-4 because the simulated *Miller* framework requires district-specific remedial classification before final scrutiny and judgment. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-899.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the district’s design, asserted justifications, lower-court judgment, historical 5-4 lineup, and separate writings. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-899.pdf))

### *Bush v. Vera; Lawson v. Vera; United States v. Vera*, 517 U.S. 952

Argued December 5, 1995 | Decided June 13, 1996

From: A three-judge district court invalidated Texas Congressional Districts 18, 29, and 30 after finding that race predominated in all three. The record reflected detailed racial mapping, minority-opportunity objectives, incumbency protection, and materially different district-specific boundary choices. Plaintiff Chen resided outside the challenged districts. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-952.pdf))

Question presented: Whether the judgment invalidating all three districts may stand under simulated *Miller*’s district-specific predominance and remedial-classification sequence.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Chen’s nonresident claim | Dismissal affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | Chen lacks standing absent another concrete personal classification. |
| District 30 | Judgment invalidating the district reversed, 5-4 | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | District 30 remains operative because racial predominance was not established. |
| Districts 18 and 29 | Judgments vacated and cases remanded, 5-4 | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | The three-judge court shall make separate remedial-predicate, fit, and scrutiny findings for each district. |

Overall disposition: Affirmed in part, reversed in part, vacated in part, and remanded.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports the complete judgment | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm invalidation of all three districts | Would find racial predominance and failure of strict scrutiny throughout |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Standing

Holding: A voter residing outside a challenged district lacks standing absent another concrete personal classification. Residents of Districts 18, 29, and 30 may challenge the districts in which they reside.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support the standing disposition.

##### District-specific racial predominance

Holding: The record establishes that race predominated in Districts 18 and 29 but does not establish racial predominance in District 30.

Reasoning: District 30’s boundaries can be explained by ordinary political geography, incumbency objectives, communities, and the effort to create a Dallas district without proof that race displaced those considerations. Districts 18 and 29 present materially different records, including racial targets, irregular connectors, and departures from subdivisions and communities that race more directly explains.

Limits and questions left open: Compactness is not an independent constitutional requirement, use of racial data is not itself forbidden, and minority electoral opportunity does not alone establish predominance.

Authority of the proposition: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer support the district-specific predominance conclusions.

##### Remedial classification of Districts 18 and 29

Holding: Districts 18 and 29 must be separately classified under simulated *Miller* before final constitutional judgment.

Operative rule: Texas bears the burden of identifying the actual district-specific constitutional or statutory injury, its corrective authority or obligation, and the fit of the racial design. A generalized desire to achieve racial targets or preserve percentages does not establish a fitted Voting Rights Act remedy.

Reasoning: Texas could lawfully examine bloc voting and minority electoral exclusion, but it could not treat the Voting Rights Act as general authorization for racial maximization. The existing record contains evidence relevant to both controlling classifications but lacks the complete findings required for final judgment.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not hold Districts 18 or 29 valid, require maximization, or immunize a remedial label from examination of the districts’ actual boundaries and operation.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm dismissal of Chen’s claim; reverse as to District 30; vacate and remand as to Districts 18 and 29.

Public role: Joined Stevens’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s district-specific standing, predominance, and remedial-classification analysis.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, would affirm the district court’s invalidation of all three districts. The dissent would find that race predominated in District 30 as well as Districts 18 and 29 and that none was narrowly tailored.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Hays* is applied to Chen’s nonresident claim. Simulated *Miller* is applied and clarified only in operation: predominance and remedial classification must be performed separately for each district, even when the districts form part of the same statewide plan.

#### Historical Comparison

Historically, a fractured 5-4 Court affirmed invalidation of all three districts. The simulation reverses as to District 30 and remands Districts 18 and 29 because Stone and the four historical dissenters apply the different controlling simulated *Miller* framework. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-952.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for standing, the three district records, the historical fractured judgment, and the competing predominance analyses. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-517/pdf/USREPORTS-517-952.pdf))
