### *Jaffee v. Redmond*, 518 U.S. 1

Argued February 26, 1996 | Decided June 13, 1996

From: After shooting Ricky Allen while responding to a disturbance, police officer Mary Lu Redmond received counseling from a licensed clinical social worker. In the resulting federal civil action, the district court compelled disclosure of treatment communications and notes and instructed the jury that nondisclosure could support an adverse inference. The Seventh Circuit reversed. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-1.pdf))

Question presented: Whether federal common law under Evidence Rule 501 recognizes a psychotherapist-patient privilege extending to licensed clinical social workers and whether the privilege is subject to case-specific evidentiary balancing.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia.

The Seventh Circuit’s remand for proceedings unaffected by the compelled privileged material remains in force.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | None | Would reverse | Would decline to create the privilege through federal common law |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Psychotherapist-patient privilege

Holding: Rule 501 protects confidential communications made for diagnosis or treatment to a licensed psychiatrist, psychologist, or clinical social worker, including treatment notes reflecting those communications.

Operative rule: The privilege belongs to the patient and is not overcome by ad hoc judicial balancing of the evidentiary need asserted in a particular case. Waiver and any recognized exception remain separate inquiries.

Reasoning: Effective psychotherapy depends on the patient’s justified confidence that therapeutic disclosures will remain private. A case-by-case balancing regime would defeat the predictability necessary for candid treatment and generate intrusive collateral litigation. Licensed clinical social workers perform the same confidentiality-dependent therapeutic function and provide a substantial portion of mental-health care, so excluding them would make the privilege arbitrary in operation.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not define waiver, communications made for a nontherapeutic forensic purpose, threats of imminent harm, or other possible exceptions not presented by the record.

Legal basis: Federal Rule of Evidence 501 and federal common law.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Stevens’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s categorical recognition of the privilege and rejection of case-specific balancing.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated psychotherapist-patient privilege under Rule 501. Its categorical protection includes licensed clinical social workers but leaves the privilege’s unpresented exceptions and waiver rules unresolved.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court affirmed 7-2. Stevens wrote for O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer; Scalia dissented, and Chief Justice Rehnquist joined one part of that dissent. Stone instead joins the Court’s opinion, producing an 8-1 simulated judgment. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-1.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the counseling relationship, compelled evidence, Rule 501 analysis, historical judgment, and opinion joins. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-1.pdf))

### Adjudication Paused: *Montana v. Egelhoff*, 518 U.S. 37

Scheduled decision date: June 13, 1996

The controlled Section II requires reversal and remand so that the Montana courts may authoritatively determine whether § 45-2-203 substantively redefined culpability or merely excluded evidence relevant to the retained elements of purpose and knowledge. It further directs a new trial if Montana retained those elements while excluding competent intoxication evidence. `OT_1995CHUNK7.md`

That antecedent classification was already authoritatively resolved by the Montana Supreme Court. The judgment under review treated deliberate homicide as continuing to require purposeful or knowing conduct, held the intoxication evidence clearly relevant to those elements, and invalidated the statute because it prevented the jury from considering that evidence. The United States Reports identify that construction and the actual jury instruction as the posture reviewed by the Supreme Court. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-37.pdf))

The verified state-law posture therefore activates Stone’s conditional new-trial branch but conflicts with his separately controlled formal disposition requiring remand for classification. Choosing between those controlled components would alter Stone’s judgment and remedy. Under the Engine’s source-conflict and revalidation rules, the matter is paused. No simulated judgment, vote, opinion, holding, or precedent is entered. The case remains at the post-argument merits stage pending a revised and expressly approved Section II. `SUPREME_COURT_TERM_SIMULATOR_ENGINE.md`

### *Koon v. United States; Powell v. United States*, 518 U.S. 81

Argued February 20, 1996 | Decided June 13, 1996

From: Koon and Powell were convicted under 18 U.S.C. § 242 for criminally excessive force against Rodney King. The district court departed downward based on several considerations, including King’s conduct, career consequences, low recidivism, susceptibility to abuse in prison, successive state and federal prosecutions, and the combination of circumstances. The Ninth Circuit vacated the sentences after applying a substantially more restrictive approach. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-81.pdf))

Questions presented: What appellate standard governs Guidelines departures, which stated factors were legally available, and whether the sentences could stand after removal of impermissible considerations.

Controlling simulated law before decision: No prior simulated holding fixed the appellate standard for Guidelines departures. Earlier the same term, *Ornelas* separated clear-error review of historical facts from independent review of a constitutional characterization, but 18 U.S.C. § 3742 separately directs due deference to sentencing judgments. `OT_1995CHUNK6.md`

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

Both sentences remain vacated. The district court shall resentence Koon and Powell without relying on career loss or ordinary low recidivism. The Court’s majority permits reconsideration of victim misconduct, susceptibility to abuse in prison, and successive prosecution within the boundaries stated below.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas in full; Stevens except Part IV-B-1; Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer except Part IV-B-3; Stone except the victim-misconduct application and Part IV-B-3 | Supports the complete judgment | Joins vary by departure factor |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone | None | Supports remand but disputes three applications | Would reject victim misconduct, prison susceptibility, and successive prosecution on this record |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | None | Supports remand | Would permit consideration of the unusual employment consequences |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Souter | Ginsburg | Supports remand | Would reject prison susceptibility and successive prosecution |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | Ginsburg | Supports remand | Would reject the same two factors on record and Guidelines grounds |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Appellate review of Guidelines departures

Holding: A district court’s decision to depart from the Sentencing Guidelines is reviewed for abuse of discretion.

Operative rule: Whether a factor is categorically forbidden, encouraged, discouraged, already accounted for, or unmentioned is a legal question subject to independent review. Historical findings receive clear-error review. Whether a legally available factor exists to an exceptional degree and places the case outside the applicable heartland is reviewed for abuse of discretion.

Reasoning: Section 3742 requires due deference, and district judges possess comparative experience with the ordinary run of Guidelines cases. Deference does not protect a sentence resting on legal error, a forbidden consideration, or an unsupported factual premise.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support the standard-of-review holding.

##### Victim misconduct

Holding: Victim misconduct was a legally available departure ground, and the district court did not abuse its discretion in treating King’s conduct as materially contributing to the offense behavior.

Reasoning: Section 5K2.10 identifies victim conduct as an encouraged consideration. The majority reads the record as supporting a sufficiently direct relationship between King’s resistance and the ensuing use of force, while requiring the sentencing court to avoid double counting conduct already incorporated into the offense level.

Authority of the proposition: Kennedy’s opinion is joined on this application by Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing an eight-Justice majority. Stone dissents from the application.

##### Career consequences and recidivism

Holding: Ordinary loss of employment and career resulting from convictions for abuse of official authority does not support departure here. The defendants’ asserted low likelihood of recidivism was already addressed by the criminal-history provisions and was not shown to an exceptional degree.

Reasoning: Career consequences ordinarily accompany conviction and cannot become a ground for reducing punishment merely because the office lost was the position abused. The Guidelines already account for ordinary recidivism differences through criminal-history categories.

Authority of the proposition: Eight Justices, all except Stevens, support the career-consequences holding. All nine support the recidivism holding.

##### Prison susceptibility and successive prosecution

Holding: On this record, the district court did not abuse its discretion in considering the defendants’ unusual susceptibility to abuse in prison and the extraordinary burden associated with successive state and federal prosecutions.

Reasoning: The majority treats both considerations as unmentioned factors not categorically prohibited by the Commission. Their availability remains confined to circumstances genuinely outside the ordinary heartland and cannot rest on official status, notoriety, or the existence of two lawful proceedings alone.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not make every successive prosecution mitigating or permit routine departure because a law-enforcement officer faces ordinary prison risks.

Authority of the proposition: Kennedy’s opinion is joined on this application by Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas, producing a five-Justice majority. Stone, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer oppose it.

##### Resentencing after mixed departure grounds

Holding: Resentencing is required because legally invalid factors contributed to the integrated departure and the record does not establish that the district court would have imposed the same sentences using only permissible considerations.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support remand for resentencing.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm in part, reverse in part, vacate both sentences, and remand.

Public role: Joined Kennedy’s opinion except its victim-misconduct application and its treatment of prison susceptibility and successive prosecution; filed a concurrence in part and dissent in part.

Announced ground: Stone agrees that departure decisions receive abuse-of-discretion review while categorical legal limits remain independently enforceable. He would hold that King’s earlier conduct did not materially provoke the criminal excessive force after the officers no longer faced resistance or danger. He would also reject prison risk based on police status or notoriety and the ordinary burden of a lawful successive § 242 prosecution on this record.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens would allow consideration of the unusual collateral employment consequences. Souter, joined by Ginsburg, and Breyer, joined by Ginsburg, would reject the prison-susceptibility and successive-prosecution grounds. Those propositions are noncontrolling except where Stone’s separate vote joins the opposition without creating a majority.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule for appellate review of Guidelines departures. *Ornelas* is distinguished: its independent constitutional review does not displace § 3742’s express due-deference command for sentencing judgments. Legal exclusions remain independently reviewable, historical facts receive clear-error review, and heartland application receives abuse-of-discretion review.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical judgment was also affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded. Kennedy’s opinion was unanimous except for Stevens’s disagreement on career consequences and the Souter-Ginsburg-Breyer disagreement on prison susceptibility and successive prosecution. In the simulation, Stone replaces the Chief Justice’s historical full join but dissents additionally from the victim-misconduct application and from Part IV-B-3, leaving those propositions supported by eight and five Justices respectively. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-81.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the departure factors, statutory review standard, historical partial joins, separate writings, and remand. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-81.pdf))

# OCTOBER TERM 1995, CHUNK 8 OF 10

The ordinary nine-member OT1995 Court participates throughout. The cumulative term state includes completed Chunks 1 through 7; the coordinated June 21 family decisions are treated together, while the June 20 and June 24 decisions otherwise use shared same-date entering-law baselines. `OT_1995CHUNK8.md` `SUPREME_COURT_TERM_SIMULATOR_ENGINE.md` `JUDICIAL_TURN_OUTPUT_RENDER_CONTRACT.md` `Supreme Court Composition — Simulation (OT1993–OT2026).txt` `STANDING_STATE.md` `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

Verified corrections: *Leavitt* was decided through a grant and summary reversal on the certiorari petition rather than after plenary argument. The Second Circuit decided *SSC Corp.* on September 19, 1995. In No. 95-345, the Sixth Circuit reversed Ursery’s criminal conviction after the earlier forfeiture settlement; it did not invalidate a later forfeiture judgment. The historical *Gasperini* judgment was 5-4, not 6-3. None of those corrections changes Stone’s controlled vote, legal position, or remedy. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/137/))

*Gray v. Netherland* presents a separate dispositive conflict. The approved brief treats the Brady claim’s preservation, default, cause, and prejudice as unresolved and directs remand for their determination. The official record establishes that Gray knew the Brady facts when he filed his first state habeas petition, that Virginia law barred later review, and that he made no cause-or-prejudice showing. Choosing between those premises would alter Stone’s controlled judgment and remand. The matter is therefore paused below. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/152/))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Summary Merits Disposition: *Leavitt v. Jane L.*, 518 U.S. 137

Decided June 17, 1996

From: The Tenth Circuit invalidated Utah’s post-20-week abortion provision as inseverable from an unconstitutional pre-20-week provision, despite an express statutory instruction that each provision was independently enacted. It did not decide the surviving provision’s federal constitutionality. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/137/))

Question presented: Whether Utah Code § 76-7-317 required the post-20-week provision to be treated as severable.

#### Judgment

Certiorari granted as to severability; judgment reversed and case remanded, 5-4.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Tenth Circuit shall treat § 76-7-302(3) as severed and resolve preserved constitutional challenges under controlling law. No enforcement contrary to an existing injunction may occur before lawful review is completed.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Per curiam Opinion of the Court | The Court | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports summary reversal | Severability only |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Opposes the grant and summary reversal | Would adhere to the Court’s ordinary practice of declining certiorari solely to correct a federal court of appeals’ state-law ruling |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Effect of an express severability command

Holding: Sections 302(2) and 302(3) are severable because Utah expressly directed that every provision be treated as independently enacted and the post-20-week provision remains complete and operational without the invalid earlier-term provision.

Operative rule: A federal court must give effect to an express state severability command when the surviving text can function independently and severance neither rewrites the provision nor creates a statutory scheme the legislature did not enact. Judicial speculation about the legislature’s preferred comprehensive bargain cannot override its enacted instruction.

Reasoning: The two subsections govern distinct gestational periods and contain separate operative commands. Section 317 eliminates the need to infer whether the legislature would have enacted the later-term provision alone. The Tenth Circuit’s contrary prediction directly displaced the State’s enacted rule of construction.

Limits and questions left open: The Court expresses no view on § 302(3)’s emergency provisions, gestational operation, undue-burden consequences, facial validity, or enforceability in any particular application.

Legal basis: Utah severability law and federal remedial restraint.

Authority of the proposition: The five-Justice per curiam coalition necessarily decided severability. Because the disposition is summary, its precedential force is confined to an express clause and a remainder that is textually and operationally independent.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Grant, reverse, and remand.

Public role: Joined the per curiam Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s enforcement of Utah’s express severability instruction and its reservation of the surviving provision’s constitutional merits.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule for an express state severability command. The abortion standards established in simulated *Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer* and applied earlier this term in *Planned Parenthood v. Miller* remain unchanged and govern only the constitutional review reserved for remand.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated summary judgment, 5-4 lineup, per curiam form, and Stevens dissent joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer match the historical disposition, with Stone occupying the Chief Justice’s historical position. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/137/))

Research sources: The official report for the statutory provisions, summary posture, judgment, and historical dissent. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/137/))

### *SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp.*, 66 F.3d 502

Decided June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: Smithtown used two distinct mechanisms to direct waste to a privately owned incinerator. Its ordinance required all residential and commercial waste generated in the town to be delivered there and imposed criminal sanctions. Its separate residential contracts paid selected haulers to provide municipal collection and disposal services and specified the same facility as part of the purchased performance. The Second Circuit invalidated the ordinance but sustained the contracts. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/66/502/487014/))

Questions presented: Whether the ordinance constitutes discriminatory market regulation, and whether the contractual routing term constitutes municipal market participation.

Controlling simulated law before decision: Simulated *C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown* invalidates a law whose practical operation reserves a waste-processing market for a favored local private enterprise unless reasonable nondiscriminatory alternatives cannot serve the legitimate local objective. It reserved materially different public-facility and governmental-market circumstances.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Criminally enforceable flow-control ordinance | Second Circuit affirmed, 8-1 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Souter | The ordinance may not be enforced. |
| Residential collection and disposal contracts | Second Circuit affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The contracts remain enforceable to the extent they govern the service Smithtown purchases. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Souter as to the contract holding | Supports both judgment components | Complete opinion for eight Justices; market-participation portion for all nine |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Souter | None | Supports the contract judgment; opposes invalidation of the ordinance | Would treat Smithtown’s integrated financing and waste-management system as a permissible local public-service arrangement |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Criminally enforceable routing to a private facility

Holding: Smithtown regulates rather than participates when it uses criminal law to require every private generator and hauler to patronize a designated private incinerator. The ordinance discriminates in operation against interstate commerce and is invalid.

Operative rule: Governmental financial exposure, planning responsibility, or contractual access to a facility does not convert a coercive legal command into a market purchase. A routing rule backed by fines or imprisonment and applicable to persons outside governmental contractual privity remains regulation.

Reasoning: The ordinance removes private disposal choices and secures tipping revenue for one favored local processor. Out-of-state and disfavored in-state processors are excluded by law rather than by Smithtown’s decision about how to spend municipal funds. Uniform fees, taxation, direct municipal purchasing, and competitively awarded service contracts provide nondiscriminatory means of financing disposal and assuring a waste stream.

Limits and questions left open: The holding does not govern a genuinely public facility operating under distinct public-service circumstances, an evenhanded health regulation, or a municipality’s own purchase of disposal capacity.

Legal basis: The dormant Commerce Clause.

Authority of the proposition: Kennedy’s opinion and O’Connor’s complete join supply eight votes for the operative discrimination and regulator-participant propositions.

##### Routing within a municipally purchased service

Holding: Smithtown acts as a market participant when it competitively purchases integrated residential collection and disposal and requires its contractors to use the designated facility in performing that purchased service.

Operative rule: A government buyer may define the suppliers, route, facility, and performance it will purchase within the transaction. Market-participant protection ends when a contractual condition controls independent transactions, noncontracting actors, commercial waste outside the purchased service, or downstream commerce in which government is not participating.

Reasoning: Smithtown pays the haulers and obtains collection and disposal for the residents of the improvement districts. The routing term identifies the service the contractors must deliver to their municipal customer. It does not use criminal law against noncontracting generators or dictate what contractors must do in unrelated transactions.

Legal basis: The dormant Commerce Clause’s market-participant doctrine.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support the contract holding.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm in both cross-appeals.

Public role: Joined Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the source-based distinction between legal coercion imposed on private commerce and contractual terms defining a service purchased by the municipality.

#### Material Separate Writings

Souter agrees that the residential contracts fall within market participation. He would sustain the ordinance as part of Smithtown’s integrated public waste system and would treat the private operator’s role as subordinate to the municipality’s financing and service responsibilities. That ordinance proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Simulated *Carbone* is applied and clarified. Its private-enterprise discrimination rule governs Smithtown’s ordinance, while its reservation of genuinely public and governmental-market arrangements remains intact. The decision adds a recurring boundary between contractual participation in a purchased service and coercive regulation of commerce outside contractual privity.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court historically denied certiorari, leaving the Second Circuit’s mixed judgment in force without Supreme Court precedent. The simulation affirms that judgment and makes the ordinance-contract distinction controlling federal law. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/66/502/487014/))

Research sources: The published Second Circuit opinion for the ordinance, criminal sanctions, contractual arrangement, corrected decision date, and historical judgment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/66/502/487014/))

### Adjudication Paused: *Gray v. Netherland*, 518 U.S. 152

Scheduled decision date: June 20, 1996

Stone’s controlled Section II directs affirmance on the new notice-only theory but vacatur and remand on both the Brady suppression claim and the affirmative-deception claim. It treats preservation, exhaustion, default, cause, prejudice, and materiality for the suppression claim as unresolved questions for the Fourth Circuit. `OT_1995CHUNK8.md`

The official record fixes a materially different threshold posture. It states that Gray knew the factual basis for his Brady claim when he filed his first state habeas petition, that Virginia’s successive-petition rule barred later state review, and that Gray made no attempt to establish cause or prejudice. The historical Court consequently held the Brady claim procedurally defaulted and remanded only the distinct misrepresentation claim. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/152/))

That verified posture directly conflicts with Stone’s controlled remand of the Brady issue. Resolving the conflict would require changing Stone’s judgment component, threshold position, and remedial instruction. No simulated judgment, vote, opinion, holding, or precedent is entered. The case remains at the post-argument merits stage pending a revised and expressly approved Section II.

### *Lane v. Peña*, 518 U.S. 187

Argued April 15, 1996 | Decided June 20, 1996

From: The United States Merchant Marine Academy separated Lane after he developed insulin-dependent diabetes. The district court found unlawful disability discrimination and ordered reinstatement, but the lower courts denied compensatory damages on sovereign-immunity grounds. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/187/))

Question presented: Whether Congress unequivocally waived federal sovereign immunity from compensatory damages for a violation of Rehabilitation Act § 504(a) in a federally conducted program.

Controlling simulated law before decision: Simulated *FDIC v. Meyer* keeps the substantive right, sovereign-immunity waiver, cause of action, defendant, and remedy analytically separate.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg. Opposing: Stevens and Breyer.

The denial of compensatory damages remains in force. Lane’s reinstatement and any otherwise authorized prospective or equitable relief are unaffected.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Breyer | Would reverse and permit compensatory relief | Reads the remedial cross-references as sufficiently clear when considered with § 504’s application to federal programs |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Federal immunity from compensatory damages

Holding: Congress did not unequivocally waive the Federal Government’s sovereign immunity from compensatory damages for a § 504(a) violation committed in a federally conducted program.

Operative rule: A substantive statutory command applicable to a federal agency does not authorize a money judgment against the United States unless enacted text expresses that consent unambiguously. Remedial cross-references or plausible structural inferences cannot supply a waiver when the statutory language supports competing readings.

Reasoning: Section 2000d-7 expressly addresses state sovereign immunity and identifies remedies in suits against States. It does not comparably identify federal agencies as consenting defendants for compensatory damages. Congress’s ability to speak expressly elsewhere makes the ambiguity in the federal-program provisions decisive.

Limits and questions left open: The holding does not diminish § 504’s substantive prohibition, disturb Lane’s reinstatement, foreclose prospective relief, or determine damages against a nonfederal recipient under a separately authorized action.

Legal basis: Federal sovereign immunity, 29 U.S.C. § 794, and 42 U.S.C. § 2000d-7.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s separation of the proved statutory violation from the distinct requirement of unmistakable federal consent to a compensatory money judgment.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens, joined by Breyer, would read the statutory remedy provisions together as placing federal agencies and federally assisted programs under equivalent compensatory consequences. That waiver proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*FDIC v. Meyer* is applied and clarified. Waiver of immunity does not follow from the existence of a right or an available cause of action, and a right applicable to the Federal Government does not itself authorize payment from the Treasury.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated 7-2 judgment, O’Connor authorship, complete majority lineup, and Stevens-Breyer dissent match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the Chief Justice’s historical join. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/187/))

Research sources: The official report for Lane’s separation, reinstatement, statutory question, judgment, and historical opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/187/))

### *Brown v. Pro Football, Inc.*, 518 U.S. 231

Argued March 27, 1996 | Decided June 20, 1996

From: NFL clubs bargained through an established multiemployer unit with the players’ union over developmental-squad employment terms. After impasse, the clubs jointly implemented the final proposed weekly salary. The D.C. Circuit held the conduct protected by the nonstatutory labor exemption and reversed an antitrust judgment for the players. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/231/))

Question presented: Whether the exemption continues through the immediate postimpasse implementation of a mandatory employment term arising directly from lawful multiemployer bargaining.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

The antitrust judgment remains vacated. Any preserved challenge under the National Labor Relations Act proceeds through labor law’s own channels.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | None | Would reverse | Would end the exemption at impasse for the coordinated imposition of a nonconsensual wage term |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Postimpasse implementation within multiemployer bargaining

Holding: The nonstatutory labor exemption protects the clubs’ joint implementation of the developmental-squad salary because the term was a mandatory subject, involved the bargaining parties, and grew directly from the lawful operation of their multiemployer bargaining relationship.

Operative rule: The exemption extends beyond the formal moment of impasse when coordinated employer conduct concerns the same mandatory subject, the same bargaining parties and unit, and the immediate implementation labor law permits after good-faith bargaining. Impasse does not automatically transfer supervision of the employment relationship from labor law to treble-damages antitrust litigation.

Reasoning: Multiemployer bargaining necessarily involves coordinated employer positions. Applying antitrust the instant impasse occurs would make conduct regulated and potentially permitted by labor law simultaneously subject to incompatible antitrust standards. It would also require antitrust courts to determine bargaining legality, duration, strategy, and good faith, matters assigned principally to labor law and the Board.

Limits and questions left open: The exemption does not cover combinations involving nonlabor markets, nonparties, nonmandatory subjects, or conduct sufficiently distant in time or circumstance from collective bargaining that antitrust review would no longer interfere materially with labor policy.

Legal basis: The Sherman Act, the National Labor Relations Act, and the nonstatutory labor exemption.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Breyer’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s treatment of impasse as a stage within the continuing labor relationship rather than an automatic return to ordinary antitrust supervision.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens would permit an antitrust action once collective agreement ends and the employers jointly impose a term without union consent. He regards the exemption’s continuation after impasse as unnecessary to protect collective bargaining. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule defining the nonstatutory labor exemption after bargaining impasse. It leaves the legality of the implementation under the NLRA to labor law and does not immunize employer coordination outside the bargaining relationship.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated 8-1 judgment, Breyer authorship, majority lineup, and Stevens dissent match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the Chief Justice’s historical join. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/231/))

Research sources: The official report for the multiemployer unit, impasse, implemented salary, exemption boundary, historical judgment, and lineup. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/231/))

### *Doe v. Kirchner*, 159 Ill. 2d 347, 638 N.E.2d 181

Decided June 21, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

#### Entering Posture and Questions

From: Richard was placed with the Does four days after birth. Kirchner had been falsely told that the child died, learned of the adoption fifty-seven days after birth, and promptly asserted his parental rights. Illinois ultimately found him fit and held that the adoption could not stand because his rights had never been lawfully terminated. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/illinois/supreme-court/1994/76063-7.html))

Question presented: Whether the Does’ established caregiving relationship gives them a federal constitutional right to retain permanent custody or to relitigate custody against the fit biological father.

Controlling simulated law before decision: Surviving family-integrity precedent gives constitutional weight to a biological father who promptly accepts parental responsibility and whose opportunity was obstructed by deception. No simulated holding creates a superior permanent right from possession under an adoption invalid against that parent.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

The adoption-invalidity judgment and rejection of the Does’ permanent custody claim remain in force. Implementation is subject to the separate child-specific procedure ordered in the coordinated *Baby Richard* decision.

Justice Kennedy delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Caregiving relationship and the rights of a fit parent

Holding: Whatever constitutional protection may attach to the Does’ established caregiving relationship, it does not give them a permanent right to defeat the custody claim of a fit biological father whose parental rights were never lawfully terminated and who promptly asserted responsibility after being deceived about the child’s survival.

Operative rule: Time in a prospective adoptive placement cannot itself transfer permanent parenthood when the adoption lacked the consent or valid termination of a fit parent’s rights. The prospective adoptive parents remain entitled to fair adjudication of their own legal claims, but not to a new comparative custody proceeding that treats possession accumulated during litigation as a constitutional source of superior title.

Reasoning: The Does’ relationship with Richard is genuine, but it developed while Kirchner actively contested an adoption entered without his consent. Kirchner did not abandon his opportunity to parent. He was told the child was dead and acted within weeks of learning the truth. Permitting litigation-generated delay to defeat his status would reward the very concealment that prevented his earlier involvement.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not hold that lawful adoptive relationships lack constitutional significance, prevent termination of an unfit biological parent through adequate procedures, or authorize an abrupt transfer without child-specific safeguards. The manner of implementation is governed separately by *Baby Richard*.

Legal basis: The Fourteenth Amendment and the family-liberty principles governing unwed fathers and state-created adoptive relationships.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone publicly joins the distinction between respect for a genuine caregiving relationship and acquisition of a superior permanent right through an adoption invalid against a fit, promptly asserting parent.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates a narrow adoption-specific boundary. A prospective adoptive placement does not displace a fit biological parent’s unextinguished rights merely because litigation allowed the placement to continue. The child’s independent procedural interest remains separate.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court historically denied merits review, and the Illinois judgment invalidating the adoption remained in force. Justice Stevens later denied an interim application on the ground that the claimed adult and child interests had already received exhaustive state proceedings. The simulation reaches the adult claim and unanimously affirms the Illinois judgment while separating the child’s implementation claim. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/illinois/supreme-court/1994/76063-7.html))

Research sources: The Illinois opinions for the deception, timing, fitness finding, and adoption judgment, and the Circuit Justice opinion for the later federal procedural posture. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/illinois/supreme-court/1994/76063-7.html))

### *Baby Richard, by his guardian ad litem, v. Kirchner*, 164 Ill. 2d 468, 649 N.E.2d 324

Decided June 21, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

#### Entering Posture and Questions

From: Richard lived with the Does from four days after birth until age four. The adult entitlement litigation established Kirchner’s fitness and custody right, but the order directing immediate surrender did not develop a child-focused record concerning the manner and timing of transition, professional support, or the effect of abrupt removal from the only home Richard knew. The transfer occurred on April 30, 1995. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/illinois/supreme-court/1995/78101-7.html))

Question presented: Whether Richard possessed an independent procedural interest requiring a focused hearing before the State abruptly implemented the lawful custody judgment.

Controlling simulated law before decision: Procedural due process requires calibration to the protected interest, the risk of avoidable error or harm, the value of additional safeguards, and the State’s lawful objectives. The coordinated *Doe* judgment forecloses relitigation of Kirchner’s fitness and permanent parental entitlement.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Scalia, and Thomas.

The Illinois court shall conduct a prompt hearing based on Richard’s current circumstances. It may order prospective transition, therapeutic, continuity, or contact measures necessary to protect him while respecting Kirchner’s established parental status. It may not recreate the former placement mechanically, reopen Kirchner’s fitness, or conduct a free-ranging comparative custody contest without an independent legal basis.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | None | Would affirm | Concludes that the custody entitlement and asserted transition interest received all process constitutionally due in the earlier proceedings |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Rejects a federal constitutional requirement for a separate transition hearing after lawful adjudication of custody |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Child-specific process governing implementation of custody

Holding: Illinois denied Richard adequate process by ordering immediate turnover after four years of continuous state-recognized placement without a proceeding capable of addressing the manner and timing of transition and the material risk of serious avoidable harm.

Operative rule: When a child has lived for a substantial period in a state-recognized family placement and the State orders an abrupt transfer to a fit parent with whom the child has no established relationship, procedural due process requires notice to the child’s representative and a prompt, focused hearing on immediate safety, timing, continuity, professional support, and transition. The hearing does not authorize a veto over the fit parent’s custody, a new determination of permanent parenthood, or indefinite delay.

Reasoning: The adult litigation determined who possessed the lawful parental right. It did not determine how the State could execute its coercive order without imposing unnecessary injury on the child. A criteria-bound implementation hearing protects Richard’s separate interest without converting psychological attachment into permanent legal parenthood or diminishing Kirchner’s established fitness.

Application: Because the historical transfer already occurred, present relief must be based on current facts. The state court may consider Richard’s existing relationships, safety, continuity, and need for structured contact or support, but it may not pretend that the pretransfer circumstances still exist or order a return solely to recreate them.

Limits and questions left open: The Court recognizes no general best-interests override of a fit parent, no right to remain permanently with prospective adoptive parents, and no authority for transition planning to become indefinite custody litigation.

Legal basis: The Fourteenth Amendment’s Due Process Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s separation of permanent parental entitlement from the child’s independent right to a procedure fitted to safe implementation.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens would affirm because the Illinois proceedings already considered the asserted family interests and the habeas order merely enforced the prior judgment. Scalia, joined by Thomas, would hold that the Constitution supplies no separate transition-hearing entitlement after lawful custody adjudication. Those propositions are noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision adds a narrow child-specific procedural rule without altering the fit-parent judgment in *Doe*. It does not create a new substantive hierarchy among biological, adoptive, or psychological parents. It governs only the State’s manner of implementing a custody judgment under circumstances presenting a material risk from abrupt disruption.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court historically issued no merits decision. Stevens denied the initial stay applications, while O’Connor, joined by Breyer, later dissented from the full Court’s refusal to stay immediate transfer and emphasized the absence of the hearing apparently contemplated by intervening Illinois law. The simulation adopts a narrower federal implementation rule through a six-Justice merits majority. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/513/1303/))

Research sources: The Illinois custody opinion and federal stay materials for the procedural sequence, placement duration, transfer order, competing due-process positions, and historical absence of a merits judgment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/illinois/supreme-court/1995/78101-7.html))

### *United States v. Ursery; United States v. $405,089.23 in United States Currency*, 518 U.S. 267

Argued April 17, 1996 | Decided June 24, 1996

From: In No. 95-345, a civil forfeiture settlement concerning Ursery’s home preceded his marijuana conviction, and the Sixth Circuit later reversed the criminal judgment on double-jeopardy grounds. In No. 95-346, criminal convictions preceded a district-court forfeiture judgment involving alleged drug proceeds, money-laundering property, and facilitating assets; the Ninth Circuit reversed the forfeiture. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/267/))

Question presented: Whether the civil in rem forfeitures constituted punishment or criminal proceedings for purposes of the Double Jeopardy Clause.

Controlling simulated law before decision: Simulated *Department of Revenue of Montana v. Kurth Ranch* requires examination of legal design and practical operation rather than labels, but concerns a materially different punitive tax. Simulated *United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property* governs forfeiture procedure, not double-jeopardy classification.

#### Judgment

| Docket | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| *United States v. Ursery*, No. 95-345 | Sixth Circuit reversed, 8-1 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stevens | Ursery’s criminal conviction and sentence are no longer barred by the earlier civil forfeiture settlement. |
| *United States v. $405,089.23 in United States Currency*, No. 95-346 | Ninth Circuit reversed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The double-jeopardy barrier to the forfeiture judgment is removed. |

Preserved Excessive Fines, due-process, innocent-owner, delay, nexus, and statutory objections remain available under their own sources.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal in both dockets | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | None | Supports reversal in both dockets | Emphasizes that a civil designation cannot immunize a materially different sanction whose practical operation supplies the clearest proof of punishment |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Supports reversal in both dockets | Would treat the Double Jeopardy Clause principally as a bar on successive criminal prosecutions |
| Concurrence in the judgment in part and dissent in part | Stevens | None | Supports reversal in No. 95-346; opposes reversal in No. 95-345 | Distinguishes forfeiture of unlawful proceeds from forfeiture of otherwise lawful facilitating property |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Civil in rem forfeiture and double-jeopardy punishment

Holding: The forfeitures authorized by 21 U.S.C. § 881 and 18 U.S.C. § 981 in these cases are civil proceedings and do not constitute punishment for purposes of the Double Jeopardy Clause.

Operative rule: The court first determines whether Congress selected a civil proceeding. If so, that classification controls unless objective design and operation provide the clearest proof that the proceeding is so punitive in form or effect that it must be treated as criminal punishment. Deterrent effect, a punitive aspect, or overlap with criminal conduct is insufficient by itself.

Reasoning: Congress used traditional in rem procedures and tied forfeiture to proceeds, facilitating property, and property involved in money laundering. Those mechanisms serve remedial functions by removing the instrumentalities and fruits of unlawful conduct. The claimants did not establish the exceptional punitive design or operation necessary to override Congress’s civil classification.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not resolve whether a particular forfeiture is excessive, disproportional, unsupported by the required statutory nexus, imposed without due process, delayed unconstitutionally, or unavailable against an innocent owner. A materially different nominally civil sanction may still constitute punishment upon the clearest proof.

Legal basis: The Double Jeopardy Clause.

Authority of the proposition: Stone’s opinion is joined by O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing a six-Justice majority for the civil-classification rule. Scalia and Thomas support both judgments on a different ground. Stevens supplies the ninth vote only in No. 95-346.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse in both dockets.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone distinguishes traditional civil in rem forfeiture from the extraordinary punitive tax invalidated in simulated *Kurth Ranch* and preserves separate review under the Excessive Fines and Due Process Clauses.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would rest on the proposition that these civil proceedings are not successive criminal prosecutions. Stevens agrees that forfeiture of unlawful proceeds in No. 95-346 is nonpunitive, but would treat the forfeiture of Ursery’s otherwise lawful home as punishment in No. 95-345.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Kurth Ranch* is distinguished and confined to an exaction whose unusual structure and operation supplied the required punitive character. *Good Real Property* remains the governing pre-seizure process rule. The Excessive Fines analysis of *Austin* remains separate from classification under Double Jeopardy.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court also reversed both judgments. The Court opinion had six members; Scalia and Thomas concurred in the judgment, while Stevens agreed only in No. 95-346. Stone replaces the historical Chief Justice as author and preserves the same docket-specific vote structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/267/))

Research sources: The official report for the corrected procedural sequence, consolidated dockets, statutory classifications, historical judgment, and separate writings. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/267/))

### *Lewis v. Casey*, 518 U.S. 343

Argued November 29, 1995 | Decided June 24, 1996

From: Twenty-two Arizona prisoners challenged the State’s law-library and legal-assistance system on behalf of a statewide class. The record identified serious obstacles affecting some illiterate, non-English-speaking, segregated, and lockdown prisoners, but the district court entered a detailed order governing facilities, staffing, materials, training, hours, and assistance throughout the prison system. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/343/))

Questions presented: What injury establishes denial of access to the courts, which legal proceedings fall within the protected access, and how broadly a federal court may remedy the violations proved.

Controlling simulated law before decision: Article III requires concrete injury. Simulated *Missouri v. Jenkins* requires structural relief to remain tied to the violation and federal control to contract as separable components comply.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-1.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

The statewide injunction shall be vacated. The district court may enter effective relief for the prisoners, institutions, and access barriers for which actual injury or a concrete recurring risk is proved, but may not impose unrelated operational requirements.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas in full; Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer as to Parts I and III | Supports reversal and remand | Five Justices support the complete actual-injury and remedial analysis; eight support the background and overbreadth-remand portions |
| Concurrence | Thomas | None | Supports reversal and remand | Would impose still stricter limits on federal judicial administration of state prisons |
| Concurrence in part, dissent in part, and concurrence in the judgment | Souter | Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports remand but rejects portions of the Court’s standing and merits analysis | Accepts the injunction’s overbreadth while preserving broader class-proof and access principles |
| Dissent | Stevens | None | Opposes the Court’s formal judgment and doctrinal narrowing | Would retain more of the district court’s liability findings and equitable authority |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Actual injury and scope of protected access

Holding: A law library or legal-assistance program is a means of providing access, not an independent constitutional entitlement. A prisoner must show that an official barrier frustrated or materially impeded a nonfrivolous direct criminal appeal, habeas claim, or civil-rights action concerning the legality or conditions of confinement, or creates a concrete and imminent recurring risk of doing so.

Operative rule: The claimant must identify the nonfrivolous legal channel affected and a present official barrier that causes or imminently threatens material impairment. A prisoner need not await final dismissal when the barrier is already preventing timely filing or meaningful presentation. Abstract inadequacy, inconvenience, or preference for a better program is insufficient.

Reasoning: The Constitution protects the ability to invoke judicial review, not a particular library, staffing model, or assistance program. Language, literacy, segregation, and lockdown conditions may make nominal resources unusable and can establish injury when connected to an actual or imminently threatened legal claim. The record proves serious barriers for some groups, but not a uniform denial in every institution and category.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not require prisoners to prove ultimate success on the underlying claim, guarantee optimal legal research, or extend the protected access beyond the legal channels identified here.

Legal basis: Article III and the constitutional right of access to the courts.

Authority of the proposition: Scalia’s complete opinion is joined by Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas, producing a five-Justice majority.

##### Scope and design of institutional relief

Holding: The detailed statewide injunction exceeds the proved violations and must be vacated.

Operative rule: Structural relief must identify the prisoners, institutions, and legal-access channels affected; cure the proven barrier effectively; omit program details unnecessary to that cure; and leave operational choice to prison officials when multiple lawful solutions exist. Systemwide relief remains available when systemwide or structurally recurring injury is actually proved.

Reasoning: Some ordered features may be necessary for prisoners who otherwise cannot prepare or file protected claims. Others reflect the court’s preferred design rather than a demonstrated constitutional necessity. Equity permits an effective remedy, but not administration of compliant prisons or unrelated aspects of the legal-services system.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not bar language assistance, trained help, monitoring, institution-wide measures, or retained jurisdiction when those devices are necessary to correct a demonstrated barrier.

Legal basis: Federal equitable authority, Article III, and the separation of powers.

Authority of the proposition: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer support vacatur and violation-fitted remand at this level of generality.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Scalia’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the functional actual-injury rule, including concrete imminent barriers, and the requirement that the district court preserve effective access while rebuilding relief from the specific violations proved.

#### Material Separate Writings

Thomas would place greater weight on state control of prison administration and questions any obligation to provide a particular form of legal assistance. Souter, joined by Ginsburg and Breyer, agrees that the order is overbroad but rejects converting every dispute concerning classwide proof into an Article III standing defect. Stevens would preserve broader liability findings and emphasizes the State’s affirmative responsibility to make retained judicial rights usable.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Bounds v. Smith* is clarified. It protects access to specified judicial channels, not a freestanding entitlement to libraries or legal assistance. Simulated *Jenkins* is applied by remedial analogy: institutional control must follow the proved violation, remain effective, and end as separable components are brought into compliance.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court also reversed and remanded 8-1. Historically, the Chief Justice joined Scalia’s complete opinion. In the simulation, Stone supplies that fifth complete join only after the controlling formulation recognizes a concrete imminent recurring barrier and acknowledges proved injuries affecting some prisoner groups. The Souter-Ginsburg-Breyer partial joins and Stevens dissent remain. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/343/))

Research sources: The official report for the class record, actual-injury question, statewide injunction, argument date, historical judgment, partial joins, and separate writings. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/343/))

### *Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc.*, 518 U.S. 415

Argued April 16, 1996 | Decided June 24, 1996

From: A federal diversity jury awarded Gasperini $450,000 after the Center lost 300 photographic transparencies. New York law had replaced its former “shocks the conscience” formulation with a more demanding rule permitting a new trial when an award materially deviates from reasonable compensation. The Second Circuit independently applied that standard and ordered a new trial unless Gasperini accepted $100,000. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/415/))

Questions presented: Whether New York’s standard governs a state-law damages claim in federal court, and how the Seventh Amendment allocates its application between federal trial and appellate courts.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 6-3.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Scalia, and Thomas.

The district court shall apply New York’s “deviates materially” standard through Rule 59. The court of appeals may review that determination only for abuse of discretion. No proper amount is fixed by this Court.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | None | Would sustain broader federal appellate authority | Concludes that the Seventh Amendment does not limit appellate application of the state damages standard as the Court holds |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reject the Court’s accommodation | Treats New York’s appellate-review prescription as incompatible with federal trial and appellate practice |

#### Decision of the Court

##### State substantive limit on compensatory damages

Holding: New York’s “deviates materially” standard governs the permissible range of compensation on Gasperini’s state-law claim in federal diversity court.

Operative rule: A state rule that materially narrows the damages available on a state-created claim must be given effect in diversity when disregarding it would alter outcomes substantially and encourage forum selection. Federal law continues to govern the judicial mechanism and allocation through which the state limit is applied.

Reasoning: New York adopted § 5501(c) to tighten the range of tolerable awards, not merely to regulate appellate housekeeping. Refusing to apply that substantive tolerance in federal court would permit systematically larger recoveries on identical state claims and undermine equal administration of state law.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not import New York’s full appellate structure, determine the proper damages figure, or establish a federal numerical cap.

Legal basis: The Rules of Decision Act and *Erie* principles.

##### Federal trial and appellate allocation

Holding: The federal district judge must apply the state excessiveness standard through the historically grounded Rule 59 new-trial power. Appellate review is limited to abuse of discretion.

Reasoning: The trial judge heard the evidence and remains closest to the jury’s valuation. Assigning primary application to that court respects the Reexamination Clause, while deferential appellate review supplies a lawful check and promotes reasonably consistent application. The Second Circuit erred by becoming the initial fact-bound remittitur tribunal.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not bar appellate correction of legal error or abuse of discretion and does not govern constitutional review of punitive damages.

Legal basis: The Seventh Amendment and Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 59.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand.

Public role: Joined Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the accommodation of New York’s substantive damages policy with the federal trial judge’s Rule 59 authority and deferential appellate review.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens would permit the federal appellate court to apply the state limit more directly. Scalia, joined by Thomas, would reject the Court’s hybrid allocation as unsupported by the Seventh Amendment and federal procedural structure. Those propositions are noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Simulated *Honda Motor Co. v. Oberg* remains unchanged. It concerns constitutionally adequate review of punitive damages and supplies no Erie or Seventh Amendment allocation rule for state compensatory damages. *Gasperini* creates the first controlling simulated rules for applying a state damages-tolerance standard in federal diversity court.

#### Historical Comparison

Historically, Ginsburg’s opinion commanded five votes. Stevens dissented separately, and Scalia dissented with the Chief Justice and Thomas. Stone rejects the Chief Justice’s historical position and joins Ginsburg, producing a 6-3 simulated judgment. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/415/))

Research sources: The official report for the lost transparencies, verdict, New York standard, Second Circuit remedy, argument date, historical 5-4 lineup, and separate writings. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/415/))
