## Decisions and Dispositions

### *Settle v. Dickson County School Board*, 53 F.3d 152

Decided June 25, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: The Sixth Circuit affirmed summary judgment for a teacher and school officials after the teacher declined to approve Dana Settle’s proposed paper on the life of Jesus. The assignment required prior topic approval, multiple sources, synthesis, and objective exposition. Settle changed from an approved topic without permission and submitted no paper satisfying the assignment. The teacher also made legally erroneous statements suggesting that personal religious subjects were categorically unsuitable for public-school work. Other students had been allowed to research subjects including reincarnation, spiritualism, and magic. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/53/152/498841/))

Question presented: Whether the teacher violated the First Amendment by rejecting the proposed topic and assigning no credit.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue* prohibits religious-viewpoint exclusion from a private after-school access program. It does not convert a graded curricular assignment into an open forum or displace legitimate pedagogical control.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

The summary judgment for respondents remains in force. No separate ruling is entered on municipal liability, qualified immunity, damages, or prospective relief.

Justice O’Connor delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Curricular topic selection and religious viewpoint

Holding: A public-school teacher does not violate the First Amendment by rejecting a proposed research-paper topic when neutral curricular requirements concerning prior approval, research method, source use, and objective exposition genuinely control the decision and would apply equally to comparable secular work.

Operative rule: A graded classroom assignment is part of the school curriculum rather than an open expressive forum. Educators may define its scope and method through restrictions reasonably related to legitimate pedagogical objectives. They may not exclude a subject or perspective merely because it is religious or use curricular authority as a pretext for hostility toward religion.

Reasoning: The assignment was designed to teach research and synthesis through an approved topic supported by multiple sources. Settle changed topics without approval and did not submit a paper demonstrating compliance with the assignment. The record leaves no genuine dispute that those neutral requirements independently controlled the grade. Ramsey’s categorical statements about religion were legally mistaken, but they do not establish that a compliant, objectively researched religious paper was rejected while a materially comparable secular departure was accepted.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not authorize a blanket prohibition on religious topics, permit exclusion of a religious perspective from an assignment inviting comparable viewpoints, or approve teacher hostility toward religion. It does not decide a case involving pretext, unequal treatment of comparable work, or a completed paper rejected because of its religious viewpoint.

Legal basis: The First Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the distinction between a graded curricular exercise and an open speech forum, including the Court’s express rejection of religion as a categorically disfavored classroom subject.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Good News/Good Sports Club* is distinguished. Its viewpoint rule continues to govern private expression in a school access program. The present decision creates a separate curricular rule under which genuine pedagogical requirements govern, subject to the prohibition on religious discrimination or pretext.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court did not historically decide the case. The simulation unanimously affirms the Sixth Circuit’s result but does not adopt any suggestion that religious subject matter is inherently unsuitable for public-school research. It instead confines the judgment to the neutral curricular requirements actually supported by the record. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/53/152/498841/))

Research sources: The published Sixth Circuit opinions for the assignment, competing reasons, comparative topics, summary-judgment posture, and historical result. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/53/152/498841/))

---

### *Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc.*, 518 U.S. 470

Argued April 23, 1996 | Decided June 26, 1996

From: Lora Lohr’s pacemaker used a Medtronic Model 4011 lead marketed through the § 510(k) substantial-equivalence process rather than full premarket approval. After the lead allegedly failed, the Lohrs asserted Florida design, manufacturing, warning, negligence, and strict-liability claims. The Eleventh Circuit rejected preemption of the design claims but found manufacturing and warning claims preempted by generally applicable FDA regulations. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-470.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether common-law duties are state requirements under 21 U.S.C. § 360k(a), whether § 510(k) or general FDA rules create the required federal counterpart, and whether parallel state duties survive.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick* requires identification of an operative federal standard governing the same aspect of performance before express or conflict preemption displaces state law.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Design and related strict-liability theories, No. 95-754 | Eleventh Circuit affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The design claims are restored for ordinary merits proceedings. |
| Manufacturing, labeling, and warning theories resting on the general federal rules, No. 95-886 | Eleventh Circuit reversed and case remanded, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | The claims may proceed unless claim-specific comparison identifies a binding and conflicting federal requirement. |
| Genuinely parallel state duties | Eleventh Circuit reversed and case remanded, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | A state damages remedy enforcing an equivalent federal obligation is not categorically preempted. |

Overall disposition: Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion announcing the judgment and Opinion of the Court in part | Stevens | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer as to the statutory background, § 510(k), general preemption framework, and disposition; Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg as to the manufacturing and warning analysis; Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg as to the broadest account of common-law requirements | Supports the complete judgment | Majority and plurality portions as stated |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone | None | Supports the complete judgment | Treats common-law duties as possible state requirements but requires a specific federal counterpart and preserves parallel claims |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Breyer | None | Supports the complete judgment | Agrees that common-law duties may impose requirements and that no adequate device-specific conflict exists here |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | O’Connor | Scalia, Thomas | Supports the design and parallel-duty results; opposes restoration of the broader manufacturing and warning claims | Would give broader preemptive effect to generally applicable FDA rules |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Section 510(k) and device-specific federal requirements

Holding: The § 510(k) substantial-equivalence process did not impose a device-specific federal safety requirement governing the Model 4011 lead’s design. It therefore did not preempt the Lohrs’ design claims.

Reasoning: Section 510(k) principally determines whether a device may enter the market because it is substantially equivalent to a predicate device. It does not represent the detailed federal evaluation and selection of a device’s design associated with full premarket approval. Treating equivalence review as an exclusive safety specification would give the transitional process a preemptive force Congress did not enact.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support the design-claim holding.

##### General manufacturing and labeling rules

Holding: The generally applicable manufacturing and labeling provisions identified here do not preempt the Lohrs’ manufacturing and warning theories because those provisions do not prescribe the Model 4011 lead’s specific manufacturing method, label, or warning content.

Operative rule: Express preemption requires a binding federal requirement applicable to the particular device or the relevant aspect of its performance, a state requirement concerning the same subject, and a material difference or addition between them. Regulatory generality, market permission, or agency inaction does not by itself establish that federal law selected an exclusive device standard.

Reasoning: The regulations below governed broad classes of manufacturers and devices. They did not establish that federal law had selected the challenged lead’s precise construction, manufacturing details, or warnings. Claim-specific proceedings remain necessary because a different federal condition, including a binding device-specific approval term, could produce a different result.

Authority of the proposition: Stevens’s opinion is supported on this question by Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing a six-Justice majority.

##### Common-law requirements and parallel duties

Holding: A common-law damages duty may constitute a state requirement for purposes of § 360k(a). It is not preempted, however, when it merely requires compliance with the same operative federal obligation.

Operative rule: The remedial form of a state duty does not determine preemption. The court must compare the conduct required by state law with the binding federal counterpart. A genuinely equivalent state duty is not different from or additional to federal law merely because state law supplies damages.

Reasoning: A damages judgment can impose a legal obligation, but that does not enlarge an absent or general federal counterpart. Conversely, a plaintiff may not label a materially different state standard as parallel merely by referring to federal law. The district court must identify the actual duties and compare them at the same level of specificity.

Authority of the proposition: Stone, Breyer, O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas support the proposition that common-law duties may be requirements, an actual five-Justice majority. All nine Justices support survival of genuinely parallel duties.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not create a private federal cause of action, decide tort liability, or determine preemption for a device subjected to full premarket approval or another specific federal condition.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm in No. 95-754; reverse and remand in No. 95-886.

Public role: Joined the identified portions of Stevens’s opinion and concurred separately in part and in the judgment.

Announced ground: Stone treats common-law obligations as possible state requirements but requires a binding federal counterpart governing the same device and subject. He joins restoration of all claims here because neither § 510(k) nor the cited general rules supplies that counterpart and because equivalent parallel duties remain permissible.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Myrick* is clarified for an express clause keyed to requirements applicable to a product. The decision adds claim-specific comparison, confirms that common-law duties may count as requirements, and establishes that equivalent parallel duties survive. *Myrick*’s rejection of preemption by regulatory absence or generalized federal policy remains unchanged.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reached the same overall claim results through fractured opinions. In the simulation, Stone joins the nonpreemption judgment rather than the historical Chief Justice’s broader dissenting position. His vote creates a six-Justice majority restoring the manufacturing and warning claims while preserving a separate five-Justice proposition that common-law duties can qualify as state requirements. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-470.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the device history, § 510(k) process, claims, lower-court judgment, partial joins, and historical disposition. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-470.pdf))

### *United States v. Virginia; Virginia v. United States*, 518 U.S. 515

Argued January 17, 1996 | Decided June 26, 1996

From: Virginia Military Institute excluded women from its distinctive public military program. After an earlier liability ruling, Virginia created the Virginia Women’s Institute for Leadership at Mary Baldwin College. VWIL differed materially from VMI in pedagogy, military environment, resources, curriculum, faculty, facilities, degree reputation, and alumni network. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-515.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether VMI’s male-only admissions policy satisfies equal protection and whether VWIL supplies an adequate remedy.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Kelley v. Board of Trustees of the University of Illinois* requires an actual important objective and a substantial, nonstereotyped relationship for an express sex classification. Earlier this term, *Klinger v. Department of Corrections* required comparison at the level of the particular opportunity and characteristics relevant to that opportunity.

Participation: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Nonparticipation: Thomas. The basis is not publicly stated.

#### Judgment

| Docket | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| *Virginia v. United States*, No. 94-2107, liability judgment | Affirmed, 7-1 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia | VMI’s categorical exclusion of women remains adjudged unconstitutional. |
| *United States v. Virginia*, No. 94-1941, remedial judgment | Reversed and remanded, 7-1 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia | Virginia must end the exclusion or provide a genuinely equal lawful alternative. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports both judgment components | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | None | Would reverse the liability ruling and sustain the separate-program remedy | Complete dissent |

#### Decision of the Court

##### VMI’s male-only admissions policy

Holding: Virginia’s categorical exclusion of women from VMI violates the Equal Protection Clause because the asserted diversity and pedagogical objectives do not supply an exceedingly persuasive justification for denying every qualified woman access to the Commonwealth’s unique program.

Operative rule: An express sex classification receives intermediate scrutiny. Government bears the burden of establishing that the classification serves an actual important objective and is substantially related to that objective through a genuine, nonstereotyped fit. The phrase “exceedingly persuasive justification” describes the rigor of that received standard; it does not create a separate tier of scrutiny.

Reasoning: Educational diversity and some forms of single-sex education may serve important purposes. Virginia did not establish that VMI’s male-only structure arose from an evenhanded effort to provide diverse public options or that excluding all women was necessary to preserve the adversative method. Average group preferences and physical differences do not prove that every individual woman lacks the capacity or desire to undertake VMI’s program.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not invalidate every public single-sex program or forbid tailored arrangements responding to genuine physical differences. Such a program must satisfy intermediate scrutiny and provide real rather than nominal equality.

Legal basis: The Equal Protection Clause.

##### Adequacy of VWIL

Holding: VWIL does not cure the constitutional violation because it does not provide women an opportunity genuinely equal to VMI in method, resources, institutional stature, degree value, professional network, and other tangible and intangible benefits.

Operative rule: A remedy for exclusion from a public educational opportunity must place the excluded class, as nearly as practicable, in the position it would have occupied absent the violation. Comparison proceeds in operation and includes institutional benefits that materially affect the value of the education.

Reasoning: The violation was exclusion from the distinctive public opportunity Virginia maintained at VMI, not merely the absence of some leadership curriculum for women. VWIL may possess educational merit, but it did not reproduce the rigorous military structure, resources, reputation, alumni influence, or professional opportunities Virginia continued to reserve for men.

Limits and questions left open: Virginia may admit qualified women to VMI or adopt another lawful system supplying genuinely equal public opportunities and benefits. The Court does not prescribe every implementation detail.

Legal basis: The Equal Protection Clause and federal equitable-remedy principles.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm the liability judgment; reverse the remedial judgment and remand.

Public role: Joined Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone publicly joins the Court’s application of intermediate scrutiny, including its treatment of “exceedingly persuasive justification” as a description of the existing standard rather than a new tier, and the operational comparison of VMI and VWIL.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Kelley* is applied and clarified. An important objective must explain the actual classification and may not rest on group stereotypes. *Klinger*’s service-specific comparison principle is extended to public educational opportunities, including relevant intangible benefits. The decision does not create a categorical rule against single-sex public education.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed and remanded 7-1, with Justice Thomas not participating. Chief Justice Rehnquist concurred only in the judgment. Stone instead joins Ginsburg’s complete opinion, preserving the 7-1 result while giving the Court’s full reasoning seven votes. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-515.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for VMI and VWIL’s characteristics, participation, judgment, author, joins, and historical dissent. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-515.pdf))

### *Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee v. Federal Election Commission*, 518 U.S. 604

Argued April 15, 1996 | Decided June 26, 1996

From: Before the Republican Party selected its Colorado Senate nominee, the state party’s federal campaign committee purchased advertisements criticizing the likely Democratic nominee. The summary-judgment record showed no consultation, agreement, or coordination with a candidate. The FEC nevertheless treated the expenditure as subject to the statutory party ceiling. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-604.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether the expenditure was independent and constitutionally protected, and whether the Court should decide the broader facial challenge to limits on coordinated party spending.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *McIntyre v. Ohio Elections Commission* and simulated *Day v. Holahan* protect independent core political advocacy. Neither resolves the constitutional treatment of actual coordinated party expenditures.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens and Ginsburg.

The challenged advertisements may not be treated as coordinated merely because the speaker was a political party. Judgment shall be entered for the Colorado Party on the FEC’s claim concerning those advertisements. The broader facial counterclaim concerning actual coordinated expenditures is restored for lower-court proceedings.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion announcing the judgment | Breyer | Stone, O’Connor, Souter | Supports vacatur and remand | Resolves only the independent expenditure and leaves coordinated spending open |
| Concurrence in the judgment and dissent in part | Kennedy | Scalia | Supports the judgment | Would invalidate party-expenditure limits more broadly |
| Concurrence in the judgment and dissent in part | Thomas | Scalia as to the principal broader-invalidation portions | Supports the judgment | Would treat party spending as the party’s own protected speech even when closely associated with a candidate |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg | Would affirm application of the party limit | Treats party expenditures as uniquely connected to candidates and contribution circumvention |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Independent political-party expenditure

Holding: Section 441a(d)(3) is unconstitutional as applied to the Colorado Party’s independently developed, pre-nomination advertisements.

Operative rule: A party expenditure is not converted into a contribution merely because the party and a candidate share political interests. Government must establish coordination, agreement, candidate control, or another legally sufficient connection showing that the spending functions as a candidate-controlled transfer or a means of circumventing contribution limits.

Reasoning: The Colorado Party developed and placed the advertisements before selecting a nominee and without candidate consultation or control. The FEC’s categorical party-status theory would eliminate the independent-expenditure category for an association formed to speak about elections. No record-supported quid pro quo or circumvention interest justified limiting this expenditure.

Authority of the proposition: Breyer, Stone, O’Connor, Souter, Kennedy, Scalia, and Thomas support the same as-applied proposition, producing an actual seven-Justice majority.

##### Coordinated party expenditures

Holding: The Court does not decide the facial validity of limits on actual coordinated party expenditures.

Reasoning: The independent advertisement supplies complete relief on the developed record. The lower courts did not establish a precise definition of coordination or develop the evidence concerning contribution circumvention and candidate control needed to adjudicate materially different spending.

Limits and questions left open: Disclosure, reporting, contribution limits, precise anti-coordination rules, and restrictions on candidate-controlled spending remain open. No controlling rationale emerges concerning those subjects.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand.

Public role: Joined Breyer’s opinion announcing the judgment.

Announced ground: Stone joins the narrow as-applied holding that party identity and common electoral purpose do not establish coordination. He announces no position on the facial validity of limits governing actual candidate-controlled or coordinated expenditures.

#### Material Separate Writings

Kennedy and Thomas, with the stated Scalia joins, would invalidate the party-expenditure limitation more broadly. Their coordinated-expenditure propositions are noncontrolling. Stevens, joined by Ginsburg, would permit Congress to treat party expenditures as sufficiently connected to candidates to justify the statutory limit.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*McIntyre* and *Day* are applied to independent party advocacy. The decision adds a recurring coordination boundary: organizational identity and shared political goals are insufficient without an operative connection to candidate control or contribution circumvention. The law governing actual coordinated party expenditures remains unresolved.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court also vacated and remanded 7-2 and left the broader facial issue unresolved. Stone joins Breyer’s narrow route rather than the historical Chief Justice’s broader position, while Kennedy, Scalia, and Thomas continue to support wider invalidation. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-604.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the advertisement, independence record, procedural posture, fragmented writings, historical judgment, and reserved facial issue. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-604.pdf))

### *Felker v. Turpin*, 518 U.S. 651

Argued June 3, 1996 | Decided June 28, 1996

From: After the Eleventh Circuit denied authorization for a successive federal habeas application under the newly enacted Antiterrorism and Effective Death Penalty Act of 1996, Felker sought Supreme Court appellate or certiorari review and separately filed an original habeas petition. His proposed claims did not satisfy the Act’s new successive-claim conditions. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-651.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether 28 U.S.C. § 2244(b)(3)(E) bars Supreme Court review of the gatekeeping order, whether AEDPA repealed the Court’s original habeas jurisdiction, and whether the successive-petition restrictions suspend the writ.

#### Judgment

| Matter | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Appellate or certiorari review of the Eleventh Circuit gatekeeping order | Dismissed for want of jurisdiction, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The Eleventh Circuit’s authorization denial remains undisturbed. |
| Original petition for habeas corpus and related stay | Denied, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | No original writ or stay issues. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports both dispositions | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stevens | Souter, Breyer | Supports both dispositions | Emphasizes the constitutional importance of preserving some Supreme Court habeas channel |
| Concurrence | Souter | Stevens, Breyer | Supports both dispositions | Reserves materially different applications that might eliminate meaningful review of a qualifying constitutional claim |

#### Threshold Decision

Threshold holding: Section 2244(b)(3)(E) expressly bars appeal and certiorari from a court of appeals’ decision granting or denying authorization to file a successive habeas application.

Reasoning and application: The provision names both forms of review and attaches the bar to the precise gatekeeping determination Felker seeks to challenge. Congress may make exceptions to the Court’s appellate jurisdiction subject to independent constitutional limits. Because original habeas jurisdiction remains available, this case does not require decision whether Congress could eliminate every possible Supreme Court channel.

Effect on judgment below: The Eleventh Circuit’s gatekeeping order remains in force and is not reviewed on its merits.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. § 2244(b)(3)(E), Article III, and the Exceptions Clause.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Original habeas jurisdiction and AEDPA

Holding: AEDPA did not repeal the Supreme Court’s original habeas jurisdiction.

Reasoning: Congress expressly altered other original-writ routes and barred original habeas petitions in the courts of appeals but did not repeal the statutes authorizing the Supreme Court to entertain an original petition. Repeal of that historically recognized jurisdiction cannot be inferred from silence.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. §§ 2241 and 2254 and Supreme Court Rule 20.4.

##### Successive claims and suspension of the writ

Holding: AEDPA’s successive-claim restrictions do not suspend the writ as applied here, and Felker’s original petition fails both the statutory conditions and Rule 20.4’s exceptional-circumstances requirement.

Operative rule: A state prisoner seeking an original successive writ must satisfy the restrictions governing repetitive or newly presented claims and must also establish the exceptional circumstances required for the Supreme Court’s exercise of original habeas authority.

Reasoning: The Act preserves a lawful route for qualifying claims while restricting repetition and abuse. Felker identifies no new retroactive rule, newly discovered evidence satisfying the statutory innocence requirement, or circumstance distinguishing his filing from an ordinary successive capital petition.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide a case in which a reliably demonstrated innocence claim would otherwise lack every lawful forum, or whether Congress could abolish all Supreme Court habeas review.

Legal basis: The Suspension Clause, 28 U.S.C. § 2244(b), and Supreme Court Rule 20.4.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Dismiss the appellate or certiorari request; deny the original writ and stay.

Public role: Author of the unanimous Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone distinguishes the eliminated appellate route from the preserved original jurisdiction and concludes that Felker satisfies neither AEDPA’s successive-claim standards nor the Court’s exceptional-circumstances rule.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens and Souter, with Breyer joining both writings, emphasize that the judgment depends materially on preservation of a meaningful original-habeas channel. Their broader constitutional reservations are noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rules governing AEDPA’s gatekeeping orders and original Supreme Court habeas petitions. Appellate and certiorari review of a gatekeeping decision is unavailable, original habeas jurisdiction survives, and the new successive-claim restrictions operate alongside Rule 20.4’s exceptional-circumstances requirement.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court unanimously reached the same dispositions and principal jurisdictional conclusions. Chief Justice Rehnquist wrote the historical opinion. Stone authors the simulated unanimous opinion and preserves the same original-jurisdiction and Suspension Clause boundaries. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-651.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for AEDPA’s text and timing, the gatekeeping denial, the original petition, judgment, separate writings, and historical treatment of Supreme Court habeas jurisdiction. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-651.pdf))

---

### *Board of County Commissioners v. Umbehr*, 518 U.S. 668

Argued November 28, 1995 | Decided June 28, 1996

From: Umbehr held an at-will contract to haul trash for Wabaunsee County and repeatedly criticized the county board. The board terminated the contract, and the Tenth Circuit recognized a possible First Amendment retaliation claim. The case involved an existing commercial relationship rather than an applicant or bidder seeking a new contract. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-668.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether the First Amendment protects an existing independent contractor from termination or nonrenewal because of protected speech, and what causation and operational defenses apply.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Waters v. Churchill* and *National Treasury Employees Union v. United States* protect public workers’ citizen speech while recognizing legitimate operational interests. *McKennon v. Nashville Banner Publishing Co.* separates unlawful decisional causation from later-discovered facts affecting remedy.

#### Judgment

Affirmed and remanded, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

The action returns to the district court for application of the speech balance, causation rules, lawful contractual defenses, and any separately preserved immunity or remedial questions.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse and reject extension of public-employee doctrine to contractors | Complete dissent, also addressing *O’Hare* |

#### Decision of the Court

##### First Amendment protection of an existing government contractor

Holding: Government may not terminate or refuse to renew an existing independent contractor’s relationship in retaliation for protected speech merely because the relationship is at will.

Operative rule: The court applies the *Pickering* balance, adjusted to the government’s interests as contractor rather than employer. The claimant must establish protected citizen speech and retaliatory causation. Government may prevail by proving that its operational interests outweigh the speech interest or that it would have taken the same action for lawful reasons under *Mt. Healthy*.

Reasoning: The absence of a contractual entitlement to continued work does not authorize government to exercise its discretion for a constitutionally forbidden reason. Existing contractors, like at-will public employees, may depend substantially on a recurring governmental relationship and remain citizens entitled to criticize government. At the same time, contractual efficiency, reliability, cost, and performance may carry weight different from ordinary employment considerations.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not govern bidders or applicants seeking a new contract, require renewal despite deficient performance, or determine qualified immunity, municipal liability, or damages. Later-discovered misconduct may limit remedy but does not retroactively supply the original decision’s lawful cause.

Legal basis: The First Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm and remand.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the adapted *Pickering* balance, the *Mt. Healthy* same-decision defense, and the separation between retaliatory liability and later facts bearing only on remedy.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Waters* and *National Treasury Employees Union* are extended from formal public employment to an existing government-contractor relationship. *McKennon*’s liability-remedy separation is applied by analogy. The decision creates no general constitutional right to receive a public contract.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court affirmed and remanded 7-2. Chief Justice Rehnquist declined part of O’Connor’s opinion. Stone instead joins the opinion in full, giving the complete contractor-speech framework seven votes. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-668.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the contract, criticism, termination, procedural posture, historical judgment, and separate dissent. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-668.pdf))

---

### *O’Hare Truck Service, Inc. v. City of Northlake*, 518 U.S. 712

Argued March 20, 1996 | Decided June 28, 1996

From: O’Hare had provided towing services to Northlake through a longstanding police rotation list. Its owner alleged that the mayor demanded campaign support, that he refused and supported the mayor’s opponent, and that the city removed O’Hare from the list in retaliation. The Seventh Circuit dismissed the complaint because O’Hare was an independent contractor rather than a public employee. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-712.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether the patronage protections of *Elrod* and *Branti*, or the speech protection of *Pickering*, extend to a regular municipal service provider, and whether the complaint states a claim despite the absence of a contractual entitlement.

Controlling simulated law before decision: The same-day *Umbehr* decision is uncoordinated and therefore does not enter the legal baseline. Preexisting patronage and public-employee speech cases supply the relevant analogies.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

The complaint is reinstated. The district court shall determine the actual reason for removal, the proper First Amendment framework, lawful performance defenses, municipal responsibility, causation, and any preserved immunity or remedy issue.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm dismissal | Dissent filed jointly with the related *Umbehr* analysis |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Patronage retaliation against a regular service provider

Holding: A municipality may not remove a regular service provider from an established government rotation because the provider refused to support the governing political faction or supported its opponent, unless political affiliation is an appropriate requirement for the work.

Operative rule: A claim based principally on compelled political allegiance or affiliation is governed by *Elrod* and *Branti*. A claim based on independent speech is governed by the *Pickering* balance adapted to the contractual setting. In either event, *Mt. Healthy* preserves a defense that the same action would have occurred for lawful reasons.

Reasoning: The complaint alleges a direct sequence from a demand for campaign support, through refusal and opposition activity, to removal from a list historically maintained on performance grounds. Government’s discretion to select service providers remains broad, but it may not be exercised to compel political support unrelated to the work. At the pleading stage, the city’s possible reliability, cost, competition, or performance explanations cannot be resolved against O’Hare.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not govern first-time applicants or competitive bidders, decide whether political affiliation was appropriate for any particular service, or determine qualified immunity, damages, reinstatement, or the mayor’s final policymaking authority.

Legal basis: The First Amendment and 42 U.S.C. § 1983.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joined Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the extension of patronage and speech-retaliation protection to an established municipal service relationship while preserving lawful selection discretion and the *Mt. Healthy* defense.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision extends *Elrod*, *Branti*, and *Pickering* beyond formal public employment to an established service-provider relationship. Together with *Umbehr*, it distinguishes constitutionally forbidden retaliation from the absence of a property entitlement and from lawful contracting discretion.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court also reversed and remanded 7-2, with Kennedy writing and Scalia dissenting with Thomas. Stone replaces the historical Chief Justice’s majority vote and joins Kennedy’s opinion in full. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-712.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the rotation-list relationship, complaint allegations, dismissal posture, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-712.pdf))

### *Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC*, 518 U.S. 727

Argued February 21, 1996 | Decided June 28, 1996

From: The challenged Cable Act provisions addressed patently offensive sexual programming on leased- and public-access channels. Section 10(a) permitted cable operators to refuse covered leased-access programming under a written policy. Section 10(b) required covered leased-access programming to be placed on a blocked channel available only after a subscriber’s written request. Section 10(c) permitted operators to prohibit the same category on public-access channels. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-727.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether each provision violates the First Amendment, including the distinct interests of programmers, operators, viewers, and government-created access channels.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. FCC* requires cable-specific attention to editorial interests, burden, and evidentiary fit. Earlier this term, *Action for Children’s Television v. FCC* required realistic adult access and materially fitted child-protection measures when protected indecent speech is channeled.

#### Judgment

| Provision | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Section 10(a), leased-access operator discretion | D.C. Circuit affirmed, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Kennedy, Ginsburg | Section 10(a) remains facially enforceable, subject to as-applied review. |
| Section 10(b), mandatory segregation and written-request access | D.C. Circuit reversed, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | Enforcement shall be enjoined. Lawful subscriber-controlled blocking remains available. |
| Section 10(c), public-access operator veto | D.C. Circuit reversed, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Enforcement shall be enjoined. The provision is severable. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion announcing the judgment and Opinion of the Court in part | Breyer | Stevens, O’Connor, Souter as to § 10(a); Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg as to § 10(b); Stevens, Souter as to § 10(c) | Supports the provision-specific judgment | Court opinion as to § 10(b); plurality reasoning as to §§ 10(a) and 10(c) |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone | Scalia and Thomas as to the private-editorial and state-action analysis of § 10(a) only | Supports all three judgment components | Joins Breyer’s § 10(b) analysis and Kennedy’s § 10(c) analysis; states a separate ground for § 10(a) |
| Concurrence | Stevens | None | Supports all three judgment components | Adds observations concerning access and the regulated speech |
| Concurrence | Souter | None | Supports all three judgment components | Emphasizes provision-specific review rather than one categorical cable rule |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | O’Connor | None | Supports the § 10(a) and § 10(b) judgments; opposes invalidation of § 10(c) | Issue-specific |
| Concurrence in part, concurrence in the judgment in part, and dissent in part | Kennedy | Ginsburg; Stone as to § 10(c) only | Opposes the § 10(a) judgment; supports invalidation of §§ 10(b) and 10(c) | Applies heightened public-forum scrutiny to § 10(c) |
| Concurrence in the judgment in part and dissent in part | Thomas | Scalia | Supports § 10(a); opposes invalidation of §§ 10(b) and 10(c) | Relies more broadly on operator editorial rights and congressional authority |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Section 10(a), leased-access operator discretion

Holding: Section 10(a) survives the facial challenge, 7-2.

Reasoning: Four Justices apply a contextual balance and conclude that the provision permissibly restores limited operator choice over a narrow category on federally created leased-access channels. Stone, Scalia, and Thomas conclude that a private operator’s decision is not attributable to government merely because federal law permits it, absent coercion, joint administration, or selective governmental enforcement.

No controlling rationale under *Marks*: Neither the contextual-balancing rationale nor the private-editorial and state-action rationale is a logical subset of the other. The judgment establishes the facial validity of § 10(a) but no unified reusable test.

Limits and questions left open: An operator policy produced by governmental coercion, administered jointly with government, or used outside the statutory category remains open to as-applied challenge.

##### Section 10(b), mandatory segregation and blocking

Holding: Section 10(b) violates the First Amendment because its segregation and written-request system imposes avoidable barriers on adults’ access to protected programming.

Operative rule: Government may channel protected indecent cable programming to protect children only through a measure that preserves realistic adult access and is reasonably fitted to the demonstrated harm. A regime requiring advance written identification, delay, and placement on a stigmatized blocked channel fails when prompt, confidential, subscriber-controlled blocking can address household exposure less restrictively.

Reasoning: Adults could not receive occasional programming without planning, disclosure, and delay. The system also reduced programmer reach and identified subscribers seeking access. Existing lockboxes and related controls enabled households to block unwanted material without imposing those burdens on every adult viewer.

Authority of the proposition: Breyer’s § 10(b) analysis is joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Ginsburg, producing a seven-Justice majority.

##### Section 10(c), public-access operator veto

Holding: Section 10(c) violates the First Amendment on the present record, 6-3.

Reasoning: Breyer, Stevens, and Souter conclude through contextual balancing that the broad veto is unsupported by the record and unnecessary in light of household blocking. Stone, Kennedy, and Ginsburg conclude that government-created public-access channels cannot be subjected to an insufficiently constrained content-based veto without compelling justification and narrow tailoring.

No controlling rationale under *Marks*: The contextual-balancing and public-forum strict-scrutiny rationales are not logical subsets of one another. The provision’s invalidity on this record is controlling, but no single rationale governs every future public-access arrangement.

Limits and questions left open: Government may address obscenity, threats, exploitation, and other unprotected material, and may employ lawful subscriber-controlled blocking or precise channeling rules.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm as to § 10(a); reverse as to §§ 10(b) and 10(c).

Public role: Joined Breyer’s § 10(b) analysis, joined Kennedy’s § 10(c) analysis, and concurred separately concerning § 10(a).

Announced ground: Stone treats § 10(a) as a limited restoration of private editorial choice absent governmental coercion or joint censorship. He concludes that § 10(b) unnecessarily burdens adult access and that § 10(c) delegates a content-based veto over government-created public-access expression without the required justification or tailoring.

#### Material Separate Writings

Kennedy, joined by Ginsburg and by Stone on § 10(c), would apply heightened public-forum scrutiny to the public-access veto. Thomas, joined by Scalia, would uphold all three provisions through a broader account of operator editorial authority and congressional control over statutory access. Those broader propositions are noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Action for Children’s Television* is applied to § 10(b)’s adult-access burden and less restrictive household controls, but its broadcast-specific setting is not converted into a universal cable test. *Turner*’s requirement of record-supported burden and fit remains applicable. No unified forum or state-action rule emerges for every cable-access channel.

#### Historical Comparison

Historically, § 10(a) was upheld 7-2, § 10(b) was invalidated 6-3, and § 10(c) was invalidated 5-4. Stone’s votes leave the § 10(a) result unchanged but enlarge the invalidating coalitions to 7-2 for § 10(b) and 6-3 for § 10(c). The decision remains fractured by provision and rationale. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-727.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the statutory provisions, access-channel structure, blocking procedures, severability, historical component votes, partial joins, and separate writings. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-727.pdf))

### *United States v. Winstar Corp.*, 518 U.S. 839

Argued April 24, 1996 | Decided July 1, 1996

From: Federal banking agencies induced private parties to acquire failing thrifts through agreements allowing supervisory goodwill and capital credits to count toward regulatory capital for defined periods. FIRREA later changed the governing capital rules and made the promised treatment unavailable. The respondents sought contract damages rather than invalidation of FIRREA or continued regulatory treatment. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-839.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether enforceable contracts allocated the risk of regulatory change to the Government and whether the unmistakability, reserved-powers, sovereign-acts, or impossibility doctrines bar damages.

Controlling simulated law before decision: Existing simulated law requires agency contractual authority to arise from enacted sources and preserves Congress’s power to change substantive law. No prior simulated holding determines the Government’s damages liability when a valid contract allocates the financial consequences of that change.

#### Judgment

Affirmed and remanded, 8-1.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer. Opposing: Ginsburg.

The Federal Circuit’s liability judgment remains in force. The cases return for transaction-specific proceedings on causation, foreseeability, mitigation, and damages. FIRREA remains fully effective.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion announcing the judgment and Opinion of the Court in part | Souter | Stevens and Breyer in full; Stone and O’Connor in all portions except the broadest unmistakability and sovereign-acts discussions | Supports affirmance and remand | Five-Justice Court opinion on contract formation, risk allocation, breach, and the core rejection of the sovereign defenses |
| Concurrence | Breyer | None | Supports affirmance and remand | Emphasizes ordinary contract interpretation and the economic substance of the transactions |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas | Supports affirmance and remand | Reaches liability through a narrower account of unmistakability and sovereign acts |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | None | Would reverse | Would find no enforceable contractual allocation of future regulatory risk |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Contract formation and allocation of regulatory-change risk

Holding: Authorized federal officials entered enforceable contracts permitting respondents to count supervisory goodwill and capital credits as specified, and those contracts allocated to the Government the financial risk that later law would prevent the promised treatment.

Operative rule: The United States is bound when authorized officials, acting within statutory contracting and assistance powers, make an express, bargained-for commitment that reasonably allocates a defined regulatory risk. The promise need not disable future legislation; it may instead require compensation if lawful sovereign action prevents performance.

Reasoning: The acquisitions would have produced immediate regulatory insolvency without the promised accounting treatment. The agencies sought transactions that private parties otherwise would not undertake, and the respondents assumed failing-thrift liabilities in exchange. Treating the accounting documents as descriptions of current policy rather than contractual commitments would remove the central inducement from the bargains.

Limits and questions left open: Not every forbearance letter, regulatory statement, or agency prediction is contractual. Authority cannot be created by promise, and the result depends on authorized, transaction-specific commitments supported by consideration.

Authority of the proposition: Souter, Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, and Breyer support the proposition at the same operative level, producing a five-Justice majority.

##### Unmistakability, sovereign acts, and impossibility

Holding: FIRREA lawfully changed capital regulation but did not extinguish the Government’s contractual obligation to pay damages for breach. The unmistakability, sovereign-acts, reserved-powers, and impossibility doctrines do not bar recovery on these agreements.

Reasoning: Respondents seek no injunction against Congress or the regulators. Damages preserve sovereign authority while enforcing the financial consequence of an authorized bargain. The unmistakability doctrine does not require special words when the contract does not surrender legislative power but allocates the cost of nonperformance. A public and general sovereign act does not excuse a risk the contract placed on the Government, and impossibility does not discharge a foreseeable regulatory risk central to the transaction.

Limits and questions left open: The Government does not insure against every later legal change, and agencies may not bargain away constitutional or nondelegable sovereign powers. Respondents remain subject to ordinary contract limitations on causation, foreseeability, mitigation, and proof of loss.

Authority of the proposition: Souter, Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, and Breyer support the controlling core. Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas concur in the judgment through a narrower doctrinal route.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm liability and remand for damages proceedings.

Public role: Joined Souter’s opinion except its broadest discussions of unmistakability and sovereign acts.

Announced ground: Stone concludes that the authorized agreements allocated the financial risk of future capital-rule change without restricting Congress’s power to legislate. FIRREA remains valid, but the Government remains answerable in damages under ordinary contract law.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, agrees that the Government is liable but would confine the rationale more tightly to the contracts’ text and the distinction between sovereign authority and contractual damages. Ginsburg would find the documents insufficient to establish a promise allocating future legal change.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for governmental contracts allocating the financial consequences of later legislation. Congress’s power to change substantive law remains intact; an authorized contract may nonetheless require damages when the Government accepted consideration and assumed the relevant risk.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court affirmed 7-2 through a fractured set of opinions. Stone rejects the historical Chief Justice’s dissenting position and joins the contract-liability coalition, producing an 8-1 judgment. His partial join also creates a five-Justice Court opinion on contract formation, risk allocation, and the core rejection of the sovereign defenses. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-839.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the thrift transactions, supervisory-goodwill commitments, FIRREA’s effect, contractual theories, partial joins, historical judgment, and remand posture. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-839.pdf))

### Summary Merits Disposition: *Pennsylvania v. Labron; Pennsylvania v. Kilgore*, 518 U.S. 938

Decided July 1, 1996

From: In both cases, the Pennsylvania Supreme Court suppressed cocaine found in vehicles after requiring a separate exigency beyond probable cause and vehicle mobility. The *Labron* judgment used interwoven state and federal reasoning without clearly identifying an independent state ground. *Kilgore* expressly rested on the Fourth Amendment. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-938.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether the Supreme Court possesses jurisdiction over each judgment and whether the federal automobile exception requires a separate exigency.

#### Judgment

| Docket | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| *Pennsylvania v. Labron*, No. 95-1691 | Certiorari granted; judgment reversed and case remanded, 7-2 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Stevens, Ginsburg | Pennsylvania may proceed without the suppressed evidence being excluded on the erroneous federal rule, subject to independent state law and other preserved issues. |
| *Pennsylvania v. Kilgore*, No. 95-1738 | Certiorari granted; judgment reversed and case remanded, 7-2 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Stevens, Ginsburg | The state court shall reconsider without a separate federal exigency requirement. |

The Court issued a per curiam opinion. Stevens dissented, joined by Ginsburg.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Jurisdiction over the state judgments

Holding: The Court has jurisdiction in both cases. *Labron* rests on federal law or state law interwoven with the federal question without a clear independent-state-ground statement. *Kilgore* expressly rests on the Fourth Amendment.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. § 1257(a) and the adequate-and-independent-state-ground doctrine.

##### Federal automobile exception

Holding: When officers have probable cause to believe a readily mobile vehicle contains contraband or evidence, the Fourth Amendment permits a warrantless search of the vehicle and containers within the scope of that probable cause. No separate exigent-circumstances showing is required.

Reasoning: The vehicle’s ready mobility and the reduced privacy associated with regulated automobile use supply the exception recognized in the controlling automobile cases. Requiring an additional emergency would add a federal element those decisions do not contain.

Limits and questions left open: The Pennsylvania courts remain free to resolve a clearly preserved independent state constitutional ground, ownership or scope question, or another suppression issue not decided here.

Legal basis: The Fourth Amendment.

Authority of the proposition: The seven-Justice per curiam coalition necessarily decides both propositions. Because the disposition is summary, its precedential effect is confined to jurisdiction over these judgments and rejection of a separate federal exigency requirement where probable cause and ready mobility exist.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Grant, reverse, and remand in both dockets.

Public role: Joined the per curiam Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s state-ground jurisdiction analysis and the settled probable-cause and ready-mobility rule.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Arizona v. Evans* is applied to the interwoven state and federal grounds. *Ornelas v. United States* continues to govern independent appellate review of probable cause, and *Whren v. United States* continues to govern objective justification. Neither decision is altered. The present disposition adds the narrow rule that the automobile exception requires no separate exigency once probable cause and ready mobility are established.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court entered the same 7-2 summary disposition, with Stevens dissenting and Ginsburg joining him. Stone occupies the historical Chief Justice’s place in the per curiam coalition. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-938.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the two vehicle searches, probable-cause posture, state-ground analysis, per curiam judgment, and historical dissent. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-518/pdf/USREPORTS-518-938.pdf))
