### *Montana v. Egelhoff*, 518 U.S. 37

Argued March 20, 1996 | Decided June 13, 1996

From: The Montana Supreme Court reversed two deliberate-homicide convictions after concluding that Montana continued to require purposeful or knowing conduct, that Egelhoff’s intoxication evidence was relevant to those mental states, and that the statutory instruction impermissibly prevented the jury from considering that evidence. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/37/))

Question presented: Whether Montana may retain purpose or knowledge as an element and require proof beyond a reasonable doubt while categorically forbidding the jury to consider competent voluntary-intoxication evidence offered to negate that retained element.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Ratzlaf v. United States*, *Staples v. United States*, and *United States v. X-Citement Video, Inc.* preserve enacted mens rea requirements. *Victor v. Nebraska / Sandoval v. California* preserves the prosecution’s burden to prove every retained element beyond a reasonable doubt. No earlier OT1995 decision materially changes the offense-definition or evidence question. `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Ginsburg.

The Montana Supreme Court’s judgment remains in force, and Egelhoff’s deliberate-homicide convictions remain reversed. Montana may retry Egelhoff. At any retrial using the same purposeful-or-knowing elements as authoritatively construed, the jury may not be categorically prohibited from considering competent voluntary-intoxication evidence offered to negate those elements.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas | Would reverse and reinstate the convictions | Would sustain the statute under the historical authority of States to withhold exculpatory effect from voluntary intoxication |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | None | Would reverse and reinstate the convictions | Would characterize § 45-2-203 as a substantive definition of criminal responsibility rather than an exclusion of evidence relevant to an unchanged element |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Exclusion of evidence directly negating a retained element

Holding: When the State’s highest court authoritatively determines that purpose or knowledge remains an element and that voluntary-intoxication evidence is relevant to whether that element existed, due process does not permit a categorical instruction forbidding the jury to consider competent intoxication evidence offered to negate the retained element.

Operative rule: A State remains free to define crimes and culpability, including by making voluntary intoxication legally irrelevant through a genuine substantive definition. If the State instead retains a subjective mental-state element and recognizes the evidence as relevant to it, ordinary reliability, competency, prejudice, and procedural rules may govern admissibility, but the State may not impose a blanket exclusion whose operation prevents meaningful consideration of competent evidence directly bearing on reasonable doubt concerning that element.

Reasoning: The Constitution supplies no freestanding voluntary-intoxication defense and does not require admission of every relevant item. This case concerns a different combination. Montana required the jury to find purposeful or knowing homicide, and the Montana Supreme Court held that Egelhoff’s intoxication evidence remained relevant to that inquiry. The trial instruction nevertheless removed the entire category from consideration. That rule did not merely regulate cumulative, unreliable, privileged, or procedurally defective proof. It prevented adversarial testing of an element the State retained and correspondingly eased the prosecution’s burden in the face of competent contrary evidence.

Application: Egelhoff presented substantial intoxication evidence directed to the disputed mental-state element. The jury was told that it could not consider his intoxicated condition when deciding whether he acted purposely or knowingly. Because the Montana Supreme Court had already authoritatively classified the elements and the evidence, no further state-law-classification remand is required.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not constitutionalize an intoxication defense, require admission of unreliable or otherwise lawfully excludable evidence, or prevent Montana from prospectively redefining culpability so that voluntary intoxication genuinely has no exculpatory relevance. It does not decide a materially different offense so defined.

Legal basis: The Fourteenth Amendment’s Due Process Clause.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court is joined by Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Breyer, producing an actual five-Justice majority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s distinction between Montana’s broad authority prospectively to define culpability and its inability, after retaining purpose or knowledge and recognizing intoxication evidence as relevant, to prohibit the jury categorically from considering competent evidence directed to that element.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, would sustain the statute based on the historical treatment of voluntary intoxication and the State’s authority to allocate criminal responsibility. Ginsburg would reach the same judgment through the narrower conclusion that the statute substantively removed intoxication from the definition of culpability. Neither proposition controls the simulated decision.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The mens rea principles of *Ratzlaf*, *Staples*, and *X-Citement Video* and the reasonable-doubt requirement reflected in *Victor / Sandoval* are applied without alteration. The decision adds a distinct due-process boundary: a State that retains an element and authoritatively recognizes evidence as competent and relevant to that element may not use a categorical exclusion to prevent the jury from considering the evidence when determining whether the prosecution proved the element.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 5-4. Scalia announced the judgment for the historical Chief Justice, Kennedy, and Thomas, while Ginsburg supplied the fifth vote on a separate offense-definition rationale; O’Connor dissented with Stevens, Souter, and Breyer. In the simulation, Stone rejects the historical Chief Justice’s position and joins O’Connor’s reasoning, converting that four-Justice dissent into a five-Justice Opinion of the Court and producing affirmance. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/37/))

Research sources: The official reported opinions for the offense, instruction, authoritative Montana construction, historical judgment, complete lineup, and competing evidentiary and offense-definition rationales. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/37/))

### *Rise v. Oregon*, 59 F.3d 1556

Decided June 14, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date

From: The Ninth Circuit affirmed summary judgment for Oregon. Chapter 669 required persons convicted of murder or specified sexual offenses to provide a blood sample for a law-enforcement DNA identification databank. Rise and the other covered plaintiffs challenged the extraction, retrospective application, and absence of a pre-extraction hearing. Milligan separately challenged demands and discipline imposed even though his attempted-murder conviction was not a statutory predicate. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/59/1556/496559/))

Questions presented: Whether the suspicionless blood draw and retained DNA profile are reasonable when the program’s central object is investigation of ordinary future crimes; whether covered persons require a separate hearing before extraction; whether retrospective application constitutes punishment; and whether the named defendants were personally responsible for the mistaken application to Milligan.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Vernonia School District 47J v. Acton* authorizes suspicionless testing in the particular non-law-enforcement, school-athletics setting stated there and expressly does not create a general waiver of Fourth Amendment protection. *Schmerber v. California* establishes that compelled blood extraction is a search. *California Department of Corrections v. Morales* governs whether a retrospective change creates a sufficient risk of increased punishment. `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fourth Amendment claim of the covered plaintiffs | Ninth Circuit reversed, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg | Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer | The district court shall determine whether samples were extracted or profiles or biological material remain in state custody and enter only properly sought declaratory or prospective relief necessary to prevent unconstitutional collection or use. |
| Covered plaintiffs’ demand for a separate formal pre-extraction hearing | Ninth Circuit affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | No separate procedural-due-process relief is entered on this record. |
| Ex Post Facto Clause challenge | Ninth Circuit affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | Retrospective application is not invalidated as punishment. |
| Milligan’s claim against Pearce and Knox | Ninth Circuit affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | Judgment remains for the named defendants because personal involvement and causation were not established. |

Overall disposition: Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded.

Destruction, segregation, or nonuse of an existing sample, profile, or retained biological material may be ordered only if properly sought and justified under the governing remedial source. The judgment does not decide damages, qualified immunity, or a claim against an official shown to be responsible for a mistaken noncoverage order.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg in full; Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer as to the procedural-due-process, Ex Post Facto, and Milligan portions | Supports the component-specific judgment | Complete opinion for five Justices; non-Fourth Amendment portions for all nine |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | None | Supports all judgment components | Would reverse on the narrower ground that an ordinary law-enforcement search for evidence of unidentified future crimes cannot rest solely on the subject’s prior conviction |
| Concurrence | Stone | None | Supports all judgment components | States the procedural boundary for a timely, material coverage or exemption dispute |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Kennedy | Thomas, Breyer | Supports the non-Fourth Amendment components; opposes the Fourth Amendment reversal | Would uphold the program as a reasonable identification measure applied to persons convicted of serious predicate offenses |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Suspicionless bodily extraction for ordinary future law enforcement

Holding: Oregon may not compel a blood sample from a person solely because of a predicate conviction and retain the resulting DNA identification profile for investigation of ordinary future crimes without a warrant, probable cause, consent, or another recognized Fourth Amendment exception.

Operative rule: A prior conviction reduces privacy expectations in settings connected to custody, supervision, institutional security, or lawful identification administration, but it does not itself create an unrestricted exception for suspicionless bodily extraction directed to gathering evidence for unknown future criminal investigations. The State must identify a recognized source of Fourth Amendment authority for the search.

Reasoning: The extraction penetrates the body and is therefore a search. Oregon’s central objective is to compare stored profiles with evidence from future crimes, an ordinary law-enforcement purpose rather than the special need beyond law enforcement that governed *Vernonia*. The limited number of qualifying offenses, standardized extraction, and statutory restrictions on secondary analysis reduce the intrusion and risk of misuse. They do not supply the missing constitutional authority for a suspicionless evidence-gathering search. Nor does the analogy to routine fingerprints resolve the bodily intrusion or transform investigation of future crimes into ordinary processing of the conviction already entered.

Application: The covered plaintiffs were selected by conviction category, not because of probable cause, individualized suspicion, a warrant, consent, or an institutional need concerning their present confinement. The Fourth Amendment judgment for Oregon therefore cannot stand.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not prohibit fingerprints, photographs, ordinary booking records, collection pursuant to a warrant or valid consent, searches justified by institutional security or another genuine special need, or use of DNA lawfully obtained from an independent source. It does not decide a nonintrusive identification method or a database created from material already lawfully possessed.

Legal basis: The Fourth Amendment.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s opinion is joined on this issue by Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg, producing an actual five-Justice majority. Scalia concurs in the same judgment on a narrower ordinary-law-enforcement ground, producing a 6-3 judgment.

##### Process before extraction when coverage is undisputed

Holding: Due process does not require a separate formal adversarial hearing before every extraction when statutory coverage rests on an undisputed final conviction and the person raises no genuine dispute concerning identity, statutory coverage, an existing-sample exemption, or another legally operative predicate.

Operative rule: The procedure required before extraction follows the factual or legal error that process can usefully correct. A State need not retry or formally rehear a predicate conclusively established by an uncontested final judgment merely because a bodily search is scheduled.

Reasoning: The covered plaintiffs did not dispute their qualifying convictions or identify another concrete predicate error. A separate hearing would duplicate facts already established without materially reducing the risk of erroneous classification. The lawfulness of the search itself remains governed by the Fourth Amendment and is not converted into a procedural-due-process question.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide the procedure required when a person timely raises a genuine dispute concerning identity, noncoverage, a health exception, an adequate existing sample, or another material predicate.

Legal basis: The Fourteenth Amendment’s Due Process Clause.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices join this portion of O’Connor’s opinion.

##### Retrospective application

Holding: Applying Chapter 669 to persons whose predicate convictions predated enactment does not violate the Ex Post Facto Clause.

Reasoning: The program alters identification administration rather than the offense definition, authorized sentence, parole eligibility, or duration of confinement. Its stated and operative purpose is investigative rather than punitive. The separate Fourth Amendment defect does not convert the measure into increased punishment.

Limits and questions left open: A later law whose design or operation increases confinement, changes the legal consequences of the conviction, or imposes an additional punitive sanction would require separate analysis.

Legal basis: The Ex Post Facto Clause.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices join this portion of O’Connor’s opinion.

##### Milligan and defendant-specific responsibility

Holding: Milligan cannot recover against Pearce or Knox because the summary-judgment record does not connect either defendant personally to the mistaken interpretation that brought his nonqualifying attempted-murder conviction within the statute.

Reasoning: Section 1983 does not impose supervisory liability by title. Milligan was subjected to repeated demands and discipline under a statute that did not cover his conviction, but the uncontroverted record attributed the underlying mistake to another employee and supplied no evidence that Pearce or Knox directed, adopted, maintained, or knowingly failed to correct it.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not authorize extraction from a noncovered person or decide a properly supported procedural-due-process claim against the official responsible for a knowing or persistent mistaken application.

Legal basis: 42 U.S.C. § 1983 and defendant-specific causation principles.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices join this portion of O’Connor’s opinion.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse the Fourth Amendment judgment for the covered plaintiffs; affirm the procedural-due-process and Ex Post Facto judgments; affirm judgment for Pearce and Knox on Milligan’s present claim; remand for properly bounded relief.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full and filed a concurrence.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s distinction between a non-law-enforcement special need and a program directed to ordinary future criminal investigation. He separately states that when a person timely identifies a genuine and material coverage or exemption error and advance correction is feasible, due process requires a meaningful pre-extraction opportunity to obtain correction. The necessary procedure may be a prompt records check or another reliable mechanism rather than a trial-type hearing. That additional proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia agrees that the extraction cannot be justified solely by the prior conviction when its purpose is to obtain evidence for unidentified future crimes. He does not join the Court’s discussion of the informational potential of retained biological material or its broader remedial observations.

Kennedy, joined by Thomas and Breyer, would affirm the Fourth Amendment judgment. The dissent treats the one-time extraction as a minimal intrusion, gives substantial weight to the reduced privacy interests following conviction, and regards the restricted profile as an identification record comparable in function to fingerprinting. That proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Vernonia* is distinguished and left intact. Its suspicionless-testing rule remains confined to a non-law-enforcement program, reduced privacy within interscholastic athletics, restricted use of test results, and the documented school safety and disciplinary interests present there. It does not establish a general convicted-person or ordinary-law-enforcement exception.

The specific-source rule is applied. Fourth Amendment reasonableness governs the bodily search; procedural due process separately governs only the risk of erroneous application of coverage or another material predicate. *Morales* is applied without alteration to the Ex Post Facto claim.

#### Historical Comparison

The Supreme Court did not historically decide *Rise* on the merits. The Ninth Circuit affirmed summary judgment, reasoning that the extraction and restricted DNA profile were minimal intrusions outweighed by the State’s identification and crime-solving interests; Judge D. W. Nelson dissented from the Fourth Amendment ruling because the program’s object was ordinary future law enforcement. The simulation reverses that component 6-3 while affirming the lower court’s remaining judgment components unanimously. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/59/1556/496559/))

Research sources: The published Ninth Circuit majority and dissent for the statutory program, search purpose, covered plaintiffs, Ex Post Facto analysis, hearing claim, Milligan’s noncoverage and disciplinary history, defendant-specific record, corrected docket and date, and historical judgment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/59/1556/496559/))

### Adjudication Paused: *Gray v. Netherland*, 518 U.S. 152

Scheduled decision date: June 20, 1996

The newly supplied Section II retains the same dispositive conflict that produced the earlier pause. Stone’s controlled disposition directs affirmance on the notice-only theory but vacatur and remand on both the suppression and affirmative-deception claims. It treats preservation, exhaustion, procedural default, cause, and prejudice for the *Brady* suppression claim as unresolved matters for the Fourth Circuit. `OT_1995CHUNK10.md`

The official record establishes that the *Brady* claim was not presented on direct appeal or in state habeas proceedings, that its factual basis was available when Gray filed his first state petition, and that Gray did not contest those determinations. Virginia’s successive-petition rule therefore barred future state review, and Gray made no attempt to demonstrate cause or prejudice. The historical Court consequently held the *Brady* claim procedurally defaulted. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/152/))

That verified posture directly conflicts with Stone’s controlled instruction to remand the suppression claim for threshold findings. Correcting the public record while preserving the controlled disposition is impossible: accepting the verified default would change Stone’s formal judgment component, threshold position, and remand instruction, while accepting the brief’s unresolved premise would contradict an authoritative record fact. The separate notice and misrepresentation positions do not cure the defect because the controlled formal disposition treats all three issues within one plenary judgment.

No simulated judgment, vote, opinion, holding, or precedent is entered. The case remains at the post-argument merits stage pending a revised and expressly approved Section II that acknowledges the established default and states Stone’s resulting judgment and remedy for the *Brady* component.

Historically, the Court held the notice-of-evidence theory barred by *Teague*, held the *Brady* claim defaulted, and vacated and remanded for determination of the separately asserted misrepresentation claim and any preserved defenses. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/152/))

Research sources: The official reported opinion for the state and federal habeas record, procedural default, absence of cause and prejudice, distinction among the three claims, historical judgment, and remand concerning alleged affirmative misrepresentation. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/152/))

# Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock

## Section I: Case, record, and procedural posture

* **Case Name:** *Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock*

* **Citation or Docket:** 20 F.3d 858 (8th Cir. 1994), No. 93-1509; historical certiorari denial reported at 513 U.S. 867 (1994). The simulation supplies a separate merits grant but no separate Supreme Court docket number or grant date.

* **October Term:** Carried merits matter from the simulated OT1994 docket into the opening OT1995 Standing State.

* **Entry Stage and Event:** Certiorari granted in the simulation; plenary briefing and oral argument completed. The scheduled merits adjudication was previously paused solely because the controlled brief incorrectly described the district-court posture. This corrected brief supersedes that description.

* **Simulated Event Date:** March 20, 1995, the previously scheduled merits event, subject to revalidation on the corrected record.

* **Question Presented:** Whether Little Rock's adult-business zoning ordinance is a valid content-neutral regulation of secondary effects that leaves reasonable alternative avenues of communication, and whether applying its amortization provisions to existing businesses violates the First Amendment, Due Process Clause, Takings Clause, or Bill of Attainder Clause.

* **Question Granted:** Same as the controlled Question Presented. No limiting order, issue exclusion, consolidation instruction, or separate grant text was supplied.

* **Legal Field:** First Amendment adult-business zoning and secondary effects; content neutrality; alternative channels; existing nonconforming uses and amortization; substantive due process; regulatory takings; Bill of Attainder.

* **Court and Judgment Under Review:** The United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit affirmed the judgment of the Eastern District of Arkansas dismissing Ambassador's constitutional challenge and sustaining Little Rock's ordinance and its application to the existing businesses. ([[Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/20/858/522958/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)][1])

* **Requested Supreme Court Judgment and Relief:** Ambassador seeks reversal and relief against enforcement of the ordinance. Little Rock seeks affirmance. The supplied simulation does not provide more specific petition language.

* **Material Facts and Record:** Little Rock adopted an ordinance restricting sexually oriented businesses from operating within 750 feet of specified sensitive uses, including churches, schools, residential areas, parks, medical facilities, historic properties, and another sexually oriented business. The ordinance did not prohibit such businesses citywide. It provided existing businesses a three-year amortization period and authorized a reasonable extension when necessary to prevent undue financial hardship. Ambassador Books and United Arcade continued operating at prohibited locations after the three-year period and brought this action after receiving notice of enforcement. ([[Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/20/858/522958/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)][1])

  The governing district-court posture is a **bench trial, not summary judgment**. After trial, the district court sustained the ordinance. It found that approximately 6.75 percent of the relevant area remained available and that 97 relocation sites existed; United Arcade in fact obtained a conditional-use permit for a permitted location. The district court also regarded the three-year amortization period as reasonable. Those subsidiary factual findings were part of the tried record. ([[Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/20/858/522958/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)][1])

  The purpose record was mixed. The City received complaints concerning adult businesses, studied reports from other municipalities associating concentrations of adult businesses with crime, property-value effects, and neighborhood deterioration, held public proceedings, and adopted an ordinance expressly directed at secondary effects. The record also included a City Attorney's early handwritten instruction stating that he wanted to shut the businesses down somehow, and the district court expressed the view that the City's predominant intent was suppression based on content rather than secondary effects. The Eighth Circuit nevertheless concluded that the ordinance was constitutionally content neutral. ([[Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/20/858/522958/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)][1])

* **Preservation, Threshold, and Vehicle Matters:** The prior source-reconciliation blocker is resolved. The authoritative lower-court record establishes a bench trial. Under Rule 52 principles, ordinary subsidiary historical findings receive clear-error treatment; the ultimate First Amendment classification, constitutional significance of the purpose evidence, and application of constitutional standards receive independent appellate review. *Bose Corp. v. Consumers Union of United States, Inc.* supplies the preexisting First Amendment independent-review principle. The Court therefore accepts supported historical findings without treating the district court's ultimate constitutional characterization of governmental purpose as dispositive. All four constitutional theories identified in the controlled grant are sufficiently before the Court for Stone to state a complete position.

* **Relevant Dates:** Eighth Circuit submission, November 10, 1993; Eighth Circuit decision, April 1, 1994; historical certiorari denial, October 3, 1994; simulated oral argument, January 10, 1995; scheduled simulated merits event, March 20, 1995.

* **Companion or Consolidated Matters:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. and Allan Dunlap, doing business as United Arcade, were appellants in the same lower-court proceeding. No separate Supreme Court companion is identified.

* **Known Participation Matter:** None identified.

* **Public Sources:** Controlled Standing State for the simulated grant, question, argument history, and carried merits posture; 20 F.3d 858 for the bench trial, ordinance, evidentiary record, findings, constitutional claims, and Eighth Circuit judgment. The published opinion confirms that the district court acted after trial and identifies the 97-site finding, three-year amortization provision, purpose evidence, and disposition. ([[Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/20/858/522958/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)][1])

### Historical Supreme Court comparator

* **Historical Outcome and Vote:** Certiorari denied. No historical Supreme Court merits judgment exists.
* **Historical Opinion Structure:** No merits opinion.
* **Historical Holding:** None. The historical Supreme Court did not adopt the Eighth Circuit's reasoning as a Supreme Court holding.
* **Historical Authority:** The Eighth Circuit judgment, 20 F.3d 858, is the historical merits comparator only.
* **Historical Sources:** 20 F.3d 858; reported certiorari denial at 513 U.S. 867.

## Section II: Entering law and Stone's position

**Approval Status:** Draft for express approval; this Section II is not controlling or frozen until approved.

### A. Law entering the case

* **Current Through:** Simulated law effective immediately before the March 20, 1995 merits event.
* **Earlier Same-Term Decisions:** No completed earlier same-term decision materially changes the adult-business-zoning questions presented here.
* **Unresolved Same-Term Dependencies:** None identified.

#### Issue 1: Content neutrality, secondary effects, and governmental purpose

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** *Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. FCC* requires government to establish an important or substantial interest unrelated to suppression of expression and a reasonable fit when a regulation is properly classified as content neutral. *City of Ladue v. Gilleo* establishes that communicative alternatives must be meaningful rather than merely formal. *Madsen v. Women's Health Center, Inc.* confirms the Stone Court's insistence that speech restrictions be justified by the actual governmental interests and record supporting the particular restraint, while its heightened injunction-specific standard does not itself govern this generally applicable ordinance.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** **Cable must-carry classification and intermediate scrutiny** supplies the current general content-neutral scrutiny framework. **Near-total elimination of the residential-sign medium** and *Madsen*'s treatment of realistic alternatives reinforce the requirement that claimed alternative channels be genuine. No separate registered adult-business-zoning standard has displaced the surviving pre-1993 secondary-effects cases.
* **Other Governing Law:** *City of Renton v. Playtime Theatres, Inc.*; *Young v. American Mini Theatres, Inc.*; *Ward v. Rock Against Racism*; *United States v. O'Brien*; *Bose Corp. v. Consumers Union*.
* **Other Authority Used:** The ordinance's enacted text, studies, public proceedings, City Attorney materials, alternatives considered by the City, district-court findings, and actual operation of the ordinance.
* **Open Point:** How the secondary-effects doctrine operates when the record contains evidence of both legitimate land-use concerns and a desire by a government participant to suppress the regulated businesses because of their expressive content.

#### Issue 2: Alternative avenues and application of the amortization provision to existing businesses

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** *City of Ladue* rejects nominal substitutes that do not realistically preserve the protected medium. *Turner* requires a reasonable fit between the established interest and the burden placed on protected expression.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** **Near-total elimination of the residential-sign medium**, by analogy as to realistic alternatives; **Cable must-carry classification and intermediate scrutiny** as to fit. Neither module creates a right to a preferred commercial site.
* **Other Governing Law:** *Renton*; *Ward*; traditional time, place, and manner principles.
* **Other Authority Used:** The trial findings concerning approximately 97 available sites, the proportion of land available, United Arcade's conditional-use permit, the three-year transition period, and the hardship-extension provision. ([[Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/20/858/522958/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)][1])
* **Open Point:** Whether relocation alternatives satisfying *Renton* are nevertheless constitutionally inadequate because of their practical or economic characteristics.

#### Issue 3: Substantive due process

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** No later simulated holding creates a special substantive-due-process protection against ordinary prospective land-use regulation of an existing commercial operation.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** No exact registered standard beyond ordinary source discipline and the requirement that government action have lawful authority and a nonarbitrary relationship to a legitimate public purpose.
* **Other Governing Law:** *Village of Euclid v. Ambler Realty Co.* and ordinary substantive-due-process limits on land-use regulation.
* **Other Authority Used:** The prospective operation of the zoning rule, transition period, hardship-extension mechanism, and secondary-effects record.
* **Open Point:** None material after the First Amendment analysis.

#### Issue 4: Regulatory taking or inverse condemnation

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** Simulated *Dolan v. City of Tigard* confirms that its heightened exaction rule does not govern ordinary zoning restrictions. Stone's property framework distinguishes physical appropriation, total deprivation of economic use, and ordinary regulatory burdens.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** The registered exaction rule does not apply because Little Rock demanded no conveyance of property as a permit condition.
* **Other Governing Law:** *Penn Central Transportation Co. v. New York City*; *Lucas v. South Carolina Coastal Council*; ordinary regulatory-takings principles.
* **Other Authority Used:** The ordinance regulates location rather than appropriating property, permits continued operation elsewhere, and supplied a transition and hardship process.
* **Open Point:** None requiring a separate remand on this record.

#### Issue 5: Bill of Attainder

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** No simulated decision has altered the received requirement that a bill of attainder involve legislative specification together with punishment imposed without judicial trial.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** No exact registered module.
* **Other Governing Law:** *United States v. Brown*; *Nixon v. Administrator of General Services*; *Fleming v. Nestor*; *United States v. O'Brien*.
* **Other Authority Used:** The prospective, generally defined zoning category; nonpunitive land-use objectives; transition period; and hardship extension.
* **Open Point:** None.

### B. Stone's position

* **Stone's Controlled Judgment or Disposition:** **AFFIRM the judgment of the Eighth Circuit.**
* **Stone's Remedy and Remand Instruction:** The judgment rejecting Ambassador's constitutional challenge remains in force. No remand is required. Stone's affirmance rests on a narrower First Amendment rationale than the Eighth Circuit's insofar as that court suggested that illicit governmental purpose is categorically irrelevant to content-neutrality analysis.

#### Issue 1: Content neutrality, secondary effects, and governmental purpose

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone reaches the First Amendment merits on the corrected bench-trial record. He gives ordinary clear-error respect to subsidiary historical findings but independently determines the constitutional classification and significance of the purpose evidence.

* **Stone's Legal Position:** The secondary-effects doctrine survives, but it is not a license to ignore actual governmental purpose. A municipality may zone adult businesses to address crime, neighborhood deterioration, property effects, concentration, traffic, and comparable secondary consequences. A regulation does not become content based merely because the affected businesses are identified by the sexually explicit character of their protected expression when the location rule is genuinely justified by those secondary effects.

  At the same time, Stone rejects any categorical proposition that a government may deliberately suppress protected expression and obtain content-neutral treatment merely by adding secondary-effects findings to the ordinance. Neutrality is assessed from the enacted text, purpose, design, evidence considered by the decisionmakers, alternatives selected or rejected, and actual operation. A proved purpose to suppress the message itself can therefore defeat secondary-effects treatment.

* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** An adult-business zoning ordinance is governed as a content-neutral place regulation when the record, independently reviewed, establishes that the adopted land-use restriction is genuinely directed at adverse secondary effects rather than suppression of the protected message. Evidence of illicit purpose is relevant and may defeat that classification, but the existence of an improper statement by one governmental participant does not itself establish that the legislative body adopted the ordinance to suppress expression.

* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** The City Attorney's statement that he wanted to shut the businesses down is serious evidence and cannot be dismissed merely because courts traditionally hesitate to investigate legislative motive. Stone considers it. He also considers the broader enactment record.

  That broader record establishes citizen complaints, research concerning crime and property effects associated with concentrations of adult businesses, reliance on studies from other municipalities, public proceedings, and an ordinance structured as a location rule rather than a prohibition. Little Rock considered a 1,000-foot rule but rejected it as unnecessarily restrictive and also rejected a 500-foot rule as insufficient to meet the identified land-use concerns. The adopted ordinance expressly preserved adult access to sexually oriented expression and left locations in which the businesses could operate. ([[Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/20/858/522958/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)][1])

  The City Attorney was an important participant, but he was not the legislative body. His initial suppressive statement is outweighed, for constitutional-classification purposes, by the enacted design and the broader evidence showing that the Board ultimately chose a land-use response to identified secondary effects rather than a prohibition on adult expression. Stone therefore does not adopt the district court's ultimate characterization of the City's "predominant intent." That characterization concerns the constitutional significance of the enactment record and is subject to independent First Amendment review.

  Stone accordingly reaches the same classification as the Eighth Circuit, but **not** because improper purpose is constitutionally irrelevant. He reaches it because the complete record does not establish that suppression of protected content materially controlled the Board's adopted ordinance.

* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone does not hold that a municipality can convert censorship into a content-neutral regulation by reciting secondary effects, commissioning post hoc studies, or hiding a content-suppressive design behind zoning terminology. A materially different record showing deliberate suppression, pretextual findings, discriminatory administration, or a practical ban would receive different treatment.

#### Issue 2: Alternative avenues and application of the amortization provision to existing businesses

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Because Stone classifies the ordinance as content neutral, he reaches whether the location restrictions and application to existing businesses leave constitutionally adequate alternative channels.

* **Stone's Legal Position:** A city need not guarantee an adult business its present location, preferred neighborhood, cheapest parcel, or commercially optimal site. It must, however, leave **ample and realistic** avenues through which the protected expression can remain available. Stone therefore rejects any categorical statement that cost and practical availability are always constitutionally irrelevant. Government may not satisfy the First Amendment by counting parcels that are legally listed but functionally unavailable.

  Commercial inconvenience, relocation expense, or the inability to obtain a particular preferred site does not by itself establish a constitutional denial. The relevant question is whether the ordinance, in practical operation, leaves a meaningful field of lawful locations for the protected businesses.

* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** Little Rock's ordinance leaves adequate alternative avenues of communication on this tried record, and its three-year amortization period, coupled with a hardship-extension mechanism, may constitutionally be applied to existing adult businesses.

* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** The district court found that approximately 6.75 percent of the relevant area and 97 relocation sites remained available. Those are subsidiary trial findings, and Ambassador has not established clear error in them. United Arcade's successful application for a conditional-use permit supplies additional concrete evidence that the permitted locations were not entirely theoretical. ([[Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/20/858/522958/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)][1])

  Stone does not adopt the Eighth Circuit's suggestion that relocation cost is categorically unimportant. Cost can matter when it demonstrates that supposed alternatives are illusory. But the record identified here does not establish that the permitted sites as a class were economically or practically inaccessible, nor that Little Rock had reduced protected adult expression to a nominal right without a realistic place to operate.

  Application to the existing businesses also does not create a separate First Amendment violation. The ordinance did not order immediate closure. It provided three years of continued operation at the nonconforming locations and authorized additional time where necessary to avoid undue financial hardship. The First Amendment does not create a perpetual grandfather right to one commercial address when a valid place regulation leaves realistic alternative channels.

* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone does not establish a universally sufficient percentage of municipal land, a universally sufficient number of sites, or a universally valid amortization period. A different ordinance may fail where nominal sites are unavailable because of zoning, title, infrastructure, prohibitive practical barriers, discretionary licensing, or other conditions making relocation unrealistic.

#### Issue 3: Substantive due process

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone reaches the separately granted Due Process theory concerning application of the ordinance to existing businesses.

* **Stone's Legal Position:** A lawful land-use regulation does not violate substantive due process merely because it alters the permissible future use of property or requires an existing commercial operation to relocate. The Constitution protects against arbitrary governmental action; it does not constitutionalize perpetual immunity from prospective zoning change.

* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** Application of Little Rock's ordinance to businesses operating before enactment does not violate substantive due process where the location rule bears a substantial relationship to legitimate land-use interests and provides a substantial transition period with an opportunity for hardship extension.

* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** The ordinance serves recognized interests in limiting identified secondary effects and does not immediately extinguish the businesses. The three-year transition and hardship provision materially weaken any claim of arbitrary treatment. Because the First Amendment's more speech-specific protections are independently satisfied on this record, the Due Process Clause supplies no additional basis for invalidating the same prospective land-use application. ([[Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/20/858/522958/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)][1])

* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone does not approve arbitrary retroactive confiscation, standardless denial of a hardship extension, selective enforcement, or a land-use measure whose asserted public purpose is pretextual.

#### Issue 4: Regulatory taking or inverse condemnation

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone reaches the takings theory independently rather than treating the First Amendment ruling as automatically dispositive of property rights.

* **Stone's Legal Position:** The ordinance creates no physical appropriation and does not, on the record presented, eliminate all economically beneficial use of the affected property. It is therefore not a categorical taking. Ordinary regulatory-takings principles govern any remaining claim, including economic impact, reasonable investment-backed expectations, the character and duration of the governmental action, and the property interest considered as a whole.

* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** Ambassador has not established a compensable taking merely because the zoning ordinance prevents continued operation of an adult business at the same location after the transition period.

* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** Little Rock has neither occupied Ambassador's property nor appropriated it for public use. The regulation concerns the location of one particular commercial use. The businesses may operate at lawful alternative sites, and the challenged locations are not shown to have been deprived of all economically beneficial use. The three-year transition and hardship-extension mechanism further distinguish the ordinance from an immediate confiscatory command. The record identified in the judgment under review therefore does not establish the constitutional equivalent of an appropriation.

* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone does not hold that zoning can never effect a taking. A parcel-specific case involving elimination of all economic use, an appropriation, a compelled conveyance, or a sufficiently severe regulatory burden under the ordinary multifactor inquiry remains governed by the applicable takings doctrine.

#### Issue 5: Bill of Attainder

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone reaches the independently granted Bill of Attainder claim.

* **Stone's Legal Position:** Legislative specificity alone is insufficient. The constitutional prohibition requires punishment imposed legislatively on identified persons or an ascertainable class without judicial trial. A prospective regulatory classification serving a genuine nonpunitive land-use function is not transformed into attainder because its burdens fall on a readily identifiable industry.

* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** Little Rock's ordinance is not a bill of attainder because its zoning and amortization provisions regulate the prospective location of a class of businesses for nonpunitive land-use purposes and do not legislatively impose punishment for past conduct.

* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** The ordinance identifies sexually oriented businesses because that is the land-use category whose asserted secondary effects the City sought to regulate. It does not declare Ambassador guilty of misconduct, impose a criminal or historically punitive sanction, or punish the businesses for completed acts. Its three-year transition period and hardship extension are regulatory features inconsistent with a legislative determination of guilt and punishment. The fact that the City knew which existing businesses would be affected does not supply the missing punitive element. ([[Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/20/858/522958/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)][1])

* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone does not foreclose a Bill of Attainder challenge to nominally civil legislation that singles out identified persons or a closed class and, in purpose and operation, imposes punishment for past conduct without judicial adjudication.

[1]: https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/20/858/522958/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Ambassador Books & Video, Inc.; Allan Dunlap, Doingbusiness As United Arcade, Appellants, v. City of Little Rock, Arkansas, Defendant-appellee, 20 F.3d 858 (8th Cir. 1994) :: Justia"          ## Section II: Entering law and Stone's position

**Approval Status:** Draft for express approval; this Section II is not controlling or frozen until approved.

### A. Law entering the case

* **Current Through:** Law effective immediately before the June 20, 1996 simulated decision event.
* **Earlier Same-Term Decisions:** No earlier same-term decision alters the procedural-default rule controlling Gray's suppression claim. Existing simulated *Kyles* doctrine governs *Brady* materiality only when a suppression claim is properly before the reviewing court. Existing habeas doctrine continues to require claim-specific treatment of exhaustion, procedural default, cause, prejudice, and any applicable innocence gateway.
* **Unresolved Same-Term Dependencies:** None.

#### Issue 1: New advance-notice theory

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** No controlling authority when Gray's conviction became final established a general constitutional right to item-by-item advance notice of all evidence the prosecution intended to introduce at a capital-sentencing proceeding.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** No exact registered standard. *Teague v. Lane* bars federal habeas relief resting on a new constitutional rule unless a recognized exception applies.
* **Other Governing Law:** *Gardner v. Florida*; existing capital due-process principles; federal habeas nonretroactivity doctrine.
* **Other Authority Used:** The constitutional theory Gray presented, the date his conviction became final, and the state of governing precedent at that time.
* **Open Point:** None material to the notice-only theory.

#### Issue 2: Brady suppression claim and procedural default

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** *Kyles v. Whitley* supplies the governing substantive rule for cumulative *Brady* materiality when a suppression claim is properly before a reviewing court. It does not eliminate ordinary habeas procedural bars. Existing procedural-default doctrine requires a federal habeas petitioner who failed to comply with an adequate state procedural rule to establish a recognized basis for overcoming the default before obtaining merits review.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** **Cumulative Brady materiality and prosecution-team responsibility** remains controlling substantive law but is not reached unless the procedural bar is overcome. **Actual innocence as a gateway to defaulted trial claims** under *Schlup v. Delo* permits merits review only upon the required showing and does not itself establish the underlying constitutional claim.
* **Other Governing Law:** *Brady v. Maryland*; *Wainwright v. Sykes*; *Murray v. Carrier*; *Coleman v. Thompson*; *Schlup v. Delo*.
* **Other Authority Used:** The established state and federal habeas record showing that Gray did not present the suppression claim on direct appeal or in his first state habeas proceeding, possessed the factual basis for the claim when that state petition was filed, and was thereafter barred by Virginia's successive-petition rule.
* **Open Point:** None requiring remand on the suppression claim. The record establishes the procedural default, and Gray did not establish cause and prejudice or invoke a successful gateway permitting federal merits review.

#### Issue 3: Affirmative prosecutorial deception or misrepresentation

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** Existing due-process doctrine independently prohibits deliberate governmental deception that materially deprives a criminal defendant of a fair opportunity to defend. Such a claim is analytically distinct from both a general advance-notice claim and a *Brady* suppression claim and must be evaluated under its own source, elements, and habeas thresholds.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** No exact registered standard displaces the existing due-process rule against affirmative prosecutorial deception.
* **Other Governing Law:** *Mooney v. Holohan*; *Napue v. Illinois*; *Raley v. Ohio*; ordinary exhaustion and procedural-default doctrine.
* **Other Authority Used:** The prosecution's pretrial representations concerning the Sorrell evidence, the evidence actually introduced at sentencing, Gray's objection to the changed presentation, and the separate manner in which Gray later characterized the alleged affirmative misrepresentation.
* **Open Point:** Whether the affirmative-deception claim was fairly presented and exhausted, whether any procedural defense specifically applicable to that claim remains available, and, if the claim is properly before the federal court, whether the prosecution made a materially misleading representation producing constitutional prejudice.

### B. Stone's position

* **Stone's Controlled Judgment or Disposition:** **AFFIRM IN PART, VACATE IN PART, AND REMAND.**
* **Stone's Remedy and Remand Instruction:** **AFFIRM** the judgment insofar as it denies federal habeas relief on Gray's new advance-notice theory and on his procedurally defaulted *Brady* suppression claim. **VACATE** the judgment only insofar as the Fourth Circuit failed separately to resolve Gray's affirmative-deception or misrepresentation claim. **REMAND solely for consideration of that distinct claim and any threshold defenses applicable specifically to it. The *Brady* suppression claim is not reopened on remand.** A new penalty proceeding may be ordered only if the affirmative-deception claim is properly before the court and satisfies its governing constitutional and habeas standards.

#### Issue 1: New advance-notice theory

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone resolves the habeas nonretroactivity threshold and does not announce an advisory prospective rule concerning capital-sentencing discovery.
* **Stone's Legal Position:** A general constitutional right to advance notice of every witness, item of evidence, or particular evidentiary detail the prosecution intends to use at capital sentencing was not dictated by existing precedent when Gray's conviction became final. Federal habeas therefore cannot create that rule and apply it retroactively in this case.
* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** Gray's notice-only theory is unavailable on federal habeas under *Teague* because it would require recognition of a new constitutional rule not dictated by precedent when his conviction became final.
* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** Existing precedent protected defendants against secret evidence of the kind condemned in *Gardner*, unconstitutional suppression, false evidence, and affirmative governmental deception. Those protections did not establish the substantially broader proposition that due process requires advance item-by-item disclosure of all nonstatutory evidence the prosecution intends to introduce at a capital penalty proceeding. Gray therefore cannot obtain habeas relief on that newly formulated notice theory.
* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone does not decide whether a jurisdiction may prospectively create additional statutory or procedural notice protections, whether a materially different use of genuinely secret evidence would violate existing law, or whether suppression or affirmative deception violates independently established constitutional rules.

#### Issue 2: Brady suppression claim and procedural default

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone resolves procedural default before reaching *Brady* materiality. The official record establishes the relevant threshold facts, so no further remand for preservation, exhaustion, default, cause, or prejudice is warranted.
* **Stone's Legal Position:** Federal habeas review does not permit a court to disregard an established state procedural default merely because the underlying constitutional theory would otherwise arise under *Brady*. Gray failed to present the suppression claim on direct appeal or in his first Virginia habeas proceeding even though he possessed the factual basis necessary to do so. Virginia's successive-petition rule thereafter barred presentation of that claim. Gray has not established cause and prejudice sufficient to overcome the resulting default, and the record supplies no successful *Schlup* gateway requiring merits review.
* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** Gray's *Brady* suppression claim is procedurally defaulted because he failed to present it in the available state proceedings despite possessing its factual basis, Virginia law forecloses later presentation, and he has not established a recognized basis for overcoming that default.
* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** The procedural posture is no longer uncertain. Gray knew the facts underlying the asserted suppression when he filed his first state habeas petition yet did not raise the constitutional claim there. The state successive-petition rule consequently prevents a return to state court, and Gray made no sufficient cause-and-prejudice showing permitting the federal courts to bypass that failure. Because the procedural bar independently prevents federal merits review, Stone does **not** decide whether the Sorrell material was favorable, suppressed, or cumulatively material under *Kyles*. The correct judgment on this component is therefore affirmance of the denial of habeas relief, not vacatur for additional threshold findings.
* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone expresses no view on the substantive *Brady* materiality of the Sorrell evidence. *Kyles* remains fully controlling when a suppression claim is properly before the court. This decision neither weakens the prosecution's disclosure obligation nor treats procedural default as a merits rejection of the underlying constitutional claim.

#### Issue 3: Affirmative prosecutorial deception or misrepresentation

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone treats this as a claim distinct from both the barred notice theory and the defaulted *Brady* suppression claim. The present record does not permit the Supreme Court to resolve in the first instance every preservation, exhaustion, procedural-defense, factual, and prejudice question associated specifically with the alleged affirmative misrepresentation.
* **Stone's Legal Position:** A defendant who cannot obtain relief on a newly asserted general notice right does not thereby lose the protection of older due-process rules against affirmative governmental deception. Likewise, procedural default of a *Brady* suppression claim does not automatically establish default of a separately pleaded claim that the prosecution affirmatively misled the defense about the evidence it intended to present. The claims depend on different governmental conduct and different legal predicates and therefore require claim-specific threshold treatment.
* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** Gray's affirmative-deception claim must be separately classified and considered under the preexisting due-process rule governing materially misleading prosecutorial conduct, rather than collapsed into either his new advance-notice theory or his procedurally defaulted *Brady* claim.
* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** Gray alleges more than failure to provide advance notice or nondisclosure of favorable evidence. He contends that the prosecution affirmatively represented that its Sorrell presentation would consist of his alleged admissions and then materially changed the evidentiary presentation after conviction. If properly preserved and proven, deliberate governmental misrepresentation that materially impaired the defense's ability to respond may implicate established due-process principles without creation of the new notice rule barred by *Teague*. But the lower courts must first determine the threshold posture of **that particular claim**, including whether it was fairly presented and exhausted and whether any preserved procedural bar applies. If the claim survives those thresholds, the court must then determine whether an affirmative misrepresentation actually occurred and whether it produced constitutionally sufficient prejudice.
* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone does not hold that every inaccurate prosecutorial prediction, change in trial strategy, late disclosure, or failure to provide a witness list constitutes unconstitutional deception. Relief requires a properly presented claim involving materially misleading governmental conduct that satisfies the applicable prejudice standard. The remand concerns only this distinct affirmative-deception claim and does not reopen either the notice-only theory or the procedurally defaulted *Brady* suppression claim.

## Decisions and Dispositions

### *Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock*, 20 F.3d 858

Argued January 10, 1995 | Decided March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date

From: After a bench trial, the Eastern District of Arkansas sustained Little Rock’s adult-business zoning ordinance. The Eighth Circuit affirmed. The ordinance established 750-foot buffers around specified sensitive uses, permitted existing businesses to continue for three years, and authorized reasonable hardship extensions. The trial record included the City Attorney’s early statement that he wanted to shut the businesses down, the City’s study of secondary-effects evidence from other municipalities, public proceedings, the rejection of both 1,000-foot and 500-foot alternatives, and findings that 97 relocation sites remained available. ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/020/20.F3d.858.93-1509.html))

Questions presented: Whether the ordinance is a valid content-neutral regulation of secondary effects that leaves reasonable alternative avenues of communication, and whether its application to existing businesses violates the First Amendment, substantive due process, the Takings Clause, or the Bill of Attainder Clause.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. FCC* supplies the general scrutiny applicable to a properly classified content-neutral speech regulation. *City of Ladue v. Gilleo* requires communicative alternatives to be realistic rather than nominal. *Madsen v. Women’s Health Center, Inc.* reinforces record-based review of governmental justification but does not extend its injunction-specific standard to this generally applicable ordinance. Simulated *Dolan v. City of Tigard* does not govern because Little Rock demanded no conveyance or exaction. `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.

Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

The judgment dismissing Ambassador’s constitutional challenge remains in force. No remand is required.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer in full; Scalia and Thomas as to the alternative-site conclusion, amortization, substantive-due-process, takings, and bill-of-attainder holdings | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for seven Justices; specified portions for Scalia and Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Supports affirmance | Would classify the ordinance principally from its enacted design and objective operation and would not make subjective legislative motive or commercial attractiveness independent constitutional inquiries |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Secondary effects, governmental purpose, and content neutrality

Holding: Little Rock’s ordinance is a content-neutral place regulation because the complete enactment record, independently reviewed, establishes that the adopted restriction was genuinely directed at crime, property effects, neighborhood deterioration, concentration, and comparable secondary consequences rather than suppression of the protected message.

Operative rule: An adult-business zoning ordinance receives content-neutral treatment only when its enacted design and actual justification are genuinely directed at adverse secondary effects. Courts consider the text, enactment record, evidence before the decisionmakers, alternatives selected or rejected, and practical operation. Evidence that suppression of protected expression materially controlled the governmental decision may defeat secondary-effects classification. An improper statement by one participant does not establish that conclusion by itself.

Reasoning: The City Attorney’s statement that he wanted to shut the businesses down is relevant and serious evidence. It cannot be disregarded under a categorical rule that illicit purpose never matters. The constitutional inquiry, however, concerns the governmental action actually adopted. The Board received citizen complaints, considered studies concerning crime and property effects, held public proceedings, rejected a broader 1,000-foot restriction, rejected a narrower 500-foot restriction as insufficient, and enacted a location rule that continued to permit adult businesses within the city. The City Attorney was not the legislative body, and the record does not establish that his initial objective materially controlled the Board’s final action.

Historical facts concerning what officials said, studied, and enacted receive ordinary clear-error respect. Whether those facts establish a content-neutral governmental purpose is an ultimate First Amendment determination subject to independent appellate review.

Limits and questions left open: Government may not convert censorship into a content-neutral regulation by reciting secondary effects, commissioning post hoc studies, or disguising a practical prohibition as zoning. A materially different record showing pretext, discriminatory administration, deliberate expressive suppression, or a practical ban remains subject to content-based review.

Legal basis: The First and Fourteenth Amendments.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s opinion is joined on this proposition by Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing a seven-Justice majority. Scalia and Thomas support the judgment on a different account of motive.

##### Alternative avenues and application to existing businesses

Holding: The ordinance leaves ample and realistic alternative avenues of communication, and its three-year amortization period, coupled with a hardship-extension mechanism, may constitutionally be applied to Ambassador’s existing businesses.

Operative rule: A municipality need not guarantee an adult business its current address, preferred neighborhood, least expensive parcel, or commercially optimal site. It must leave a meaningful field of lawful locations in practical operation. Relocation expense and economic conditions matter when they demonstrate that nominally available sites are functionally unavailable, but ordinary commercial inconvenience does not establish a First Amendment violation.

Reasoning: The district court found that approximately 6.75 percent of the relevant area and 97 relocation sites remained available. United Arcade obtained a conditional-use permit for a permissible site. Ambassador did not establish that the sites as a class were inaccessible because of title, zoning, infrastructure, licensing, or prohibitive practical barriers. The ordinance also allowed the existing businesses three years at their current locations and authorized additional time to prevent undue financial hardship. Those provisions distinguish the ordinance from immediate closure or nominal relocation rights. ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/020/20.F3d.858.93-1509.html))

Limits and questions left open: The Court establishes no universally sufficient acreage, number of sites, or amortization period. A site count fails when the listed parcels are unavailable in practice.

Legal basis: The First Amendment.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support the case-specific conclusion. The seven-Justice Court majority supports the stated treatment of practical availability and relocation cost.

##### Substantive due process

Holding: Application of the ordinance to businesses operating before enactment does not violate substantive due process.

Reasoning: The ordinance bears a substantial relationship to legitimate land-use interests, regulates prospective location rather than imposing liability for past operation, and provides both a substantial transition period and a mechanism for hardship relief. An existing commercial use possesses no substantive constitutional immunity from an otherwise lawful prospective zoning change.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not approve arbitrary confiscation, standardless denial of a hardship extension, selective enforcement, or a pretextual land-use measure.

Legal basis: The Fourteenth Amendment’s Due Process Clause.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support this holding.

##### Regulatory taking

Holding: Ambassador has not established a compensable taking merely because the ordinance prevents continued operation of an adult business at the same location after the transition period.

Reasoning: Little Rock neither physically occupied the property nor compelled a conveyance. The ordinance regulates one commercial use and does not eliminate all economically beneficial use of the affected property. The record also does not establish a regulatory burden sufficient to require compensation under the ordinary multifactor inquiry.

Limits and questions left open: A parcel-specific claim involving physical appropriation, total deprivation, compelled dedication, or a sufficiently severe burden under ordinary regulatory-takings principles remains open.

Legal basis: The Takings Clause, applied to the States through the Fourteenth Amendment.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support this holding.

##### Bill of attainder

Holding: The ordinance is not a bill of attainder.

Reasoning: Although it defines an ascertainable class of businesses, it prospectively regulates location for nonpunitive land-use purposes. It does not declare Ambassador guilty of misconduct, impose a historically punitive sanction, or punish completed conduct without judicial trial. The transition and hardship provisions further confirm its regulatory operation.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not foreclose a challenge to nominally civil legislation that identifies persons or a closed class and imposes punishment for past conduct without adjudication.

Legal basis: Article I, § 10.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support this holding.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joined O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone publicly joins the Court’s conclusion that actual governmental purpose is relevant to secondary-effects classification, its independent review of the complete enactment record, and its insistence that alternative locations be realistic rather than merely listed. He announces no additional ground.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, agrees that the ordinance is constitutional and that the remaining claims fail. The concurrence would not invalidate an otherwise lawful place regulation through an inquiry into subjective legislative motive and would treat commercial cost as outside the alternative-channel analysis unless the ordinance itself legally foreclosed operation.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*City of Renton v. Playtime Theatres, Inc.* is clarified. The secondary-effects doctrine does not make actual content-suppressive purpose categorically irrelevant, but an isolated improper statement does not overcome an enacted design and record genuinely directed to secondary effects.

*Turner Broadcasting* is applied to the important-interest and fit inquiry without alteration. *City of Ladue* is applied to require realistic alternatives without creating a fixed acreage or site-count safe harbor. *Madsen* remains confined to injunctions. Simulated *Dolan* is distinguished because ordinary zoning without a compelled conveyance is not an exaction.

#### Historical Comparison

Historically, the Supreme Court denied certiorari at 513 U.S. 867, leaving the Eighth Circuit judgment in force without creating Supreme Court precedent. The simulation reaches the merits and unanimously affirms, while rejecting the Eighth Circuit’s broad suggestion that suppressive governmental purpose is categorically irrelevant and its categorical treatment of relocation cost. ([U.S. Court of Appeals](https://ecf.ca8.uscourts.gov/opndir/02/03/011869P.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Research sources: The published Eighth Circuit opinion for the bench-trial posture, ordinance, enactment evidence, trial findings, available sites, amortization provision, lower-court judgment, and constitutional claims; the reported certiorari denial for the historical comparator. ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/020/20.F3d.858.93-1509.html))
