### *Gray v. Netherland*, 518 U.S. 152

Argued April 15, 1996 | Decided June 20, 1996

From: Before Gray’s capital trial, the prosecution represented that its Sorrell-murders presentation would consist of statements Gray allegedly made to other inmates. After the guilty verdict, it disclosed that it would also introduce crime-scene and autopsy photographs and testimony from an investigating detective and medical examiner. Gray later advanced three distinct theories: a general notice-of-evidence claim, a *Brady* suppression claim, and an affirmative-misrepresentation claim. The established habeas record shows that the factual basis of the *Brady* theory was available when Gray filed his first state petition, that Virginia law barred a later petition, and that Gray made no cause-and-prejudice showing. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/152/case.pdf))

Questions presented: Whether Gray’s general advance-notice theory requires a new constitutional rule barred by *Teague*; whether his *Brady* claim is procedurally defaulted; and whether the lower courts separately considered his claim that the prosecution affirmatively misled the defense.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Kyles v. Whitley* and earlier same-term *Wood v. Bartholomew* govern cumulative *Brady* materiality only when the suppression claim is properly before the reviewing court. Existing habeas doctrine independently governs exhaustion, procedural default, cause, prejudice, and any innocence gateway. `OT_1995CHUNK1.md` `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded, 5-4.

Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Fourth Circuit is affirmed insofar as it denies relief on Gray’s notice-only theory and procedurally defaulted *Brady* claim. Its judgment is vacated only insofar as it failed separately to resolve the affirmative-deception or misrepresentation claim.

On remand, the Fourth Circuit shall determine whether that distinct claim was fairly presented and exhausted and whether the Commonwealth preserved any claim-specific procedural defense. It shall reach the merits only if the claim survives those thresholds. The *Brady* suppression claim is not reopened. A new penalty proceeding may be ordered only if the misrepresentation claim is properly before the court and satisfies its governing constitutional and habeas standards.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports affirmance in part, vacatur in part, and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | Stevens, Souter, Breyer | Opposes denial of immediate penalty-phase relief | Would treat Gray’s due-process complaint as an established right to a meaningful opportunity to answer the State’s capital-sentencing evidence rather than as a new discovery rule |
| Separate dissent | Stevens | None | Opposes the Court’s judgment and joins Ginsburg’s dissent | Would additionally hold that the unsupported use of the unadjudicated Sorrell murders rendered the sentencing proceeding fundamentally unfair |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Procedural default of the Brady claim

Holding: Gray’s *Brady* suppression claim is procedurally defaulted and is not cognizable in federal habeas because he failed to present it in the available state proceedings despite possessing its factual basis, Virginia’s successive-petition rule forecloses later presentation, and he established no recognized basis for overcoming the default.

Operative rule: When an unpresented claim is no longer available in state court because of an adequate and independent state procedural rule, the claim is technically exhausted but procedurally defaulted. Federal merits review requires a sufficient showing of cause and prejudice or another established gateway.

Reasoning: The district court found that Gray did not present the suppression claim on direct appeal or in state habeas and that its factual basis was available when he filed his first state petition. Gray did not contest those determinations. Virginia law therefore bars a later petition, and Gray made no cause-and-prejudice showing. The procedural bar must be applied before *Brady* materiality is considered. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/152/case.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: The Court expresses no view on whether the Sorrell material was favorable, suppressed, or cumulatively material. Procedural default is not a merits rejection of the disclosure claim.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. § 2254 and federal habeas procedural-default doctrine.

##### Claim-specific treatment of affirmative misrepresentation

Holding: Gray’s claim that the prosecution affirmatively misrepresented the evidence it intended to use is analytically distinct from both his general notice-of-evidence theory and his *Brady* suppression claim. The Fourth Circuit must determine whether that particular claim was fairly presented and what claim-specific procedural defenses remain available.

Operative rule: Fair presentation requires both the operative facts and the particular federal constitutional theory supporting relief. Presentation of facts capable of supporting one due-process theory, or a general invocation of due process, does not necessarily exhaust a materially different theory resting on distinct governmental conduct. Procedural default likewise must be raised and preserved claim by claim.

Reasoning: A failure to provide general advance notice, suppression of favorable evidence, and an affirmative representation that materially induces defense reliance concern different governmental acts and different constitutional rules. Existing decisions concerning deliberate deception could support relief if the prosecutor intentionally misled the defense and the conduct caused constitutionally sufficient prejudice. But the ambiguity in the federal proceedings prevents the Supreme Court from deciding preservation, exhaustion, waiver of defenses, and merits in the first instance.

Application: If the misrepresentation claim was never fairly presented, ordinary exhaustion and default rules apply. If it was addressed in the federal proceedings and the Commonwealth failed to preserve a procedural defense, the Fourth Circuit shall consider the surviving merits and defenses.

Limits and questions left open: An inaccurate prediction, change in strategy, late disclosure, or failure to provide a witness list is not automatically unconstitutional deception. The claimant must establish materially misleading governmental conduct and the governing prejudice requirement.

Legal basis: The Fourteenth Amendment and federal habeas exhaustion and default rules.

##### New advance-notice theory

Holding: Gray’s general notice-of-evidence theory cannot support federal habeas relief because recognizing a constitutional entitlement to item-by-item advance notice of the prosecution’s nonstatutory capital-sentencing evidence would create a new rule not dictated by precedent when his conviction became final.

Reasoning: Existing law protected defendants against secret evidence, suppression of favorable material, false evidence, and affirmative governmental deception. It did not establish a general constitutional right to advance disclosure of every witness, photograph, report, or evidentiary detail the prosecution intended to use at sentencing. Gray also requested exclusion rather than a continuance, and existing law did not dictate exclusion as the constitutionally required response to late notice. The proposed rule therefore lies outside federal habeas review under *Teague*.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not restrict legislatures or courts from adopting prospective notice requirements. It does not govern genuinely secret evidence, a properly presented *Brady* violation, or deliberate prosecutorial deception under preexisting law.

Legal basis: Federal habeas nonretroactivity under *Teague v. Lane*.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm denial of relief on the notice-only and *Brady* theories; vacate and remand solely on the affirmative-misrepresentation claim.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone separates the three theories by constitutional source and habeas posture. The established default forecloses the *Brady* merits; *Teague* forecloses creation of a general notice rule; and the older due-process rule against deliberate governmental deception requires claim-specific preservation and merits treatment on remand.

#### Material Separate Writings

Ginsburg, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Breyer, would treat Gray’s claim as an established demand for a meaningful and potentially effective opportunity to answer the State’s penalty-phase case. The dissent concludes that the prosecution’s assurance and last-minute expansion of the Sorrell presentation deprived Gray of that opportunity and would require a new penalty proceeding.

Stevens additionally concludes that the evidentiary basis linking Gray to the unadjudicated Sorrell murders was too weak and inflammatory to support constitutionally reliable capital sentencing even with advance notice.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Kyles v. Whitley* and *Wood v. Bartholomew* remain unchanged. Their cumulative-materiality rule is not reached when the *Brady* claim is procedurally defaulted.

*Teague* is applied without alteration to the general notice theory. The decision adds a recurring claim-specific habeas rule: due-process theories arising from a common factual episode remain distinct for exhaustion and default when they depend on materially different governmental conduct and legal guarantees.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court also vacated and remanded 5-4. Chief Justice Rehnquist wrote for O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas; Ginsburg dissented with Stevens, Souter, and Breyer, and Stevens wrote separately. In the simulation, Stone occupies the controlling judgment position, authors the five-Justice Court opinion, and adopts the same principal treatment of the defaulted *Brady* claim, the distinct misrepresentation claim, and the new notice theory. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/152/case.pdf))

Research sources: Official United States Reports for the grant scope, state and federal habeas posture, established procedural default, distinction between notice and misrepresentation, *Teague* analysis, judgment, historical lineup, and separate writings. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/518/152/case.pdf))
